<meta>This is the title page of the 1575 Basel edition of Pierio Valeriano’s monumental work on hieroglyphics. It establishes the author, editors, and the philosophical scope of the book.</meta>

# HIEROGLYPHICS,

## OR, ON THE SACRED
## LETTERS OF THE EGYPTIANS AND OTHER NATIONS
## The Commentaries of Giovanni Pierio Valeriano
## Bolzani of Belluno,

Expanded by two books ~~BY CAELIUS AUGUSTINUS CURIO~~, and <insert>by Caelius Curio</insert> illustrated with many images.

-> TO THE READER. <-

You have in these Commentaries not only an explanation of various histories, coins, and ancient inscriptions, but also—besides the Egyptian and many other mystical matters—a vast forest of commonplaces <note>The term "forest" (Latin: *sylva*) refers to a rich collection of raw materials or topics for study</note> provided with great delight. Furthermore, you will find an exquisite interpretation of the Sacred Scriptures, in which we see quite often that Christ himself, the Apostles, and the Prophets used expressions of this kind. Thus, you may understand that it was not without reason that Pythagoras, Plato, and other supreme men traveled to the Egyptians for the sake of learning; for indeed, to speak hieroglyphically is nothing other than to reveal the nature of divine and human affairs. Farewell, and enjoy happily this most pleasant benefit now offered through Pierio.

-> ~~AT BASEL,~~ <-
-> BY THOMAS GUARINUS, 1575. <-

<insert>1649, during the time of Father Bernardino Lucceio, Prior</insert> <note>This later handwritten addition likely marks the book's acquisition or inventory by a monastery or library</note>

This edition was printed in Basel by Thomas Guarinus and includes additions by the scholar Caelius Augustinus Curio.</summary>

<meta>This page begins the formal dedication of the work to Cosimo I de' Medici. It follows the title page and preliminary front matter.</meta>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS COSIMO
## DE' MEDICI, DUKE OF THE FLORENTINES AND PRINCE OF TUSCANY, AN INCOMPARABLE HERO,
### GIOVANNI PIERO VALERIANO

->Sends Fondest Greetings<- <note>original: "S. P. D." (Salutem Plurimam Dicit), a standard formal greeting in Latin correspondence.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative historiated initial 'Q' containing a circular scene of a man (possibly a scholar or ruler) seated at a desk, surrounded by architectural and floral motifs.</image-desc>

When I reflect within myself, most illustrious Prince COSIMO—and indeed I reflect upon it very often—how great the felicity of both your spirit and your natural talent has been in the choices of your most praiseworthy life and affairs; when everyone, by the unanimous consent of the entire fatherland, placed you at such a tender age in charge of so vast a dominion and raised you to this high seat of honor, then certainly I began to be anxious (as was my duty) as to how you could bear and sustain such a great mass of responsibilities in this time of upheaval. But when I consider the progress of your actions and administration, and the way of life you have embraced, I am filled with such great joy and exultation that I clearly see you have not only been able to protect that position of Fortune through the singular goodwill of all and the highest reputation for virtue, but you have also stirred up such an opinion of your virtue that you have shown yourself worthy of governing even the greatest kingdoms. For that fruit which is usually expected from a good and true prince—even the greatest fruit—you have already shown us, not as a shadowed image, but as a bright and expressed form. And so that you might bring a notable benefit to your subjects, you have first of all given your labor to thoroughly cleansing your Republic of stains and contagious vices <margin>1</margin> <note>The margin note '1' likely refers to a specific decree or chapter regarding public morality.</note>, just as one clears a field destined for bearing better crops of weeds and tares. You do this while you restrain corrupters, and to the best of your ability and by your right, you prohibit, amend, remove, and punish external insults to the Divine Name, blasphemies, perjuries, and extreme crimes of that sort. You have judged nothing more worthy of your dignity, nor any matter more pleasing to God (for every good Prince is and is called His minister), nor more healthy for your citizens, than if you strive diligently and strenuously in your career toward this goal and finish line of honesty and piety. For this labor and untiring zeal, you know for certain that an immortal reward has been set before you by Heaven, since CHRIST, the leader and master of so glorious an endeavor and course, bountifully promises the most happy success in all things to those who ardently love a purer religion and zealously strive to adorn it. Therefore, most distinguished Prince Cosimo, let it be permitted to me, I pray, in my own fashion and out of a certain singular devotion toward you and your family, to more freely employ and bring forward certain things which, out of duty, I should not omit—

<summary>In this dedication, Piero Valeriano praises the young Cosimo I de' Medici for his virtuous governance of Florence, specifically highlighting his efforts to purge the state of vice and blasphemy. The author frames the Duke's political success as a divine reward for his commitment to religious and moral purity.</summary>
<keywords>Cosimo I de' Medici, Florence, Tuscany, Piero Valeriano, virtue, piety, governance, Renaissance dedication, religious morality</keywords>

<meta>The catchword "mnafiis" suggests a transition from a previous section discussing administrative or scholarly duties. This page addresses Cosimo I de' Medici directly.</meta>

## To the Most Illustrious Cosimo de' Medici
<note>Cosimo I de' Medici (1519–1574), Duke of Florence and later the first Grand Duke of Tuscany.</note>

I cannot remain silent: I must exhort and admonish you—or rather, as the saying goes, spur on a horse already running—that you do not lay aside that spirit you have hitherto brought to aiding the public good. Instead, consider that you must be ever more vigilant and keep watch for the Republic with your counsel and labor. All your studies, thoughts, and cares must be placed in those matters by which you may hold the helm of so great a dominion amidst the greatest waves, and protect and govern your ample territories during the utmost difficulties. You will easily achieve all of this if you truly acknowledge the true God in CHRIST, as you already do; if you worship, love, and revere Him with the highest faith and observance; and if you strive day and night to amplify and preserve the glory of His most holy name, His heavenly doctrine, and the tranquility of the Church. In the dominion to which you have been called, seek the public safety as you have done until now; care for and protect the well-being of your subjects; establish honorable discipline, or preserve what is already established; and continue to stabilize your flourishing territory with laws, justice, and concord.

You will say, "This is a great and arduous task, full of troubles, and one that can only be performed by those to whom a peculiar gift has been granted by divine providence." For me, a man enjoying a sufficiently deep leisure, it is far easier to prescribe these things than to find anyone who aims more closely at this target. Quite right, indeed—but to what end is this? It is so that you may look upon yourself, Most Distinguished Prince, and firmly believe yourself to be the one upon whom God, the Best and Greatest, <note>original: "Deus Opt. Max." (Deus Optimus Maximus), a title borrowed from Jupiter but applied here to the Christian God.</note> by His immense kindness, has willed to confer such ample benefits. He has appointed you as the sole Leader and, as it were, a shepherd to great and widely scattered peoples, so that by ruling and feeding them conveniently and prudently, a certain ideal of a holy Republic might shine forth in this your principality. 

Therefore, He willed to add to you two noble companions: Justice and Religion. By the one, you preserve and maintain the fortunes, lives, and dignity of citizens and peoples; by the other, you call everyone to true salvation and true happiness, leading them by your own praiseworthy example. Look, therefore, Prince COSIMO, at the office assigned to you by God and the talent so liberally bestowed upon you. Consider that your celestial name <note>A play on "Cosmos," suggesting order or the universe.</note> fell to you by divine will so that (as the proverb once tossed about regarding Sparta says) you might cultivate the Tuscany <note>original: "Hetruriam"</note> you have obtained and adorn it with every kind of virtue. 

But above all, as becomes a lover of true virtue, you should embrace with your study, liberality, and service the arts in which the liberal doctrines are contained. For in those arts are the fountains and causes of wisdom, virtues, laws, and honorable discipline. You, best of all Princes, know how necessary all these things are to your sovereignty and provinces; you who have spared no expense and no labor to bring the most excellent teachers of the noble arts to your gymnasiums... <note>The text cuts off as it prepares to discuss the patronage of universities or schools.</note>

<summary>A formal dedication to Cosimo I de' Medici, urging him to maintain his pious and just rule over Tuscany through the support of the Church and the liberal arts. The author frames Cosimo's political power as a divine "talent" that must be used to cultivate a model state.</summary>

<keywords>Cosimo I de' Medici, Tuscany, statecraft, Justice and Religion, patronage of arts, Christian leadership, political philosophy</keywords>

-># Dedicatory Epistle<-

<note>original: "Epiftola nuncupatoria"</note>

<center>* 3</center>

...were placed in charge of gymnasiums <note>The term "gymnasiums" here refers to high schools or academies for classical learning</note>, so that they might instruct the youth in the liberal arts. And if at last anyone is found who derives his recommendation from some outstanding virtue, you judge him worthy of your benevolence; you kindly embrace him and defend him with your authority; you cherish him and adorn him with worthy rewards. Thus, no one should now wonder that honest men and the most learned scholars eagerly and willingly commend the offspring of their intellect to you, for by the splendor of your name alone, they procure favor and ornament for their writings.

For which reason, I—who have been among your clients for very many years—did not think I should allow it to happen that, while many others declare in writing that incredible pleasure they perceive in their souls from your great dignity, and wish for you to administer this highest honor (earned by your merit and splendor) for the longest possible time, I alone should remain silent amidst such universal joy. Nor did I wish to fail to testify in writing that our common joy is born chiefly from this: that we see you alone, in this extension of harsher times, chosen by divine providence to cherish and illuminate letters and men of letters with singular kindness, and to defend them against intolerable ignorance and monstrous barbarism.

Therefore, so that I might give some sign of this joy I have felt, I wished for these commentaries—which are quite numerous and were bound together into one "body" at this time at the request of various friends (whom I neither could nor should have refused)—to appear under your name. These commentaries concern the **sacred letters of the Egyptians** <note>A reference to hieroglyphics, which Renaissance scholars believed were mystical symbols of divine secrets</note>. All good men who deign to read our work will easily understand that we have applied great effort and diligent study in either describing or treating these matters.

How successfully this has been attempted by us will be for others to judge. Certainly, we have sustained incredible labors while striving to bring forth an explanation of those things which—shrouded by the obscurity and variety of nature and obstructed by many difficulties—could exercise and detain the minds of many even remarkably learned men. Thus, I hope the Reader will be more favorable toward me when he sees that I was the first, amidst such a variety, magnitude, and difficulty of matters, who wished to labor on this subject with the greatest zeal and the greatest exertion of mind, and to excite the studies of many by an honorable example.

And yet, there have been those at this very point who, at the very entrance of the work, studied to deter me from such a laborious undertaking. They even lashed out at me with the mark and charge of either arrogance or imprudence, because I—one man out of many—should think that I could now at last bring some absolute explanation to this so obscure a subject, or trust that I could unearth such an immense series of hidden things from the secrets <note>original: "arcanis"</note> of the Egyptian priests. Rather, with the difficulty of the matter placed before their eyes, they judged that the labor of this inexhaustible work ought to be declined, especially since in unravelling such things, not a few from...

<summary>The author defends his ambitious study of Egyptian hieroglyphics against critics who call him arrogant, dedicating the work to a patron who protects the learned from "barbarism." He frames the recovery of "priestly secrets" as a difficult but necessary service to the Republic of Letters.</summary>

<keywords>dedication, patronage, Egyptian hieroglyphics, sacred letters, priestly secrets, Renaissance humanism, defense of learning</keywords>

<meta>The catchword "accu-" from the previous page suggests the author was likely discussing the "accuracy" or "accumulation" of ancient knowledge before addressing his patron.</meta>

## To the Most Illustrious Cosimo de' Medici
<note>Cosimo I de' Medici (1519–1574), Duke of Florence and later Grand Duke of Tuscany, a major patron of the arts and sciences.</note>

Greek, Latin, and foreign writers would have spent all their study and labor fruitlessly, because a work of this kind encompasses the reasoning and nature of almost all things and arts. Furthermore, since the ancient monuments of those letters have been lost—partly through the passage of time and partly through human neglect—they used to say that we must struggle with such a scarcity of writers that even if age, talent, and many other things were present, it would hardly be permitted to bring such a massive undertaking to a conclusion. They argue this subject has been so removed from human memory, or rather abolished, that this midnight labor <note>original: "elucubratio," referring to intense study performed by lamplight</note> will never be of any use, especially since at this very time there is no one, however deeply learned, who can understand or interpret either the obelisks still seen in Rome and elsewhere, or that wonderful Table of Bembo. <note>The "Mensa Isiaca" or Bembine Tablet, a famous bronze tablet with Egyptian-style hieroglyphs owned by Cardinal Bembo, which fascinated Renaissance scholars.</note> They claim it is vain to apply one's talent so stubbornly to something you can never hope to fully achieve.

To these people, I wish to respond: I am not unaware that many of these things—along with the kingdom, learning, and institutions of the Egyptians, and nearly their entire name—have been so abolished that even if Heraïscus himself were to return to life (who is said to have once understood all the monuments and mystical letters of their priests at a single glance and provided an interpretation of them), even he would now labor in vain if he attempted such a thing. But suppose that neither I nor anyone else can survey this entire sacred field; should we therefore desist from a noble and laborious undertaking because it is denied to one man to excel in everything? Should we therefore take the ancient coins and skillfully wrought statues—which we seek out with such zeal, dig up with such diligence, and care to restore at such great expense—and cast them into the furnace fire to be used for lime just because they are mutilated in the nose, hand, foot, or some other limb? For how can they be a source of pleasure or admiration to us if they are not perfect in every part, if these critics think nothing is worthy or precious unless it is absolutely complete?

Come then, COSIMO, if you approve of their opinion, cast out the various monuments, both ancient and modern, from your most pleasant villas; see to it that those statues fashioned by the marvelous industry of Michelangelo Buonarroti <note>The famous Renaissance artist Michelangelo (1475–1564).</note> are removed from the Church of San Lorenzo; for something is missing from them too, and they lack the final hand of the artist. <note>A reference to Michelangelo's unfinished sculptures in the Medici Chapel.</note> To speak only of those whom we gladly accept as guides in the course of our commentary: no one would want to have Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the "Natural History."</note> in their home; for having professed that he would publish books on the history of nature or the world, he did not know everything, did not investigate everything, omitted much in his description of the globe, did not review several mountains, failed to mention many rivers, did not recognize all the cities then famous, and understood too little of the force and properties of quadrupeds, fish, birds, plants, and certain other things...

<summary>The author defends his study of Egyptian hieroglyphs and antiquities to Cosimo de' Medici, arguing that even if ancient knowledge is fragmented or "mutilated," it remains precious and worthy of restoration and study.</summary>
<keywords>Cosimo de' Medici, Egyptology, hieroglyphs, Bembine Tablet, Michelangelo, Pliny the Elder, restoration of antiquities, Renaissance humanism</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle (Epistola nuncupatoria) addressed to Cosimo I de' Medici, likely from Pierio Valeriano’s "Hieroglyphica." The author is justifying the incomplete or fragmentary nature of historical and antiquarian research by comparing it to the recovery of ancient statues.</meta>

-># Dedicatory Epistle.<-
<margin>Signature: * 4</margin>

...has he set forth with accuracy? Why should I mention Plutarch, Aelian, Athenaeus, or Pausanias, since none of these men said everything that could have been said on the subject they undertook? What of Paolo Giovio <note>original: "Paulum Iovium"; a famous 16th-century Italian historian and physician</note>, whose praise in writing history is so great that he has surpassed most of the ancients and is considered equal even to the very best? Shall we reject the writings of this incomparable man because, in that series of times, the history of some years is written more concisely? I believe no one, unless they are utterly unskilled in human affairs, would approve of that. You certainly, Prince Cosimo <note>Cosimo I de' Medici, Grand Duke of Tuscany</note>, disapprove of that very thing by your own domestic example; for with as much zeal as you can, you restore whatever can be repaired, and you studiously seek out the images or works of those whose virtues you either imitate or even excel.

But lest I be wordy on this point, the labor of these commentaries seems to me to be compared to nothing more than that zeal for digging up and restoring statues of which I spoke. For just as those statues, however mutilated they may be, are accustomed to be pleasing, so these works of ours (unless love for the undertaken work deceives me), even if they do not embrace everything, will nevertheless, I hope, possess something with which human wits can delight themselves. Therefore, let me have nothing to do with those who, because they despair of the limbs of the "unconquered Glycon" <note>A reference to the Farnese Hercules, signed by the artist Glykon; the author argues that even if we cannot achieve perfection, we should still strive for health</note>, do not wish to protect a body from knotty gout. We know that the boundaries of human strength are circumscribed, and that it is something to "advance this far, even if it is not granted to go further" <note>A quote from Horace, Epistles 1.1.32</note>. Why, then, should anyone be of an unjust mind toward him who endeavors in any way to delight or help human minds by his industry and labors?

For concerning the delight or utility of the present argument, which the explanation and knowledge of these things brings, if I wished to speak according to its dignity, no end would ever be found. But if no small nobility is gained for every thing from its antiquity, it is the opinion of writers that these letters which the Egyptians have used since then were first devised when those ancient men before the Flood—who are said to be the first of mortals to have tracked the logic of celestial things—erected two columns of different material, one of brick and the other of stone, upon which they inscribed the whole secret of the consummation of the world. For there are those who assert that a description of this kind consisted of the figures of animals and other things, in which, nevertheless, philosophers, poets, historians, and even those of divine disciplines saw the meanings of their teachings hidden.

For it was celebrated by a most constant fame that those Egyptian priests professed all the obscurity of nature to be so clearly known to them that they possessed it as a hereditary discipline, handed down as if by hand. Moved by this very noble fame, that admirable Pythagoras thought Egypt must be visited and surveyed by him, and there he received such lessons in secret things from those same priests that it was said of him everywhere, not without reason, that "in mind he approached the Gods, and what nature denied <insert>to human sight, he drew forth with the eyes of his heart</insert>." <note>A paraphrase of Ovid, Metamorphoses 15.62-64, regarding Pythagoras</note>

<summary>The author defends the value of his work on Egyptian hieroglyphics by comparing it to the restoration of ancient statues, arguing that even partial knowledge is valuable. He traces the origin of these symbols to antediluvian pillars and explains how Pythagoras sought this "hereditary discipline" from Egyptian priests.</summary>

<keywords>Cosimo I de' Medici, historiography, restoration of antiquities, Egyptian hieroglyphics, Pythagoras, antediluvian columns, Egyptian priests, Paolo Giovio</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Cosimo I de' Medici, Duke of Florence, establishing the historical and divine precedent for using symbols and allegories to convey sacred truths.</meta>

# To the Most Illustrious Cosimo de' Medici

->human sight, he drank those things in with the eyes of his heart.<- So great was the admiration for this man <note>referring to Pythagoras, mentioned on the previous page</note> that the people of Croton and Metapontum (where he is said to have departed from this life) made a temple out of his house and worshipped him as a divinity. What of Plato? That he was held and called the prince of intellect and learning, and the God of philosophers—must he not credit that to his Egyptian travels? Was not Moses himself—whom Pharaoh’s daughter adopted and educated in a royal manner by employing skilled Egyptian teachers—honored with the name of a great and wise man?

But lest I seem to labor too much in seeking out many examples, I find that the divine scriptures of our own people have a similarity to this hieroglyphic method of instruction; for everything was written with a certain mystical sense, whatever Moses, David, and the rest of the Prophets brought forth when inspired by the heavenly spirit. Indeed, in the New Law and the New Instrument <note>The New Testament</note>, when our Savior says: *I will open my mouth in parables, and I will speak of ancient things in riddles* <note>original: "Aperiam in parabolis os meum, & in ænigmate antiqua loquar" — a reference to Psalm 78:2 and Matthew 13:35</note>, what else did he mean than "I shall speak in the manner of hieroglyphs, and bring forth the ancient monuments of things through allegory"? And that phrase, *Jesus spoke to the crowds in parables*—did he not cover his speeches with a kind of secret veil? In the same way, we see that the Apostles departed from the common manner of speaking, so that sacred sayings about God might be distinguished from other writings by a certain form, just as they are by their inherent dignity. This was done lest the majesty of heavenly mysteries be revealed everywhere and without discrimination, and lest *that which is holy be given to dogs, and pearls be cast before swine.*

Certainly, Demetrius Phalereus <note>A Greek orator and statesman involved in the Library of Alexandria</note> affirmed to King Philadelphus of Alexandria, in the hearing of many, that the Greeks were not permitted to touch Hebrew history. He claimed that certain illustrious writers who dared to treat that subject were afflicted with mental illness and bodily weakness for as long as they persisted, until they ceased from their undertaking—as if that secret were forbidden to profane nations by the manifest anger of the deity. If many people would look back on this today, they might behave themselves more modestly.

For you see what antiquity had hidden concerning human and divine discipline under the coverings of certain things and words, and that all the most skilled men were accustomed to use this kind of riddle among themselves. Therefore, it ought not to be turned into a fault against me if I have applied all my effort and study to the explanation of these things, which we know have always been held in such high value by all the most excellent men. It was given as a mark of highest praise to Angelo Poliziano, Pietro Crinito, and Filippo Beroaldo <note>Renowned Renaissance humanists and scholars</note> that they were the first to interpret one or two passages of this kind. Why then should I be denied the right to attempt the same? And if I am not one who would dare to compete with them in erudition (for they were among those whom "impartial Jupiter loved" <note>A classical idiom for men of exceptional genius</note>), nevertheless, if my unremitting labor and perseverance in unrolling more remote books might avail anything...

<summary>The author justifies his study of hieroglyphics by citing the precedent of Plato, Moses, and Jesus, arguing that divine truths are intentionally veiled in symbols to protect them from the "profane." He aligns his work with the tradition of great Renaissance humanists like Poliziano.</summary>
<keywords>Cosimo de' Medici, Hieroglyphics, Allegory, Moses, Plato, Parables, Sacred Mysteries, Renaissance Humanism, Demetrius Phalereus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dedicatory preface, transitioning from the author's general motivations for writing to the specific dedication of the work to his patron.</meta>

# Dedicatory Letter

<note>original: "Epistola nuncupatoria"</note>

...unless I am mistaken, grateful readers will acknowledge that I have written not just one or two passages, but many volumes on secrets of this kind, to the eventual congratulation of posterity; and they will see that I have not separated pleasure from utility in the treatment of these matters. We shall certainly attain the greatest advantages and benefits if we are willing to take examples from the many creatures devoid of reason—both of the virtues we should follow and the vices we should flee. Nor will opportunity be lacking for those of right mind who might appropriately relate and explain these things in accordance with our religion. Nor is the consideration of trees and plants an idle task for us, since the Blessed Paul, and before him David, teach that the greatness and dignity of God are understood through the knowledge of created things. Since these things are so, who among us could be of such a sluggish spirit, so immersed in earthly dregs, that he would not confess himself bound to God by innumerable benefits? For he sees that he was created as a human being, and that all things contained in the heaven, air, water, and earth were brought forth for the sake of man; and that he was established not only as a contemplator of celestial things, but truly as the master of all that the universe contains within its embrace. If these things were weighed with one's own strength and mind, just as they are perceived daily by the eyes, they would excite in us a wonderful love toward God.

But now it is time that I present for viewing in a single glance those things which, at the urging of my closest friends, have been comprised in individual commentaries. These commentaries, O best of Princes <note>The author is addressing his patron, likely a member of the Medici family or a similar high-ranking noble</note>, I send to you, so that I might declare by what services I am able my loyalty and perpetual respect toward you. I ask you again and again, most earnestly, that you do not spurn these works as they come to you—that is, this small gift from your Pierius <note>The author refers to himself, Pierio Valeriano, in the third person</note>, by which he declares that he wishes to honor your outstanding virtue with everlasting memory—and that you undertake to protect and preserve these books together with their author.

I thought it best to begin with the Lion, the most excellent and strongest of all living creatures. When I cast my eyes more attentively upon it, that most ardent vigor of your own soul immediately shone forth. Therefore, I judged that it could be dedicated more fittingly to no one than to you alone. I was especially incited to do this because in your city, which has always been a nourisher of lions <note>A reference to Florence, where the lion (the Marzocco) is a civic symbol and live lions were famously kept</note>, great hope was offered that my Lion, recently weaned, would sumptuously obtain the growth of its education under your care. For it requires much food; and I know of no one today who, such is your kindness, would more liberally and willingly undertake this expense than you alone, especially since you have the most abundant supply of those things on which my Lion must be fed. Soon, indeed, when it has gained suitable strength, fattened by the help and care of your learned men, it may be presented in the theaters without any shame on our part, and sent forth into any public spectacle under the most happy auspices of your name. Farewell.

<summary>Pierio Valeriano dedicates his work on hieroglyphics and symbols to his patron, arguing that studying nature reveals God's greatness. He specifically highlights the Lion as the opening subject, mirroring the patron's own vigor and the heraldic traditions of the patron's city.</summary>

<keywords>dedication, natural theology, Lion, Pierio Valeriano, virtue, patronage, symbolism, Florence</keywords>

## CONTAINED WITHIN THESE SIXTY
### COMMENTARIES.

<image-desc>Decorative historiated drop cap 'P' at the beginning of the first entry, depicting a seated figure at a desk, likely a scholar or the author.</image-desc>

<meta>This page serves as a poetic preface to the monumental work "Hieroglyphica" by Pierio Valeriano. The author of the poem, Charles Utenhove the Younger, is a Flemish humanist addressing the editor Arnold Arlenius and the printer Michael Isengrin.</meta>

### AN EPIGRAM BY CHARLES UTENHOVE THE YOUNGER OF GHENT, to those most distinguished men, Arnold Arlenius Peraxylus and Michael Isengrin, regarding the fifty-eight books of the *Hieroglyphica* by Giovanni Pierio Valeriano.

Come now, Muses <note>original: "Pierides," a common epithet for the Muses</note>, who will grant me the word to speak to all,
Who allowed you to sing such songs as these?
Quickly let us be raised up in praise of our own
Pierius <note>Giovanni Pierio Valeriano (1477–1558), the author of the following work on Egyptian symbols</note>, the light of the Graces and the glory of accomplishments,
Pierius, the highest head of the Ausonian <note>Italian</note> land,
Pierius, the sun of the Italian marshlands.
Come now, celebrate the immortal mysteries of the Muses,
Celebrate, for the Muse herself was his nurse;
Truly, the Muse was not merely his nurse, but his mother,
She bore him, and his father was Phoebus <note>Apollo</note> the prophet.
The Muse, having rejoiced for the Italian father,
Brought forth a divine support for the foam-born one <note>likely Aphrodite/Venus, symbolizing beauty or art</note>.
It is no wonder that he was the greatest even before this,
The Muse even wrote through me, and taught me to speak.
Not even for the thousands of Erasmus the Great,
Nor for the deep-set bronze-engraved types <note>printing presses</note>
Recently carved, has anyone felt such love among mortal men,
Nor for the books of Melanchthon <note>original: "Melachthoniais," referring to the reformer Philipp Melanchthon</note>.
As much as I rejoiced seeing this labor of Pierius
Concerning the sacred writings of the Memphite <note>Egyptian</note> sages.
Here you will learn the mysteries of the people of the Nile,
Here the images of the unutterable rites of Isis,
Here you will learn the rebirth of myths without end,
Of all kinds of Greeks, and all sorts of Latins.
That which was previously unknown, you have now made common to all,
O Isengrin <note>Michael Isengrin, the printer in Basel</note>, most excellent of fine printers,
Of the printers of Basel who are always the best,
(Even though Basel holds many famous things)
You who spared neither your expense nor your labor,
So that you might cast forth better books into the light.
Give thanks first to Pierius, and then to
Arlenius <note>Arnold Arlenius, a scholar and editor from Flanders</note> himself, the glory of all the Flemish-born;
To Arlenius, the seeker of beautiful things, both ancient
And new, of Greek and Roman books;
Lastly to Isengrin, by far the best of printers,
Who engraved these bright things with his own types.
Take this treasure; your libraries
Will not possess anything of such value for a long time, even if you are a king.
<margin>To Pierius.</margin>
Farewell, write more books like these,
And as you write such a lovely book,
May you complete these things; God will grant
To you a long-lasting end to your life.

---

->Farewell, and may you be fortunate in every way.<-

<summary>A laudatory Greek epigram by Charles Utenhove praising Pierio Valeriano's "Hieroglyphica," as well as the editor Arnold Arlenius and the printer Michael Isengrin for bringing the work to light. The poem emphasizes the book's importance in revealing Egyptian mysteries and its superiority over other contemporary publications.</summary>
<keywords>Charles Utenhove, Pierio Valeriano, Arnold Arlenius, Michael Isengrin, Hieroglyphics, Basel printing, Humanism, Egyptian mysteries, Muses, Erasmus</keywords>

<meta>This is the opening page of Pierio Valeriano’s "Hieroglyphica," a seminal 16th-century work on Egyptian symbols. The translation maintains the formal yet enthusiastic tone of a Renaissance humanist addressing his patron.</meta>

# 10. PIERIO VALERIA-
## NUS TO COSIMO DE' MEDICI, DUKE
### OF THE FLORENTINES, CONCERNING THOSE THINGS
->which are signified by the Lion, according to the sacred<-
->writings of the Egyptians.<-

---

<margin>A</margin>
<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'M' featuring classical architectural elements and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>
MANY indeed are the things which philosophers as well as historians, and even soothsayers, have discussed concerning the Lion: but none have observed or commended truer or more admirable facts to posterity than the Egyptian priests. For so many things exist which those men—who, I dare say, were privy to all the secrets of nature—explored with the greatest diligence regarding the dispositions of animals. Indeed, all should know that such is the excellence in the Lion that even its individual members have provided evidence of some mystical meaning, not only through the invention of those priests, but by the consensus of almost all nations and ages. There are so many monuments of the ancients in which the Leonine face, expressed in various hieroglyphics, signifies now magnanimity <note>greatness of soul or courage</note>, now the strengths of mind and body, now the Sun and the earth, now the obedience of children toward parents, now the tamer of the soul: and soon in other pic-

<margin>B</margin>
tures it demonstrated the wantonness of love, a part of the oratorical faculty, vigilance, guardianship, a terrifying man, a ruler, the flooding of the Nile, clemency, vengeance, and many other things. Yet no power of its nature is judged more admirable than that very magnanimity in which it preeminently excels. <margin>Concerning the magnanimity of the Lion.</margin> We shall therefore begin first of all from this, which the Egyptian priests wished to be understood through the painted Lion for this reason: because among quadrupeds they found no other animal to excel more in spirit. Furthermore, such is the image of the Lion that it displays a likeness of a great and noble soul, as is found in the observations of the Physiognomists <note>scholars who judged character from physical appearance</note>. For it has a large head, fiery pupils, a rounded face, and manes spread everywhere in the likeness of rays, with which it clothes its neck and shoulders. Indeed, the Roman theaters quite often showed that this kind of Lion is the more spirited. And truly, the more these manes are spread, the more they indicate a contempt for wounds. Homer calls them "irresistible" <note>original Greek: ἀνυποστάτους (anypostatous)</note>: of whom it is said that the lion is not terrified by masks, nor pricked by a cobbler's awl. Wherefore Diogenes, when he was serving as a slave under Xeniades and his friends wished to ransom him, denied that he wanted it:

<margin>C</margin>
Saying, "Do you not know that Lions do not serve those by whom they are fed, but rather the feeders serve the lions?" With equal magnanimity Antigonus, the son of Demetrius, when he was once forced to retreat by pressing enemies, denied that he was fleeing, but said he was "pursuing an advantage situated at his back." Indeed, you may see a face of this kind, bristling with a mane in the manner of an irritated porcupine, on a coin of Antoninus; <margin>A coin of Antoninus.</margin> and such are those which the Egyptians placed beneath the throne of the Sun, demonstrating the affinity which they seemed to have with God himself through the imitation of the manes. For those are altogether degenerate and likewise more fearful which have curlier and shorter manes. Nor does the Divine reading <note>the Holy Scriptures</note> mean anything else when it writes that the people of God rise up like a Lion's whelp, and leap forth like a lion. For how great are those signs of a great soul when one takes up his cross and follows Christ, when he despises the rages of Princes, when he scorns fierce and cruel lashings, when finally, struck by swords like a sacrificial sheep, he shows no consternation by neither murmur nor complaint; when, having conquered the terror of the age and despised the punishments of the body, they are handed over to fire, beasts, and torturers: for they look down upon and despise all things which are

<summary>Pierio Valeriano introduces his study of the Lion as a hieroglyphic symbol, dedicating the work to Cosimo I de' Medici. He focuses on the Lion as a primary symbol of magnanimity (greatness of soul), drawing parallels between Egyptian mysticism, classical history, and Christian martyrdom.</summary>
<keywords>Lion, Pierio Valeriano, Cosimo de' Medici, Hieroglyphics, Magnanimity, Physiognomy, Antoninus Pius, Diogenes the Cynic, Solar symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the lion as a hieroglyphic symbol, specifically focusing on its representation of mental and physical strength. It follows the previous page's introduction of the lion's noble nature.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano: The Lion, or

<margin>D, E, F</margin>
in mortal power, and imitates him who is called the Lion of the tribe of Judah <note>original: "Leo de tribu Iuda"; a biblical reference to Christ</note>: and this, finally, is that very undaunted man whom Democritus demanded as a specimen of the highest good—one who, naturally, had freed his mind from terror.

<margin>STRENGTH OF MIND AND BODY.</margin>
Indeed, they say that Admetus <note>A king in Greek mythology served by Apollo</note> yoked a lion and a boar together, by which it is understood that he joined the virtues of the mind and the body. For by the lion, they interpret the power of the mind; by the boar (as said in its own place), the strength of the body. They add that, by this same reasoning, he was most acceptable to Apollo and Hercules, whom he had won over through a balanced wisdom and a natural disposition of innate virtue. Furthermore, poets have been accustomed to call men who are fierce in war "lion-hearted" <note>original: "θυμολέοντες" (thymoleontes)</note>.

<margin>STRENGTH.</margin>
<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration showing a muscular, bearded man (likely Hercules) wrestling a lion, holding it in a headlock.</image-desc>
There is also that hieroglyphic where strength is signified by the foreparts of the lion, because in that animal those members are the most robust; for the Egyptians took their meanings from a certain excellent power found in each thing. Lucretius Carus expressed this strength when he wrote:
> *In the beginning, the fierce breed of lions and the savage generations*
> *Were protected by their courage.* <note>original: "virtus" - here meaning physical excellence or bravery</note>

Many have recorded that the lion was added to the heavens <note>referring to the constellation Leo</note> for no other reason than that the sun is most powerful and robust at the time it passes through that sign, and soon degenerates as it declines toward the hind parts. Because, as Pausanias says, it is fitting for a warrior in conflict against an enemy to rage, setting aside all gentleness and mercy, the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> called Mars "the Hunter" <note>original: "θηρατὰν" (theratan)</note>. He says that Homeric line spoken of Achilles looks to this: "Like a lion, he knows only savage things" <note>original: "Λέων δ’ ὥς ἄγρια οἶδεν"</note>. Authors report that a sign of this kind was usually seen on the tomb of Simandius <note>likely Ramses II</note>, the most famous King of the Egyptians, whom you would have seen depicted in the form of a lion putting enemies to flight, so that from it one might perceive the courage, strength, and vigilance of the king. Indeed, one can see in a <margin>Coin of Trajan.</margin> coin of Nerva Trajan this fortitude depicted such that a club rests upon a lion's head. It was said in the commentary on "Strength" <note>referring to a different section of this book</note> that fortitude is signified by the club; however, by the lion's head, we interpret the generosity of the soul. For both are necessary for one who aspires to claim the name of an unconquered Leader. 

Regarding the strength of the lion, authors record that Hyllus, the son of Hercules, was surnamed the "Cithaeronian Lion" because he excelled in strength. When Euchemus the Arcadian killed him, he was hailed as King by the Megarians, among whom there was an oracle that they should adopt as their own whoever had killed the lion. This was similar to the deed of Diocletian, who had received word from oracles that the supreme power would come to him as soon as he had slain a boar <note>Latin: "aper"</note>; for this reason, when he had dispatched many boars, he used to complain jokingly that he killed boars every day but was never declared Emperor—until he cut the throat of a man named Aper <note>Arrius Aper, whom Diocletian killed to seize the throne</note>. On a <margin>Coin of C. Publicius, son of Quintus.</margin> very beautiful coin of C. PUBLICIUS, son of Quintus, one can see an image of fortitude: it shows a lion being strangled, with a club spread at the feet and a quiver with arrows placed in front. The poet Hippothoon described this lion-like strength in a very beautiful short verse <note>term: "senariolo" - a small verse of six feet</note> which we find in Joannes Stobaeus: "The old age of a lion is better than the youth of fawns" <note>original: "γῆρας λέοντος κρεῖσσον ἀκμαίων νεβρῶν"</note>. That old man in Aristophanes' *The Wasps* says words of this kind: "I consider my own old age to be better than many youths with their fancy hair" <note>original: "ἐγὼ τἠμὸν νομίζω γῆρας εἶναι κρεῖττον ἢ πολλῶν κικκίνων νεανιῶν"</note>. Hippothoon writes that the old age of a lion is better than the most flourishing youth of fawns; and that old man of Aristophanes says his old age is better than the youthful virtue <note>catchword: virtu-</note>

<summary>This page explores the lion as a symbol of combined mental and physical strength, citing classical myths like Admetus and Hercules, and historical figures like Trajan and Diocletian. It emphasizes that the lion's "foreparts" represent the peak of power, much like the sun in the constellation Leo.</summary>
<keywords>Lion, Fortitude, Hercules, Trajan, Hieroglyphics, Admetus, Symbolism of Strength, Egyptian Kings, Numismatics</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of symbolic attributes in Book One, moving from general virtues to the specific symbolism of the lion.</meta>

# Book One.

A. He boastfully prefers [this] to virtue. Cicero, however, in his *Duties* <note>original: "Officiis"</note>, says that force is no more peculiar to the lion than fraud is to the fox.

->**VIGILANCE AND GUARDIANSHIP.**<-
<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a lion's head from a frontal perspective, set within a double-lined rectangular frame. Above the lion's head, the phrase "VIGILANCE AND GUARDIANSHIP" is printed in capital letters.</image-desc>

Truly, however, the Egyptian priests represented vigilance and guardianship through the head of a lion. They added as a reason that this is the only animal among those with curved claws that can see as soon as it is born. And for this reason, Plutarch thinks the lion is considered a solar animal. Indeed, seekers of etymologies say that the name "always-beating heart" <note>original Greek: "πὰρ πάλαις ἦτορ" (par palais ētor)</note> was bestowed upon it. Furthermore, it is very sparing of sleep, and has eyes that shine while sleeping and are in a certain way open, which they have accepted as a sign of guardianship. Indeed, many have believed that lions are entirely sleepless, which Manetho the Egyptian relates in those things

B. he wrote to Herodotus. Some have observed as an argument for this matter that it constantly moves its tail while resting. However, it is incredible, as Aristotle thinks, that any animal should enjoy perpetual wakefulness; but those who claim to have observed such things were persuaded because the lion has large eyes and very small eyelids, so that it cannot completely cover the eye. To one looking on, that brilliance presents itself which flashes abundantly from its pupil; wherefore it always seems to be devoid of sleep. For this reason, quite significantly, lions were placed as guardians of divine things—as can be seen everywhere—not only at the gates of Mycenae, but 
<margin>Why iconic heads are attached to the gates and folding doors of temples.</margin>
also on the doors of other buildings, and especially on the folding doors of shrines and the vestibules of sacred houses. And those who attribute sleeplessness to them claim they are dedicated to the Sun for that reason, as the Sun shares the same condition as the lion, since he protects the earth with a perpetual and indefatigable gaze from his open and fiery eye. Since, moreover, it is well known that Alexander the 
<margin>Alexander the Great on coinage.</margin>
Great, besides other things which were excellent in the man, was also most vigilant (which we have said elsewhere in the example taken from the crane), I do not wonder—even if there may be other reasons for this matter—that he is seen on certain coins wearing a lion's head, on the other side of which is an image of Jove <note>Jupiter</note> sitting, leaning on a staff with his left hand, 

C. holding out an eagle with his right, with the letters ALEXANDER <note>original Greek: "ΑΛΕΞΑΝΔΡΟΎ"</note>; namely, because he traced his lineage from Hercules through Caranus, the first king of Macedonia. For Hercules was everywhere distinguished by the spoil of the Nemean Lion, or because he laid low a lion of extraordinary size while a Spartan legate was watching. Although I am not unaware that King Philip, after his marriage to Olympia, seemed to himself in his sleep to have fixed a prominent seal upon his wife's womb, the carving of which had the image of a lion; which the skilled interpreters replied meant both that his wife was pregnant and would give birth to a spirited son. Hence Alexander, in honor of his mother, greatly rejoiced in the lion's skin; and for the same reason, when he had built Alexandria in Egypt after being warned by a dream, 
<margin>Leontopolis <note>Lion-city</note></margin>
he called it Leontopolis; although his successors preferred it to be called Alexandria after the founder himself. Regarding what pertains to the sleep of the lion, Adamantius says the Natural Philosophers <note>original: "Physiologos"</note> write this about the lion's cub: that as soon as it is born, it sleeps for three days and three nights, and then, by the roar of the father, as if the place of its bed were trembling and shaken, the sleeping cub is awakened. To this is drawn that saying by Jacob in the sacred reading: *Judah is a lion's whelp; from the prey, my son, thou art gone up: he stooped down, he couched as a lion, and as an old lion; who shall rouse him up?* <note>Genesis 49:9</note> And also that from the second response of Balaam: *He lay down as*

<sig>a ij</sig>

<summary>This page explores the lion as a symbol of vigilance and guardianship in Egyptian and Greek thought, noting its physical traits like "sleeping with open eyes." It connects this symbolism to Alexander the Great's lineage and biblical metaphors regarding the Lion of Judah.</summary>
<keywords>lion symbolism, vigilance, Alexander the Great, Egyptian priests, Aristotle, Hercules, Leontopolis, biblical allegory, Judah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the lion as a symbol, following the previous page's introduction of the lion as a figure for strength and vigilance. The catchword "tantum" links to the final word of the previous page.</meta>

<margin>D</margin>
The lion will rest, and like a lioness, who shall dare to rouse him? <note>original: "leo requiescet, & quasi leæna, quã suscitare quis audebit"</note> This undoubtedly pertains to the burial of Christ. Indeed, we can also understand that he slept like a lion because of his eyes, which shine even while sleeping; thus we understand that his divinity itself was not slumbering at all along with him. Moreover, the detail we added regarding the eyes shining during sleep was done for this reason: to "pluck the ear" <note>A Latin idiom meaning to give a sharp reminder or warning.</note> of those who, though otherwise men of great learning and piety, nevertheless fell into the error of thinking that the soul of Christ suffered among those in the underworld during the time his body lay hidden in the tomb—a notion that the very human nature of pious men dreads to hear. Note, however, that the Latin translation of Origen in this passage, as is common almost everywhere, is greatly corrupted.

### THE TERRIFYING ONE.

<margin>The shield of Agamemnon.</margin>
And by that same sign by which they indicated strength and vigilance, they also signified a terrifying man who could strike others with consternation by his sight alone. Pausanias <note>A 2nd-century AD Greek traveler and geographer.</note> bears witness that such an image was on the shield of Agamemnon to strike fear into others. This shield hung for some time in the temple at Olympia, with an inscription added:
<margin>E</margin>
> This is the Terror of mortals, and he who holds it is Agamemnon. <note>original Greek: "Οὗτος μὲν φόβος ἐστὶ βροτῶν, ὁ δ᾽ ἔχων ἀγαμέμνων."</note>

Which we can render thus:
> This is the dread of men, and he who carries this is Agamemnon.

Now, the Lion is of such a nature that even if he attempts nothing fierce, he nevertheless terrifies those looking at him; such is the power and majesty in his eyes. Whence both Greek and Latin poets, when describing terror, most willingly took their comparisons from the ferocity of this animal. Rightly, therefore, Chabrias, the leader of the Athenians—as others say of Philip of Macedon—used to say that an army of deer led by a lion was more formidable than an army of lions led by a deer. Furthermore, such great force and vigor in an otherwise robust animal seems to reside so entirely in the eyes that it appears to be weakened beyond belief by even the slightest injury to them; by the lightest covering of a cloth, it becomes more cowardly than a sheep. This has been seen frequently in the theaters at Rome and was proven by the example of Lysimachus, whom Alexander the Great had shut in the same enclosure with a ferocious lion. But Lysimachus, entering upon this path, tamed and strangled the beast, becoming an object of admiration to the King and saving his own life; he earned the right to have this deed prolonged in eternal memory on coins. Indeed, his terror consists not only in his eyes, but also in his roar. Thus Pindarus calls him "deep-roaring" <note>original Greek: "βαρύβομον"</note> in the *Olympian Odes*, as we said regarding the Fox. This happens with such great trembling of all wild beasts that, as Ambrose and Basil <note>Early Church Fathers who wrote on the natural world (Hexameron).</note> testify, many animals that might have escaped his attack through speed are nevertheless struck down and stunned, as if by a blow, upon hearing the horrendous voice of his indignation; they often fail so completely out of lost fear that they are captured with no effort. Furthermore, the interpreters of dreams <term>Onirocritæ: dream-interpreters</term> maintain that he who imagines in a dream that he has a lion's head is signified to be terrible to his adversaries, and sometimes will attain princedoms and dominions. Because of the greatness of such a roar, Mark, one of the four Evangelists, is to this day represented hieroglyphically by the image of a lion (as foreseen by Daniel), because at the very beginning of his work he thunders like a voice crying in the wilderness with a great mouth. So says Eucherius.
<margin>The famous deed of Lysimachus witnessed on a coin.</margin>
<margin>F</margin>
<margin>The hieroglyphic image of Mark the Evangelist.</margin>

### THE RULER.

Certainly, Basil posits the lion as the ruler <note>original: "dominator"</note> of the animals devoid of reason. It has been observed in portents that if by chance some woman should give birth to a lion, the republic in which it happened would be conquered by foreign nations; this is not only in human...

# Book One.

<margin>A</margin> You may find among the soothsayers <note>aruspices: ancient diviners who interpreted omens, often from animal entrails or unusual births</note> that the lion's power prevails not only in its own kind, but also in any other living creature of a different form. It was observed on the island of Cos that when a ewe from the flock of a private citizen named Nicippus gave birth to a lion, the outcome of this event was not without effect, as he seized sovereign power from that moment. From this comes that "lion’s share" partnership <note>original: "societas leonina"</note>, which claims far more for itself than it leaves for others. Regarding this, the legal consultant Aristo reports that Cassius responded that such a partnership cannot be entered into where one party feels only the gain and the other only the loss; and he was accustomed to call this a "lion’s partnership" in the Law Books <note>referencing the Digest of Roman Law, XVII. T. pro Socio. l. XIX</note>. Furthermore, Dio Chrysostom, in his discourse *On Kingship* <note>original: <term>περὶ βασιλείας</term></note>, says that Homer wisely compared Agamemnon to an ox in that verse:
> Just as the bull stands out far above all the herd. <note>Iliad 2.480</note>

Indeed, he by no means called him like a lion or an eagle, because those are examples of absolute domination.

### THE SUPREME CUNNING OF CHARACTER.

<margin>B</margin><margin>An elegant fable.</margin> I shall not find it burdensome to add an ancient fable on this subject, which may serve as a very useful lesson for mortals. A rumor had spread throughout the world of an oracle that had come forth from Jupiter Ammon, stating that Alexander the Macedonian would shortly come to him, whom the god confessed to be his own son. Because of this, the kings and princes of the earth were stirred to obedience, competing among themselves with gifts to win his favor. Especially Ptolemy, the King of Egypt, intending to venerate the son of his god, prepared a great sum of money—specifically, the amount of tax he had collected in a single day from the mouths of the Nile and the customs houses of the city of Memphis. There were several thousand talents of every kind of currency collected from all sources; he arranged for all of this to be carried to Alexander as a chosen gift of hospitality. A mule, a horse, a donkey, and a camel voluntarily offered their labor as pack animals, undertaking to carry the money faithfully. They had progressed scarcely two days from Memphis when they encountered a lion. The lion himself, having understood the cult of Hercules which the Macedonian had adopted, was traveling toward Macedonia to greet the King while looking out for his own interests. Greetings having been given and received on both sides, and when all had described their intended journey (as usually happens), the lion joined them as a companion, acting as a guard and protection against robbers. Having been informed about the money, he said that he also had a certain sum of drachmas which he was carrying for his provisions; but they were very inconvenient for him, because he was by no means accustomed to carrying burdens. He therefore asked that they each take up a light addition to their load, distributed among them, and he would regard this as a great favor. They complied most dutifully, and having divided those very few drachmas of the lion among themselves, each mixed them into their own bags and soon continued their journey. They had come into the lush plains of Asia when the lion, seeing a multitude of herds, decided it would be to his advantage to stay there for a few days. Feigning exhaustion, he said he needed a few days of rest and asked back for the money he had deposited. They immediately opened their bags and told him he was free to take back what was his. When the lion saw many other drachmas marked with the same stamp in every single bag, he let out a great and joyful roar: "My drachmas," he said, "have given birth to many more drachmas each!" And he soon took away as his own as many as were similar to his. What of the fact that they say Hanno the Carthaginian, a famous man mentioned by Pliny, was driven into exile because he had accustomed a lion to carrying burdens? The argument taken from this, as Plutarch says in his *Political Precepts* <note>original: <term>ἐν πολιτικοῖς παραγγέλμασι</term></note> to Trajan, was that he who could tame a lion might aspire to far greater things.

### THE MAN WHO HAS SUBDUED EITHER HIS OWN OR ANOTHER'S FEROCITY.

<margin>E</margin> By the same omen, lions were joined to the triumphs of mortals by Mark Antony; and he who...

# Pierio Valeriano: The Lion, or

<margin>D</margin> Indeed, in a civil war, it portends that noble spirits will submit to the yoke. This is what Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, Roman statesman and orator</note> reproaches him <note>Mark Antony</note> with in the *Philippics*, when he mentions that his chariot was yoked with lions, displaying the arrogance of an imperious man. And in the *Letters to Atticus*, who dreaded that omen, he says: "Beware of fearing Antony's lions; nothing is more pleasant than that man," and so forth. Truly, we understand a man who, by art, talent, or authority, makes one far more powerful than himself devoted and subject to him, through the image of a lion tamed by a man; for he was able to soothe and soften the attack and ferocity of a beast otherwise most fierce and strong. Nazianzen <note>Gregory of Nazianzus, a 4th-century Church Father</note> uses this comparison when he says that Basil <note>Saint Basil the Great</note> not only won over Eusebius previously, but so drew him to his own opinion that he seemed to obey his counsels and warnings in all things. It is known from history what happened to the people of Megara, among whom Cassius had left lions which he had prepared for the games of the aediles. But when the city was being captured by Calenus, the Megarians broke open the cages of the beasts, loosening their chains so that, being set against the rushing enemies, they might cause them trouble for a while, while they themselves could take counsel through some delay. But it turned out far otherwise, because the lions, turning with savage cruelty upon the citizens themselves, caused such a slaughter of the unarmed—attacking, prostrating, and tearing apart everyone they met—that the spectacle was most pitiable even to the enemies themselves. And to pass over many such things, there is now the Pythagorean precept that "hook-clawed" animals (if I may so translate the Greek <term>gampsyonycha</term> <note>original: γαμψώνυχα</note>) are not to be nourished, because rapacity must be removed from the state. Aeschylus feels the same when he says that a lion's cub should not be raised in the Republic in these words:

<margin>E</margin> ->One must not rear a lion's whelp within the city,<-
->Above all, do not rear a lion in the city.<-
->But if one is reared, one must cater to its ways.<-
->*To rear a lion's cub in the city is of no use,*<-
->*Greater care must be taken lest you nourish a lion,*<-
->*For he who has raised it must also endure its character.*<-

But Euripides thinks they should by no means be admitted, following the precept of Pythagoras; in which Aeschylus indeed wisely said these things regarding Alcibiades <note>A controversial Athenian general</note>: that men of that powerless <note>meaning "lacking self-control"</note> kind should not be received; but if they have been received, they must be borne with a calm mind. But Euripides, as Aristophanes' Dionysus cries out in *The Frogs*, spoke more nobly, since as it is held in Homer,

<margin>F</margin> ->There are no faithful oaths between lions and men.<-

-><insert>UNTAMED MADNESS.</insert><-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a lion standing in profile. The lion, which has a large, detailed mane, is shown in the act of mauling its own young. It appears to be holding a cub down with its forepaws while biting into its midsection. This visual representation serves to illustrate the concept of "Furor Indomitus" (untamed madness) discussed in the accompanying text.</image-desc>

Therefore, if one wished to signify an immense madness, by which one burns most uncontrollably, they depicted a lion tearing its own cubs to pieces, or rather "de-boning" them—to translate the Greek word <term>exosizonta</term> <note>original: ἐξωσίζοντα</note>, and not <term>ekpiizonta</term> (which, as it is placed twice in the same chapter in the common copies of Horapollo, signifies nothing). Authors record that Hercules was once seized by such madness, and driven to such a point of insanity that he not only killed the sons of his host, but was even accustomed to rage so far against his own sons that he killed them: which is not unlike the picture of a lion tearing its own cubs. Indeed, Theocritus, in *Megara the Wife of Hercules*, says this of the madness he exercised upon his children:
->Wretched man, who with his own arrows destroyed his offspring.<-

<summary>This page explores the lion as a symbol of political danger and uncontrollable rage, citing classical anecdotes about Mark Antony, the disaster at Megara, and the Greek poets' warnings against "rearing a lion" (a powerful tyrant) in a city. It concludes by linking the image of a lion devouring its cubs to the "untamed madness" of the mythological hero Hercules.</summary>

# Book One.

<margin>A</margin>
> Whether by the Fates, or by the terrible missiles of a guest,
> He struck down his own children, and their dear life fled,
> And driven mad in his own house, he returned to slaughter.
> These things I myself beheld with my own eyes, <margin>struck down by their father.</margin>
> *Unhappy is he who, by the gifts of Apollo <note>original: "missilibus de munere Phœbi" referring to arrows</note>,*
> *Whether by the Fates or the impious weapons of the Furies,*
> *Driven by madness, took the dear life from his own*
> *Children, so that the house itself swam, splattered with blood.*
> *These I saw fall, pierced by their father's hands,*
> *Ordered to watch with my own eyes, a miserable sight.*
<note>The author provides both a Greek verse and its Latin translation (here rendered in English) describing a tragic scene of madness, likely referring to Hercules.</note>

Our poets also note the wrathful nature of the lion, hence that passage in Horace: *That Prometheus added the force of the mad lion to our stomach*, when he was speaking of glowing passion. Most elegantly and most learnedly, Lucretius also investigates the cause of this fury; I shall not be reluctant to set down his verses, which are not unpleasant to repeat:

<margin>B</margin>
> *But there is more heat in those who have fierce hearts*
> *And a wrathful mind that easily boils over in anger,*
> *In which kind the violent force of lions is foremost,*
> *Who often burst their breasts with roaring and groaning,*
> *Nor can they contain the waves of anger in their breast.*

Indeed, he had said earlier when speaking of the variety of the mind:
> *There is also that heat which the mind takes on in anger,*
> *When it boils, and a sharper fire flashes from the eyes.*

Truly, the sign of this glowing passion is when he is seen to rouse himself by the lashing of his tail, which Hesiod touches upon in the *Shield of Hercules*:
> And with his tail he lashed both his sides
> and shoulders, and tore the earth with his feet; nor did anyone
> dare to look him in the face and come near, nor to fight him.

Catullus followed him in his Galliambic poem regarding Attis:
> *Come, strike your back with your tail, let your lashes be felt,*
> *Make all the places echo with a bellowing roar.*

<margin>C</margin>
And there follow signs and incitements to fury: for he is seen to wrinkle his brow when about to fight, which the Greeks called <term>skyzen</term> <note>original: σκύζεν; to wrinkle the nose or brow in anger</note>: and they call the contraction of the eyebrows <term>idioklonia</term> <note>original: ἰδιοκλωνίαν; a peculiar twitching or contraction</note>. However, the chief indicator of the lion's spirit is the tail, just as the ears are for horses; and in the lion, it is specifically called the <term>alcea</term> <note>from the Greek 'alke' meaning strength or valor</note>, because its principal strength consists in it: or because it is a certain incitement to take up strength and move the spirit. At the beginning of his anger, the earth is flagged; but as the glowing passion increases, they even lash their own backs. Alexander of Aphrodisias thinks this happens because their soul is moved more vehemently and is greatly desirous of revenge: the tail is used just as men use the hand, with which they often strike themselves in a fit of passion. Thus these animals, moved by anger, when they cannot avenge what hurts them, are accustomed to devise solace for themselves in this way. Therefore, fury is familiar to the lion, hence we read in Maro <note>Virgil</note>, *The angers of lions*. And Ovid says of the same: *His face holds anger*. For this reason, the poets peculiarly call the lion "the wild beast." Thus Callimachus:
> The skin of the beast worn over the shoulders,

As for the fact that they depict the cubs being "de-boned" by him, the reason is that the lion has either no marrow or very little, so that they seem to consist entirely of solid bones like all animals of that type; as if the fury increases from the fact that the labor of crushing these bones becomes the reward.

# Pierio Valeriano’s *The Lion*, or
## On the Lion’s Nature

<margin>D</margin>
...of the smaller parts, since it is the smallest portion that can serve as nourishment. Furthermore, those bones are so hard that when struck together, fire is struck out just as if from a flint. For this reason, the animal is extremely prone to fever. Indeed, it is clear by the consensus of all physicians that fever is nothing other than an overcoming heat of the entire body. Finally, the name for fever is derived from fire in Greek, *pyretos* <note>original: "πυρετὸς"</note>, and from fervor in Latin. Lions are said to be tormented by this disease throughout their entire lives; for that reason, Lucretius called them the "grim brood of lions." <note>Lucretius was a Roman poet and philosopher; "grim brood" refers to their fierce and melancholic nature.</note> Truly, 
<margin>Proverb.</margin>
a proverb emanated from this: "the lion laughed" <note>original: "ἐγέλασεν ὁ λέων"</note>, used whenever we behold a rare example of cheerfulness. This saying of Thucydides <note>A famous Greek historian.</note> was noted in describing the Cylonian crime: namely, when the Athenian people finally grieved that Cylon, through most factious conspiracies, was disturbing the quiet state of the Republic, and as if after a long period of sadness they had finally looked back toward cheerfulness—so that, having made an attack upon the man, they pursued him even as he fled into the temple of the inviolable Goddess, and having dragged him from there, they slaughtered him to the great joy of the entire city.

<margin>A REMEDY FOUND FOR FEVER.</margin>
<margin>E</margin>
-><image-desc>A woodcut illustration enclosed in a rectangular frame. On the left, a lion stands on rocky ground, looking toward a monkey or ape crouched on a slight elevation to the right. In the background, a detailed landscape features a walled city with towers and spires situated along a river or body of water, with a bridge connecting two parts of the terrain.</image-desc><-

Lions are thus vexed by this discomfort; although Albert <note>Referring to Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century German scholar.</note> alone denies that they are feverish. Whether more faith should be placed in him—born, nourished, and educated in Germany, where he spent almost his entire life—when speaking of the lion, or in the Egyptians, who were in a sense born among lions and spent their whole lives with them, let others decide. I, certainly following their teachings in such a matter, say it has been discovered that a lion seized by fever, even if he seethes to the point of madness, is freed by the eating of a single monkey. Therefore, if the Egyptian priests wished to signify a man suffering from fever and procuring help for himself, they were accustomed to set down the hieroglyphic of a lion eating a monkey. For the lion bears the monkey with such indignation that he desires to destroy no animal more greedily. The cause is the animal's petulance, harassing the lion in most unworthy ways. For as soon as it finds an opportunity to leap upon the lion, whether from some tree or 
<margin>F</margin>
from another safe and hidden place, it makes it a great business to attach itself to the tail, to fasten itself to the buttocks, and thus to mock its King with whatever insults it can. Since the lion bears this impatiently, he becomes quite uncontrollably wild against this kind of animal—which we have discussed more fully in the commentary on the *Cynocephalus* <note>The dog-headed baboon.</note>. But so that we may cease to wonder that an animal so ignoble is hostile to the most noble of all, we know also that gnats rage so petulantly against herds of lions that they sometimes drive them to destruction with great slaughter. For among the reed-beds of Mesopotamia, innumerable lions wander near the riverbanks and thickets during the time when the winter's mildness is softest there, always harmless. But when the sky burns through the summer, and those regions are heated and almost scorched by the glow, they are agitated both by the heat of the star and by the multitude of gnats, with whose densest swarms all things in those lands are filled. These gnats, flying toward the brightness of the eyes as if toward moister members, attack them, settling bitingly upon the edges of the eyelids. From this, the lions, tormented for a long time, are either swallowed up by the rivers to which they flee for the sake of a remedy, or, having lost their eyes, they become more monstrously wild. Were this not to happen, that entire region which spreads widely toward the East would be overrun with beasts of this kind. This was discovered...
