# Book One.

<margin>A</margin> Ammianus Marcellinus <note>A 4th-century Roman soldier and historian.</note> testifies to this in the eighteenth book of his *History*.

->ANXIOUS OVER FIRE.<-

And indeed this animal, which carries an innate fire hidden within its very bones, nevertheless fears fire above all else; so much so that it fears nothing as much as held-out torches, which are the most effective means for taming its ferocity. Homer also testifies to this in that poem:
*And the burning torches, and the fire against the rushing fire.* <note>original: "Καὶ ὁπύσεαί τε δέ τε πῦρ, πρὸς ἰσύρῃνος πῦρ."</note>
*And burning torches, which it dreads, however much it rages.*
And Pindarus in the *Nemean Odes*: *The all-powerful fire and the sharp-pointed claws of bold-fighting lions and the most terrible gap of their sharp teeth.* Struck by admiration for this trembling, the Egyptian priests, if they wished to represent a man fearfully anxious over fire and acting as if driven mad, would paint an image of a lion and a small torch. We hardly believed these authors until we saw lions being tamed by this very method, first in Florence and finally in Rome. I saw this image carved in marble in Rome on the Via Leoniana, which leads to the People’s Church <note>The Basilica of Santa Maria del Popolo.</note>, a fragment unearthed from the Mausoleum of the Augusti. <margin>B</margin> lion. The lion was sitting on the ground with its head raised and turned toward its back; carved across it was a pine torch with a nut at the top of the handle, and a ribbon extended from the torch to the lion's back. Although I have found it written in authors that nothing else is signified by a hieroglyphic of this sort than, as we were saying, one who is terrified of fire; yet if I may be allowed to add my own opinion to theirs, I would say the interpretation of this thing is "subdued fury." The Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy.</note> offer this reason why the lion so greatly fears fire: because the chief strength of that animal consists in its eyes; and those who have drier and hotter eyes avoid fire most of all. The leaf placed under a lion's foot could be brought into this same meaning; for the Magi say that a lion becomes sluggish if it steps on a holm-oak leaf. The same power is said to be in the leaf of a squill <note>A flowering sea-onion plant.</note> by Zoroaster—not the ancient one, I mean, but the one who lived after Plutarch.

And lest we wander too far from the lion's fears, it likewise dreads the rooster, and especially a white one, as Ambrose says; Pythagoras also commands one to abstain from it. Some have handed down that this is accepted as a symbol for worshipping divinity. <margin>C</margin> Indeed the rooster, of whom Lucretius says:

->RELIGIOUS DREAD.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a lion and a rooster (cock). The lion, on the left, is in a defensive or submissive crouch, looking upward with a wary expression toward the rooster on the right. The rooster stands tall with its head held high and its tail plumage displayed.</image-desc>

> Whom the rabid lions are unable to stand against
> And look upon, so immediately do they remember flight.

...carries something divine about it, as we have discussed more extensively in the Commentary written on that subject. Truly, every earthly power dreads and reveres divinity. Furthermore, since lions are dedicated to the Mother of the Gods, the Earth, they are understood to be devoted to her; and we said above that a good part of the lion—that which remains from the mane—represents the earth. But Proclus, in his book *On Magic*, brings forward another cause for this fear: he says that the rooster and the lion are primarily solar animals; but why lions fear the rooster cannot be assigned a reason by us from matter or sense, but only from the contemplation of the higher order. This is because the presence or force of solar virtue is infused more into the rooster than is granted to the lion. He seems to conjecture this from what is known to everyone: that the rooster applauds the rising Sun with hymns and calls forth its radiance, especially at that time when, having departed from the middle of the sky of the Antipodes, it comes to us...

<summary>This page explores the symbolic fears of the lion—specifically fire and the rooster—as used in Egyptian hieroglyphics to represent anxiety or religious awe. It cites classical authorities to explain these behaviors through both physical temperament and solar spiritual hierarchies.</summary>
<keywords>lion, fire, rooster, hieroglyphics, solar animals, symbolism, Rome, Egyptian priests, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the lion as a symbol, specifically its mystical relationship with the rooster and its role in Egyptian iconography regarding the Nile.</meta>

# Pierius Valerianus: The Lion, or...

...turns away. He <note>Likely referring to Proclus or a similar Neoplatonist mentioned previously</note> affirms also that certain solar angels have sometimes appeared in the form of a rooster; <margin>D</margin> since these beings are formless in themselves, they exhibit themselves to us—who are formed into a specific physical shape—as possessing that form to be seen. On the other hand, it is said that Demons have sometimes been seen with the face of a lion, who, when a rooster is presented to them, suddenly vanish into thin air. For this reason, even in these days, those devoted to such superstitions are accustomed to sacrifice a young cockerel for the sake of warding them off. To this, some of the Greeks who follow the teachings of the Egyptians add that the soul of Cecrops <note>The mythical founder and first king of Athens</note>, which they believed had been transformed into a lion, can be summoned by sacrificing roosters and inscribing certain characters <note>magical symbols or sigils</note>, and that the soul then reveals itself to them. However, Aeneas Euxitheus <note>Aeneas of Gaza, a Christian Neoplatonist</note> affirms this to be a mockery and contends that Demons thus deceive us with such illusions.

Proclus says that the reason Demons appearing with a lion’s face vanish when a rooster is presented is that, within the same cosmic order, those things which are lower are always compelled to revere those which are higher. Just as many modest men, when they gaze upon the images of divine men, are accustomed by that very sight to fear committing anything base. Lucretius, however, according to the precepts of his own sect <note>The Epicureans, who sought physical rather than mystical explanations</note>, says that there are certain <margin>E</margin>

> Seeds in the bodies of roosters, which, when they are cast into the eyes of lions,
> Pierce the pupils and cause such sharp pain
> That the fierce beasts cannot endure to face them.
> <note>Original Latin: "Semina, quae cum sunt oculis immissi leonum / Pupillas interfodiunt..." from De Rerum Natura, Book IV</note>

It is truly a marvel what the ancients observed: that the lion also so abhors the herb called "Lion-leaf" <note>Leontice leontopetalum, a plant thought to harm lions</note>—which does great damage by entangling itself with its little forks—that if a girl who is still a virgin, yet menstruating, should walk naked with her hair loose around a field of legumes while holding a rooster in her hands, that grass will wither and completely perish. Whatever the truth of this may be, it was handed down by Democritus, as Sotion asserts. But those who are further removed from superstition and contemplate a certain secret power of nature say that the seeds should be touched with the blood of a rooster, and once sown in this way, they will thereafter suffer no injury from the "Lion" herb.

### T H E  F L O O D.

When they wished to show the rising of the Nile—which in the Egyptian language they call *NUM*, meaning "new and fresh" among us—they repeatedly made a hieroglyphic of a lion. Indeed, when the Sun enters the back of the Herculean Lion <note>The zodiac sign of Leo</note>, it excites the flood of the Nile, and a double portion of fresh water often overflows while the Sun lingers in that sign. This force of water, spread widely through the spacious plains of Egypt, renders the soil pregnant with such fertility that not only do the natives gather food for themselves, but they relieve a great <margin>F</margin> part of the world from hunger. Because of this abundance of water, which they experience every year as a benefit of the "Lion," it has become an established custom, now accepted by all nations with one consent, that channels, pipes, and siphons that <margin>Lion heads vomiting water.</margin> belch out water should have the water sent through holes bored into lion heads, which are usually sculpted for this purpose in suitable places, so that the water appears to be vomited from the lions' gaping mouths.

Vitruvius teaches at length the method by which these lion heads should be placed especially on the simas <note>The upturned edge of a roof gutter</note>, and in what order they should be arranged; and that only those which are positioned over columns should be bored through to the channel that receives the rainwater from the tiles. The others between these should be solid, so that the force of water falling through the tiles into the channel is not cast down through the spaces between columns, nor soaks those passing by, but only those over the columns should emit the burst of water from their mouths. For he had observed that the abundance of water was signified by the lion among the Egyptians, and he had seen this done everywhere in all the buildings of the ancients. And to speak plainly, fountains and water pipes that projected from any aqueducts were adorned with lion figures; such as the fountain of admirable workmanship at Viterbo <note>A city in Italy famous for its fountains</note> which [preserves] this ancient...

# Book One

<margin>A</margin> displays this discipline. To set aside other examples, in Rome in the Lateran Square there are two lions of black stone placed before the bronze statue of Marcus Aurelius <note>The famous equestrian statue of the Emperor Marcus Aurelius, which stood at the Lateran before being moved to the Capitoline Hill in 1538.</note>, which undoubtedly served as fountains. The evidence for this is the holes drilled into their open maws, the pipes inserted below through the throat, and a small channel hollowed out between the paws at the chest to receive and discharge water. I observed something similar in the territory of Brescia, outside the Eastern Gate at the second milestone; there, next to the road—which is entirely equipped with great water channels and diversions—is a marble lion of very ancient workmanship, swollen like a wineskin, holding its maw open in the same manner for the outflow of water. But it was not only for the purpose of vomiting water that the images of lions were customarily placed; it was also because the lion—I mean the celestial one <note>The zodiac sign Leo.</note>—is seen to open and close the floodgates of the waters. Even to these times, the ancient custom has never grown obsolete of decorating door-latches, keys, and rings fixed to doors with the maws of lions. Theon, in his commentaries on Aratus, says this was a common practice among the Egyptians; although, regarding doors, I would believe it refers more to the "guardianship" mentioned previously than to <margin>B</margin> the flow of water. I will not omit what Horus Apollo <note>A 5th-century author of a treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> records: that in supplications asking for rain, it was the custom in many places to wash the mouths of lions with wine. However, it should be noted that the common manuscripts of Horus are corrupted in this passage.

### GIVING BIRTH ONLY ONCE.

Since the Egyptians were persuaded that lionesses give birth only once in their lives—a claim that many others have put forward along with Herodotus—they customarily signified a woman who had been the mother of only one son by a sculpted lioness. There is a fable of Aesop on this sentiment approved by ancient writers. <margin>Aesop's fable.</margin> When a fox reproached a lioness for her fertility in order to boast of her own nobility, and attacked her because she gave birth only once in her life and to only one cub, the lioness replied that she indeed gave birth once and to one, "but to a lion." Many have devised explanations for the cause of this rarity, and they especially hit upon the idea that the cubs, having already developed claws in the womb, tear the matrix apart and are born after the womb has been lacerated by that sharp edge of claws during labor; or that she loses her reproductive organs along with the first birth. Aristotle asserts that this is a silly <margin>C</margin> fable, and demonstrates that they give birth as many as five times in a lifetime, which he investigated in the land of Syria. He says they first bear five, and in each subsequent year one fewer, until they decline to a single cub and soon to sterility, in which they spend the remainder of their lives. At other times they mostly bear twins, which has been found by experience over a long series of years in Florence; but while six is the most, sometimes it is even one. Philostratus says it is affirmed by those skilled in such matters that they give birth three times in their whole life: first three, then two, then one; yet a lioness was once seen who carried eight in her womb. But whatever reason the Philosophers have tried to find for this rarity in birthing, Basil the Great <note>A 4th-century Greek bishop and theologian.</note> refers it to the providence of God, by whose command it was brought about that those animals which could easily be captured should be far more fertile than others. Therefore, hares as well as deer and even sheep almost always give birth more often and to twins or more offspring, lest the race fail against those wild beasts who delight in blood and are carnivorous. But those which feed upon others are certainly far less fertile; wherefore he says, "A lioness barely becomes the mother of one lion." Since this rarity of birthing happens more certainly to no other kind of animal, the Egyptians—observing what happens in the majority of cases—rightly showed a woman who bears once through the hieroglyphic of a lioness.

<summary>This page explores the lion's symbolic role in hydraulics and architecture, as well as the ancient (though biologically debated) belief that lionesses only give birth once, making them symbols of singular, noble motherhood.</summary>
<keywords>lions, fountains, Marcus Aurelius, Egypt, Aesop, Aristotle, fertility, hieroglyphics, Basil the Great, Florence</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the lion as a symbolic figure in Egyptian and classical thought, focusing on the virtues of mercy, discipline, and vengeance.</meta>

-> Pierio Valeriano: The Lion, or **CLEMENCY** <-

Beyond these, other hieroglyphics involving the lion are found, which I will touch upon briefly; although I have not found them among those who traditionally interpret hieroglyphic letters, they seem to me to be discovered through careful observation. Among these is **Clemency**, which is signified by a lion and a man lying beneath it—provided the lion remains unharmed. This is because a lion, even when most fiercely provoked by a man, will not tear him with its claws or inflict any injury so long as the lion itself is untouched; instead, it merely shakes him, and once it has terrified him thus, it lets him go. Ovid writes of this:

> *It is enough for the great-souled lion to have laid the body low.* <note>original: "Corpora magnanimo satis est prostrasse leoni." From Ovid's Tristia, 5.1.79.</note>

<margin>A coin of Severus Pius Augustus.</margin> Regarding this meaning, it was not in vain that we saw on a coin of the Emperor Severus Pius a figure of a woman sitting upon a stretched-out lion; in one hand she holds a spear fixed in the earth, and with the other she appears to be casting away a thunderbolt—not in a gesture of throwing it—with an inscription that reads: **THE INDULGENCE OF THE EMPEROR TOWARD CARTHAGE** <note>original: "INDVLGENTIA AVG. IN CAR." referring to Carthage.</note>; nothing else can be read there.

### D I S C I P L I N E

<margin>E</margin> Beyond clemency, we have learned that the judgment of **Discipline** <note>or "Chastisement"</note> is also present in the Lion. This is based on a story cited by Aelian from Eudomus: a lion, a bear, and a dog were raised and educated together in the same quarters by a certain master. They lived together most peacefully for a long time without any injury, as if they were domestic animals of the same species. However, when the bear, stirred by a sudden impulse, tore the companion dog to pieces, the lion—moved by the crime of violated hospitality—rushed upon the bear, tore it apart in the same manner, and thus exacted the deserved punishment on behalf of the dog.

### V E N G E A N C E

Far different from the previous meaning is the fact that many have established the practice of depicting the desire for **Vengeance** through a lion pierced by a weapon. For a wounded lion, by a wonderful power of observation, recognizes its striker; among any number of people, it seeks out that one individual, and if given the chance, tears and rends them most foully. They say that Juba, King of the Moors, had a young companion of excellent spirit. While the King was traveling through the deserts of Africa with his army to settle his affairs, a lion encountered on the journey was struck by an arrow from that very youth and retreated into the woods. A year later, when Juba was leading his troops back through the same area after successfully settling his affairs, that same lion—having watched for the youth from whom it received the wound—attacked that one man out of so great a number of soldiers <margin>F</margin> with a most violent charge. No force could restrain it until it had seized and miserably torn the youth to pieces; having harmed no one else, it departed, satisfied with its revenge. <margin>Cambyses.</margin> Furthermore, the Pythagoreans used to say they detected the soul of King Cambyses of Egypt in a lion, which is why his spirit was so greedy for empire and always sought the greatest things. However, another reason can be assigned: in the times when he flourished, no one was known to be more prone to vengeance than he, who never ceased to pursue the Egyptians with hatred and arms, afflicting them with every inconvenience of harassment. He remained hostile to them until, having gained power over all Egypt and profaned all their sacred rites, he grew weary rather than satisfied by his ill-treatment of the people of the entire region. To this is added the fact that interpreters of omens and augurs claim that the sighting of a lion indicates the beginning of war, which is expressed in an elegant Greek verse:

> To see lions indicates the battles of enemies. <note>original: "Λεόντων ἰδεῖν δυσμενέων δηλοῖ μάχας."</note>
> *The sight of lion-images at night indicates slaughter by enemies.*

### R O Y A L  O M E N S

In other respects, lions have been accustomed to give omens to kings, such as the one which appeared to the Emperor Julian...

# Book One.

<margin>A</margin> To the Emperor Gordian <note>Gordian III, Roman Emperor r. 238–244 AD</note>, as he passed by a burial mound with his army, a lion of immense body presented itself as if to attack his battle line, but it was pierced through by a multitude of spear-thrusts. Ammianus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, a 4th-century Roman historian</note> says that the death of the king was portended by this event. However, previously, when Maximinus Caesar was about to engage in battle with Narses, the King of the Persians, a huge lion and a boar were likewise slain and presented at the same time; yet it happened that he departed safe and without loss, having thoroughly conquered that wild nation. And Sandrocottus the Indian <note>Chandragupta Maurya, founder of the Mauryan Empire</note>, who was of obscure birth, when he had escaped the fury of Alexander by the swiftness of his feet and, exhausted, sought sleep in a forest, felt his sweat being licked away by a lion. Trogus <note>Pompeius Trogus, a Roman historian</note> records how, after the prefects of Alexander were overwhelmed, he thereafter seized the kingdom of India.

-> Y E A R S  A N D  M O N T H S . <-

And since we have happened upon auguries, it would not be out of place to mention that a lioness, who was carrying eight cubs in her womb, was slain and disemboweled by hunters in Mesopotamia at the time when Apollonius of Tyana <note>A famous Neopythagorean philosopher and reputed miracle-worker</note> was traveling there. By his interpretation, she indicated that he would remain with Barsanes of Babylon for one year and eight months. When his companions asked why he did not interpret it as nine years—since Calchas <note>The seer of the Greeks during the Trojan War</note> in Homer had understood nine years from a sparrow and eight chicks devoured by a serpent—Apollonius replied: "Those were already born, but these were still in the womb and had not yet completed their gestation; therefore they are likened to months rather than years."

-> E X C E S S I V E  D R I N K I N G  A N D  G L U T T O N Y . <-
<note>original: "Crapula"</note>

There are also those who wish a man gorged to the point of satiety to be indicated by a lion devouring pieces of flesh. For this animal uses food in a very unrestrained manner, and devours many solid things without any carving, whatever it can; then, after its fill, it eats nothing for two or three days. Juvenal <note>A Roman satirist</note> touches upon this voracity where he said the lion must be fed with much meat; and from this it happens that his breath, which is always of a most foul odor, exhales heavily because of indigestion. Wherefore some, signifying a heavy breath, have been accustomed to depict the gaping mouth of a lion. Because of this stench, Martial <note>A Roman epigrammatist</note> seems to have called a woman's private parts a "lion" in that verse:
> Therefore, if you have any shame, Ligella, do not
> pluck the beard of a dead lion.
<margin>C</margin> For he censures her because, although she was an old woman, she attempted to pluck out the hairs. And others have called a stinking brothel by this name. Although there are some who refer the interpretation of this passage to a more far-fetched source, the *Cenchrenae* <note>A type of serpent</note>, which Nicander called "lions"; for serpents of this kind suck out blood by their bite, and physicians agree that the genital seed is made from the most precious decoction of blood, whence Juvenal said:
> Let him indeed receive the reward of blood.

***

-> T H E  C O R T E S A N . <-
<note>original: "Meretrix"</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration shows the profile of a lion on the left and the profile of a woman's head and shoulders on the right, facing each other. The woman has an elaborate classical hairstyle.</image-desc>

It is established by many opinions and monuments of the ancients that courtesans are understood by the name of the lioness. You have something of this sort in Aristophanes' *Lysistrata*, in the oath of the women: "I will not stand as a lioness upon a cheese-grater" <note>A reference to a specific sexual position depicted in ancient art</note>. Because of this kind of wantonness, Ezekiel calls Jerusalem a lioness, and Jehoahaz her cub, who was led into Egypt. Then another cub, Jehoiakim, whom Nebuchadnezzar brought to Babylon in a cage, as the Seventy <note>The Septuagint, the Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> have published, Michael of Byzantium...

<keywords>Gordian, Sandrocottus, Apollonius of Tyana, omens, gluttony, Martial, Lysistrata, Ezekiel, symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the lion as a symbolic figure in classical antiquity, specifically focusing on its association with women, silence, and coinage. Catchword from previous page: "work".</meta>

# Pierius Valerianus: The Lion, or...

<margin>D</margin> [Diodorus] says that certain prostitutes from Megara <note>A city-state in ancient Greece</note> were called "Sphinges" <note>Sphinges is the plural of Sphinx</note>, because they presented a gentleness in their human heads, but the rest of their lion-like bodies indicated the greed and the power they exercised over their lovers. It was common to speak of "Megarian Sphinges" as a term of reproach for prostitutes, specifically because of the impure morals of the Megarians, which all ancient writers condemn. A very charming little six-foot verse by an ancient poet relates to this matter:
->The cruelty of a lioness and a woman are equal.<-
> <note>original Greek: Τῶν λεαίνης καὶ γυναικὸς ὠμότης.</note>
> *The cruelty of a lioness and a woman is equal.*
Furthermore, before the city of Corinth, there was a temple of Venus, and nearby the tomb of Lais <note>A famous Greek courtesan</note>, upon which a statue of a lioness appeared to be placed, grasping a ram between her front paws: this undoubtedly indicated that wantonness for which rams are particularly lecherous, about which we have spoken in its proper place.

### SILENCE.
<term>Taciturnitas</term> <note>The virtue of remaining silent or secretive</note>

The Athenians set up a bronze statue of a tongueless lioness, the work of Iphicrates; by the image of the lioness, they indicated the name of a prostitute <note>The woman's name was Leaina, which means "Lioness"</note>, but by the absence of a tongue, they indicated her silence. For when Harmodius and Aristogiton had entered into a plan to liberate their fatherland from tyrants, and the conspiracy was betrayed, they [the tyrants] seized the "Lioness," a courtesan very familiar to both of them, and tortured her to death with various torments. 
<margin>B</margin> She, however, endured everything with the greatest constancy and betrayed no one—a memorable example of admirable courage beyond female weakness. So that the Athenians might honor her with distinctions, yet not appear to be celebrating a prostitute, they decreed that an animal of the same name, without a tongue to represent her silence, should be erected. Tertullian <note>An early Christian author</note> cites this most steadfast example of this prostitute so that our own people might become more courageous, since even women have been found who, for so slight a cause, were of such brave spirit that for the safety of their friends they not only made light of intolerable torments, but voluntarily increased their own agony. For she herself, as he says, when the executioner was already exhausted, finally spat out her own bitten-off tongue into the face of the raging tyrant, so that she might spit out her very voice, lest she be able to confess the conspirators even if she had wanted to after being overcome.

### THE CYZICENES.

<margin>Money of the Cyzicenes.</margin> If anyone should happen upon a coin that has the image of a lion on one side and Cybele, the mother of the gods, on the other, let him know that it is the money of the Cyzicenes <note>People of Cyzicus, an ancient city in Anatolia</note>, which is quite famous because they were so beautifully engraved. And because they were valued at twenty-eight Attic drachmas—a sum that yields two gold ducats and a little more (for one silver drachma equals the weight of a Venetian Marcello)—these are the "Cyzicene staters," which indicated a thing excellently made.
Furthermore, the superstition of the Magi <note>Ancient Persian priests/magicians</note>, considering the lion's alacrity and its very swift gait, devised the use of a weasel's tooth bound in lion skin for swelling and even weakness of the feet. Lucian mocks this in the character of Tychiades, when a dispute arose between the physicians Cleodemus and Dinomachus, one of them preferring lion skin and the other deer skin, to apply as an amulet through incantations for the suffering Eucrates. The whole controversy between them, while he miserably implores for some relief, is whether the deer or the lion has stronger feet, and which of them is carried with a swifter gait. Rightly, therefore, Virgil’s Aeneas gives the huge hide of a Gaetulian lion to Salius as a prize for speed, who would have earned the first crown in the race had he not been disrupted by the trickery of Nisus.

### WHAT THE HERCULANEAN LION SIGNIFIES.

What the Herculanean lion <note>The Nemean lion slain by Hercules</note> means is more widely known than...

<summary>This page explores the symbolic link between the lioness and female figures, specifically the "Megarian Sphinges" and the heroic silence of the courtesan Leaina. It also discusses the numismatic value of Cyzicene coins and the use of lion skins in ancient medical superstitions.</summary>
<keywords>Sphinx, Megara, Leaina, Harmodius and Aristogiton, silence, Cyzicus, coinage, Cybele, amulets, Virgil, Aeneas</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of leonine symbolism from the previous section, moving from the physical nature of the lion to its role in historical and mythological allegory.</meta>

# Book One.

<margin>A</margin> I shall not attempt to explain this work more broadly in this commentary. However, I should not omit that the Egyptian Hercules—who, as the writers of *Antiquities* <note>likely referring to Berossus or similar ancient historians</note> record, liberated Italy from the bitter yoke of the Giants alongside Osiris—is understood through the symbol of the lion, and no other. For the historians Arrian and Diodorus have shown that there were several men named Hercules; they record that this Egyptian Hercules was the very first of them all to wear the insignia of the lion. Furthermore, Heraclitus of Pontus says the story of Hercules overcoming the lion was invented because that famous Argive Hercules <note>the Greek Hercules/Heracles</note> finally tamed that famous madness from which he suffered greatly due to "black bile" <note>melancholy or rage</note>. For it has been sufficiently explored from what was said previously that this animal is, by its very nature, stirred by immoderate spirits. There are some, however, who mystically understand the light of the Sun through Hercules; since the lion is dedicated to the Sun, it is clear what the relationship between Hercules and the lion signifies. Indeed, the star Phosphorus <note>the Morning Star/Venus</note> is called *jubar* <note>radiance/sunbeam</note> for this reason: because its splendor is spread out in the manner of a lion’s mane <note>original: "iuba"</note>.

### VIRTUE.

<margin>B</margin> In whatever way it is used, the lion’s skin is a <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>a sacred symbol conveying a hidden concept</note> of virtue, and for that reason it is dedicated to Hercules, whom the ancients established as the representative of virtue. Therefore, when Diogenes saw someone priding himself gloriously in that outfit, he exclaimed: "Why do you disgrace the garment of virtue?" Yet we have learned from the commentaries of Apollonius that it was a heroic custom to wear the skin not only of a lion, but also of other animals, where the Poet <note>Homer or Apollonius Rhodius</note> writes:

*And about his shoulders he wore the skin of a small animal and twelve others.* <note>original Greek: Δέρμα δ’ ὁμοῦ παύροιο καὶ δώδεκ’ ἀμφ’ ἔχετ’ ὤμους.</note>

And in Virgil, Evander:
> He twists back the skin of a panther hanging from his left side.

<margin>C</margin> On a coin of Antioch, you may see a certain lion almost crouching on the ground with an owl flying above it, which some have judged to indicate the evening twilight; for as the Sun sets, the night (which they wish to be understood by that bird) arises. I, however, would believe that through a hieroglyphic invention of this kind, it signifies that **strength yields to wisdom.** For strength is understood by the lion, and Minerva <note>the goddess of wisdom</note> by the owl, as these commentaries show in their proper places. I think the lion on the coin inscribed with the word "OF THE MILESIANS" <note>original Greek: MIΛHTΩN</note> has the same meaning; it bows its front feet to the ground while a <term>caduceus</term> <note>the staff of Hermes/Mercury, representing peace and eloquence</note> is extended above it, clearly showing that even the most ferocious strengths yield to the eloquence of the wise. I saw this coin at the house of the Maffei family in Rome. Furthermore, the fact that the statue of Juno at Argos was seen crowned with a vine-shoot, with a lion's skin placed beneath her feet, had this hieroglyphic meaning: it showed the stepmother insulting the spoils of both her stepsons <note>Hercules and Bacchus</note>. They say this was the invention of Callimachus, who gave the vine to Juno. Tertullian touches upon a matter of this kind.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the lion as a symbolic figure in ancient hieroglyphics, specifically focusing on its relationship to celestial bodies and the earth.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano’s *The Lion*, or

<margin>D</margin>
->THE MOON.<-

In the temple of Diana at Olympia, a winged statue of the goddess was once seen, holding a panther in her right hand and a lion in her left. By the wings, let us interpret the swift course of the Moon, which traverses the entire zodiac in the space of twenty-seven days—a path the Sun barely completes in the span of a year. The panther indicates her variations <note>The "spots" or phases of the moon</note>, which we have enumerated elsewhere under twelve names for each month. The lion signifies the power of the Sun, from which she acquires her splendor, shining only in that part which is illuminated by his rays.

->THE SUN. &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; THE EARTH.<-

<image-desc>Two circular woodcut emblems side-by-side. The left emblem (SOL) depicts a radiant male figure with a crown of rays riding a lion. The right emblem (TERRA) depicts a female figure (Ceres/Terra) holding stalks of grain and seated on a lion.</image-desc>
<margin>B</margin>

Among the ancients, it was also customary to create certain radiant statues, fashioned in such a way that the rays of one statue all pointed upward, while those of the other were directed downward; these figures were designed to be carried upon lions. We should know from the commentary of Vettius <note>Vettius Bassus, a Roman grammarian</note> that the ancients understood the Sun and the Earth through this imagery. The Assyrians called them *Adad* and *Adagartin*: *Adad* indeed being the Sun, who, with rays inclined downward, gives life to, soothes, and rears the lower things, being the author of all things born of the earth. *Adagartin*, however, is the Earth, which raises upward the virtue it has conceived from the solar rays, as much as it is able. Thus, like a bride longing for her husband—to borrow a Platonic saying for a moment—crowded with the offspring of all her things, she offers herself up to be held. Indeed, the very image of the lion seems to represent both hieroglyphics, since it depicts the Sun in its foreparts <margin>F</margin> and the Earth in its hindquarters. 

Vettius Bassus, in his commentary on Germanicus (who wrote the *Phaenomena*), considers that *Adagartin* stands for the earth where he discusses Erigone <note>The constellation Virgo</note>. He says most have thought the "Virgin" is called Ceres, evidenced by the ears of grain she holds; others prefer *Adagartin*. <margin>TAMER OF THE SOUL.</margin> There are those who also call her Fortune, since she is placed among the stars without a head. Nigidius Figulo <note>A celebrated Roman scholar and occultist</note> identifies her as Justice or Equity, by which names we have proven elsewhere the Earth itself is called. You will find Ceres called the "Lawgiver" everywhere among the poets, so that this name does not seem foreign to the title of Justice, and it harmonizes with the "most just Earth." Hence, in the works of Maro <note>Virgil</note>, the ship of Aeneas bears a prosperous omen for the subjugation of the land: *First the prow holds the Phrygian lions yoked beneath it.* <note>original: "Prima tenet rostro Phrygios subiuncta leones." From the Aeneid, Book X.</note> Just as you may also sometimes see a man sitting upon a lion, whom he [drives] with a goad...

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration showing a man riding a lion, steering it with a goad or stylus.</image-desc>

<summary>This page explores the lion's role in ancient iconography as a vehicle for the Sun (Adad) and Earth (Adagartin), while also linking the lion to the Moon's light and the goddess Ceres.</summary>
<keywords>Lion, Moon, Sun, Earth, Diana, Ceres, Adad, Adagartin, Vettius Bassus, Nigidius Figulo, Hieroglyphics, Astronomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the lion as a symbolic figure, specifically exploring its dual nature as a representation of both divine and earthly qualities.</meta>

# Book One.
<note>Page 15</note>

<margin>A</margin>
Let him rule with a goad, which seems to signify being the absolute king of the soul: for that ancient reference is not only related to the ancestral rites usually performed in honor of Cybele <note>The "Great Mother" goddess of Phrygia</note>; nor does it only look back to the favor which, as it was added, was most pleasing to the Trojan refugees on Mount Ida.

<margin>Mount Ida was most pleasing to the Trojan refugees.</margin>
Indeed, lions are harnessed to the chariot of the Great Goddess, and they likewise pull the chariot of the Sun; and although these things were handed down so variously by the ancients, they ultimately tend toward one and the same understanding. Finally, this dual nature in the lion—whereby it represents heavenly things with its front parts and the earth with its hind parts—can easily solve that question which causes some to wonder why the image of the lion is attributed in our Holy Scriptures sometimes to Christ and sometimes to the Devil. For it is placed for Christ among the Prophets more than once, as we said above; but for the Evil One, Peter in his first epistle called him a "roaring lion" <note>1 Peter 5:8</note>; and David cries out, "Deliver my only soul from the lions" <note>Psalm 22:20</note>. To these I make answer: the front parts of the lion signify strength and royal virtue, and thus are used for Christ. For Irenaeus <note>Early Christian theologian, c. 130–202 AD</note>, where he argues that the mystery of God is shown in the vigorous carrying out of affairs, in dominating, and in the power and authority of ruling, says that the image of the lion foreshadowed this, which was attributed to one of the four Evangelists <note>The lion is the traditional symbol of St. Mark</note>. However, the lower parts, being the earthly portion, signify flight and treachery: for it tucks its tail under its belly when it flees; it strikes with its tail from the side through treachery. All these things describe the Devil, than whom nothing is more fleeting from the good, and nothing more treacherous toward mortals.

-> CHRIST MAN GOD. <-

<margin>B</margin>
Finally, so that we may philosophize in another way, the lion is heavenly in its front part; therefore the admirable works of Christ set true divinity before our eyes. "For no one," says Nicodemus, "could do those works which he himself did, unless he were from God" <note>John 3:2</note>. In its hind part, it seems to savor of the earth: that part is cold, and from this the lion is fearful and most ready for flight at certain very small things—namely, the crowing of a rooster, a held-out torch, the sight of a light garment, or even at a monkey, the leaves of a holm-oak, and even a sea-onion <note>A type of flowering plant (Scilla)</note>. And the human Christ fears death, though not ignorant of the eternal immortality that would immediately follow; he tries to escape the torture and prays to the Father that the most bitter cup be taken from him; and he was so weakened from it that he could not stand on his knees because of trembling. But if both natures are in Christ, since the lion is of such a kind, it is rightly adapted in Holy Scripture sometimes to God and sometimes to mortals.

<margin>C</margin>
But I would especially approve the opinion of those who think that even the more ferocious spirits subject themselves to justice through a hieroglyphic of this kind. This is because recently, in the suburban village of Cavarzano in the territory of Belluno, a great number of coins were found. On one side, a woman distinguished by a crown and dressed in a long robe sits, extending her hand to the hilt of an inverted sword so that the top of the hilt fills her palm; the letters inscribed around it are: JUSTICE. On the other side is the same image of the woman, almost naked, with her entire garment hung on her right hand stretched behind her, and her left hand placed upon the head of a lion sitting on its hind legs; the letters engraved there are: THE HUMILITY OF THE LION.

<margin>The worship of JUSTICE.</margin>
<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a woman in classical attire sitting atop a lion. The woman wears a crown and a long gown; she holds the hilt of a downward-pointing sword in her right hand, while her left hand rests upon the lion's head. The lion is seated on its haunches, facing left. The background features a sparse landscape with a tree and a small building under a clear sky.</image-desc>

<summary>This page explores the dual symbolism of the lion, representing both the divine strength of Christ and the earthly vulnerabilities or treacherous nature of the Devil. It concludes with an archaeological reference to coins found in Belluno that use the lion to symbolize the submission of ferocity to Justice.</summary>

# Pierio Valeriano’s *The Lion*, or

<margin>
THE OBEDIENCE OF CHILDREN
toward parents.
</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting the goddess Cybele (the Great Mother) seated in a chariot with a spoked wheel, which is being drawn by two lions. She is wearing a crown and holding a drum or tambourine.</image-desc>

As for the tamed lions harnessed to the chariot of the Great Mother <note>Cybele, an ancient Phrygian goddess of the earth and fertility</note>, Lucretius interprets this differently. He suggests the fiction signifies that children, however fierce they may be, ought to be obedient to their parents; for he says:
> They joined the wild beasts to show that offspring, however wild,
> Ought to be softened, conquered by the dutiful care of parents. <note>original: "Adiunxere feras, quòd quamuis effera proles / Officiis debet molliri victa parentum." from Lucretius, *De Rerum Natura*</note>

### AGRICULTURE.

Varro, however, seems to have considered two things regarding Cybele’s chariot. First, that the lion is a symbol of the earth, as we were saying a little earlier. Second, because this kind of animal is found in distant regions very remote from us, he says there is no part of the earth so remote that it should not be cultivated and subdued. Thus, everyone pulls these things—whether they are hieroglyphics or mystical in any way—into their own art or the discipline they profess. Diodorus, however, as is his custom, fashions histories for his own convenience; he says the fiction of lions pulling Cybele’s chariot arose because she was raised by them. Whether the Goddess is carried in a chariot or sits on a throne matters little regarding the meaning; for on a <margin>A coin of Faustina.</margin> coin of Faustina <note>likely Faustina the Elder or Younger, Roman Empresses</note> you may see the Turreted Cybele sitting on a lion-throne, holding an orb between her hand and knee; the inscription reads: TO THE GREAT MOTHER <note>original: "MATRI MAGNÆ"</note>.

### MIXED MEANINGS.

<margin>
A coin of Augustus Caesar.
</margin> Regarding what can be seen on many ancient monuments, especially sepulchral ones—lions having seized some animal, such as a sheep, a bull, a snake, or something of this sort—you can conjecture from this that the soul or nature of the buried person was like the animal that was seized. For instance, if it is a sheep or a lamb, as in the tomb kept at Rome under the portico of Aesculapius, you should understand it as a man who, having demolished his ferocity, embraced gentleness. If a bull, it signifies temperance; if a snake, prudence, or something of this kind, interpreting it according to what the captured animal signifies. If, however, the animal is captured or torn apart in a hostile manner, it signifies an enemy of that nature being treated badly. For on a certain silver coin of Augustus Caesar, you may see a lion of very powerful form overcoming a stag with its teeth fixed in the shoulders; this can perhaps be referred to the victory at Actium <note>The decisive naval battle in 31 BCE where Augustus defeated Mark Antony and Cleopatra</note>, which he—

->FEROCITY LAID ASIDE,<-
->gentleness assumed.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a lion standing over a small sheep or lamb, representing ferocity laid aside in favor of gentleness.</image-desc>

---

->THE VICTORY AT ACTIUM.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a lion attacking a stag, biting it on the shoulder, representing the victory at the Battle of Actium.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of leonine symbolism from the previous section, moving from the fear inspired by Apollo's favor to the complex allegories of the Chimera and the architectural use of lions in sacred spaces.</meta>

# Book One.

<margin>A</margin> He achieved this through the favor of Apollo, in which, as found in Maro <note>The poet Virgil</note>,
> *Every Arab, and all the Sabaeans, turned their backs.* <note>original: "Omnis Arabs, omnes verterunt terga Sabæi."</note>
For we have said that terror is signified by the deer in its proper place.

### THE IMPUDENCE OF LOVE.

Someone here might ask what is meant by the foreparts of the lion, which are said to have been in the Chimera, whose form Homer described thus:
> *A lion in front, a serpent behind, and a goat in the middle.* <note>original Greek: "πρόσθε λέων, ἔπιθεν δὲ δράκων, μέσση δὲ χίμαιρα."</note>

Many famous authors maintain that this signifies the impudence of love, which was restrained by Bellerophon. For such are the beginnings of love, which seems to attack us with leonine ferocity at the very first assault; as the comic poet Epicharmus said:
> *The softness of love must be tamed by the strength of a lion.*
> *Vigorous love is the tamer by means of leonine power.* <note>original Greek: "δαματὴς ἔρως λεοντείᾳ δυνάμει θαλερός."</note>

Horace seems to have looked toward this when he says of a youth captured by a desperate love:
> *Hardly will Pegasus free you*
> *from the entangled, triple-formed Chimera.* <note>original: "Vix illigatum te triformi / Pegasus expediet chimæra."</note>

<margin>B</margin> The goat signifies boldness and wantonness, which the same spirited Hero bravely resisted. The serpent signifies the assault and a fight so dangerous—all of which are explained in their own places throughout this work from specialized commentaries.

### THE THREE PARTS OF ORATORY.

Gregory of Nazianzus, and likewise an interpreter of Hesiod, understand the three parts of Rhetoric through the Chimera: namely, the Judicial part through the lion, because of the terror it strikes into the accused; the Demonstrative <note>or Epideictic</note> part through the goat, because in that genre the style is usually most wanton; and finally the Deliberative part through the serpent, because of the variety of arguments and the longer circuits and coils which are necessary in persuasion. For this reason, the mother of the monster is fashioned by Hesiod with the name Echidna, through whom they contend is understood a *variegated and manifold mind* <note>original Greek: "ποικίλον νόον καὶ πολυειδῆ"</note>, specifically a mind endowed with many and various disciplines.

### THE BEGINNINGS OF SACRED RITES.

Nor should that face of a lion be passed over, which was the first of all seen by Ezekiel, through which those skilled in that discipline say an admonition for initiating sacred rites is offered to us.
<margin>C</margin> It was seen first because from the beginning of life, the consensus of the whole people is accustomed to establish a law for itself. Therefore, at the doors of temples—sometimes supporting the doorposts, sometimes the lintels, sometimes the porches—lions are substituted everywhere, so that we may remember, along with the saying of Maro, or rather the chief precept of the Supreme God, to venerate the Gods first. And there is no nation so wild, nor any spirit so untamed, that it cannot be softened by the taming of religion and its benefit, and thus grow so mild that it will refuse no burden therefrom.

### WHAT THE LIONS OF TREVISO SIGNIFY.

The two lions before the doors, as almost all the more notable temples have, are those of Treviso <note>original: "Tarvisini"</note>. In the porch of the cathedral, the one on the right crushes a winged dragon with its claws dug in; the dragon, with its neck twisted back, bites the lion's breast: indeed, a depraved thought attacks the heart, but a brave soul crushes it immediately. On the left, a lion supports a captured cub against its breast: for a noble soul conquers itself.

### WHAT SPHINXES SIGNIFY.

As for the sphinxes with human heads and the rest of the body like a lion, which are seen standing before temples everywhere, there are those who interpret them entirely as a hieroglyphic device with the understanding that we are warned that human nature stands above all other living creatures. For elsewhere we have discussed how leadership is understood from the head.

b iiij <note>Printer's signature</note>

<summary>This page explores the symbolic meanings of the Chimera and lions in classical and Christian contexts, linking them to the stages of love, the branches of rhetoric, and the architectural guardianship of temples.</summary>

<meta>This page marks the transition from the discussion of the Lion to the introduction of the Elephant and Rhinoceros. The author, Pierio Valeriano, concludes his first book and addresses his patron for the second section.</meta>

<margin>D</margin> It is manifest that the Lion is the prince of all beasts, who, being subject to human command, draws the rest along with him. Indeed, in the Divine Scriptures, we read that dominion over all beasts was given to man. Adamantius <note>A 4th-century writer on physiognomy</note> relates through a Greek myth that the Sphinx, a monster, displayed the form of a maiden in its upper half. Others interpret this to mean that the lordship of the whole earth yields to religion, to which even the most ferocious animals submit themselves and look up to its authority.

->END OF THE FIRST BOOK.<-

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND REVEREND GUIDO ASCANIO SFORZA, CARDINAL OF SANTA FIORA, CONCERNING THOSE THINGS WHICH ARE SIGNIFIED BY THE ELEPHANT AND THE RHINOCEROS ACCORDING TO THE SACRED WRITINGS OF THE EGYPTIANS.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'T' featuring scrolled foliage and classical motifs.</image-desc>
<margin>E</margin> As I was about to release the <term>hieroglyphics</term> <note>In this context, symbolic meanings or "sacred carvings"</note> of land animals for public viewing, I arranged for the Lion to go out first—as he is their master—under the auspices of the most renowned Cosimo de' Medici, Duke of the Florentines. This was both because it has always been the pursuit of that most famous city to nourish lions publicly and to maintain them honorably in the Egyptian manner, and because their noble magnanimity shines forth in the Duke himself through so many praiseworthy examples that this subject should be dedicated to no one else. Next followed the Elephant, an animal than which nature has produced nothing more vast or portentous. Yet nature has distinguished the beast's deformity with so many gifts, and such remarkable ones, that in every course of life the human race (which poets sing of as the "holier animal") seems if not surpassed by this brute, then certainly challenged by it, or competing on equal terms. In this, however, the Elephant excels: by the suggestion of its own nature, it provides lessons in both character and virtue which men confess they can hardly attain unless they are taught. Indeed, there are many things which men have been compelled to take and imitate from him as if from a "model of character." For the Elephant is noble for his innate munificence, distinguished for his temperance, conspicuous for his supreme equity, a chief shunned of madness, an enemy of empty talk, a cultivator of piety, and so celebrated for his gentleness that <margin>F</margin> he shrinks even from the cruelty of others. He disdains to fight against less powerful wild beasts and is never carried away into rage unless provoked by unfair bitterness or some enormous injury. In short, he seems to reflect a regal spirit in all things—not to pile up every detail here. These matters, Most Reverend Lord, will be related in this commentary and proven by cited examples—not so that they might teach you (for as they say, "the sow does not teach Minerva" <note>original: "sus Mineruam" - a Latin proverb meaning an ignorant person should not try to instruct an expert</note>), but so that they may be brought to mind. Nor could this commentary be sent to anyone of your rank more aptly or conveniently than to him who is by far the most renowned for humanity, faith, piety, and the commendation of many virtues which are seen in you beyond your years. Farewell.

<summary>Valeriano concludes his discourse on the Lion and introduces his study of the Elephant and Rhinoceros, dedicating this new section to Cardinal Guido Ascanio Sforza while praising the virtues of the Elephant as a model for human morality.</summary>
<keywords>Lion, Elephant, Rhinoceros, Hieroglyphics, Cosimo de' Medici, Guido Ascanio Sforza, Virtue, Symbolism, Egyptology</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal symbolism from Book One, now transitioning into Book Two with a focus on the Elephant as a hieroglyphic symbol of power and kingship.</meta>

<header>Book Two.</header>

<margin>A</margin>
### STRONG IN ONE'S OWN MIGHT.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'V' featuring a classical figure amidst scrolling foliage.</image-desc>
To begin, therefore, with what the Egyptians have handed down regarding the Elephant: they used this animal—or rather, its trunk alone—as a hieroglyphic to signify a man who is especially wealthy and strong in his own might, needing nothing from others, who can safely say, "all my hope is in myself." Such a man, through a kind of natural instinct, obtains whatever is useful to him. They were moved to this admiration because the elephant possesses a trunk so versatile that it alone suffices for almost all its needs. It uses it in place of a hand (from which it even takes its name <note>The author likely refers to the Greek 'proboskis', meaning 'feeder' or 'hand-like'.</note>); it drinks with it, brings food to its mouth, and raises or offers it to its driver, to whose every command it is most obedient, whether the driver wishes to be lifted to the top of the animal's neck or descend to the ground. Furthermore, it overthrows trees with it, snatches weapons from the hands of combatants in the heat of battle, drags riders from their horses, and hurls seized infantrymen wherever it pleases. Whenever it walks submerged through water, the trunk itself remains raised high so that it may breathe. For this reason, Lucretius, using a precise and elegant term, called elephants "snake-handed" <note>original: "anguimanos"</note>, because it can flex, reflect, contract, extend, smooth, and curve its trunk in every direction like a snake. There are those who affirm that in our own age they have seen elephants with a sword two cubits long <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches; thus a three-foot blade.</note> tied to the trunk, causing the most terrible slaughter in war. Truly, as far as strength is concerned, Cato says in Cicero's writings that when he was a young man, he never desired the strength of a bull or an elephant; which indicates beyond doubt that among brute animals, bulls and elephants surpass all others in might.

### T H E  K I N G.

In addition, the Egyptians understood a King through the image of an elephant. This was not only because they always walk in herds and the oldest leads the group—which was the custom of many nations and peoples in choosing kings, that the office be entrusted to the elders (hence among the Hebrews the "Elders," among the Athenians the "Paleologi" <note>Literally "ancient speakers" or "old words," here referring to elder counselors.</note>, and among the Romans the "Senators" were accustomed to hold the reins of government)—but also because the elephant has this peculiar trait of a royal mind: it does not bend its knees as noticeably as other animals, but only slightly curves the heel. For not bending the knees shows, beyond doubt, that it is not a suppliant <note>A "suppliant" is one who kneels to beg for mercy; the elephant's stiff legs symbolize a refusal to submit.</note>; yet the curving of the heel indicates the humanity with which it is greatly endowed. For among the philosophers who teach morality, the foot is a sign of that affection which is applied to those of lower status. It is indeed fitting for one who holds power over affairs to be humane and moved by human concerns, yet it is clear that he ought not to bend his knees. Even in omens, the elephant was a sign of royal sublimity. One was given to Aurelian long before his reign, and he was the only private citizen at that time to be the owner of an elephant, as can be read in Flavius Vopiscus. There is also the story of Sandrocottus <note>Also known as Chandragupta Maurya, founder of the Mauryan Empire.</note>, an Indian man of humble birth, whom Alexander the Great had ordered to be killed for his boldness of speech. Having escaped in swift flight, and while he was later gathering bandits to wage war against Alexander's governors to liberate India from their rule, a wild elephant of remarkable size offered itself to him of its own accord. With tamed gentleness, it took him upon its back; this was a portent that he would be king, and the outcome did not disappoint. Indeed, having crushed Alexander's governors, he claimed the kingdom of India for himself and flourished with such authority that Seleucus, Alexander's successor in Persia, when he sought to claim India, was satisfied to leave that kingdom to him and make peace. Furthermore, historians record that elephants wage a constant struggle with serpents; serpents, among other meanings arranged in their proper place, signify the world and its provinces...

<summary>This page explores the elephant as a symbol of self-sufficiency and kingship in Egyptian and Classical thought. It highlights the animal's trunk as a "hand" and its physical posture as a metaphor for a ruler's dignity and humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Elephant, Hieroglyphics, Kingship, Strength, Sandrocottus, Alexander the Great, Roman History, Natural History</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the elephant as a symbol in Roman hieroglyphics and numismatics, specifically focusing on its association with imperial power and the conquest of provinces.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano’s Elephant.

...and we have shown that regions are signified by the various gestures of the image. Truly, he who wishes to tame these regions must be endowed with supreme strength, abound in wealth so that he may easily procure the necessities of war, and always exercise arms within the provinces themselves. For this reason, as it seems to me, Nature herself described the knowledge of human affairs through this example; for she fortified the elephant most excellently with its own strength, added ornaments becoming of a king, and bestowed even royal character and virtues upon that one animal above all other brutes, as we shall teach a little further below. Regarding the subjugation of provinces, this can be seen in a coin of Gaius Caesar.

<margin>A coin of Gaius Caesar.</margin>
<margin>D</margin>
<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of a Roman coin showing an elephant with its trunk raised facing a serpent. Below the elephant, the inscription "C A E S A R" is printed.</image-desc>
On the coin of Gaius Caesar <note>Julius Caesar</note>, an elephant is shown with its trunk <term>promuscide</term> <note>The Latin term for an elephant's trunk, often likened to a fly's proboscis in early natural histories.</note> raised, against which a serpent rises up as if to do battle. On another, you may see the serpent crushed under the feet of the elephant itself, bearing the inscription: CAESAR. From the former, you can understand a provocation to war; from the latter, its completion. It does not escape me that in the language of the Moors, an elephant was commonly called a *Caesar*, which Spartianus <note>A Roman historian, one of the purported authors of the Augustan History.</note> records in his life of Aelius Verus, and that the first to be called by the surname Caesar was one who had killed an elephant. But this seems to me an inept reason for the inscription; I would rather believe it refers to the munificence with which Caesar exhibited elephants in public games.

<margin>B</margin>
<image-desc>Woodcut illustration showing an elephant trampling a snake underfoot. In the background is a walled city with multiple towers and a castle-like structure.</image-desc>

->A F R I C A.<-

<margin>A coin of Q. C. Marius.</margin>
As for the elephant you find on the coin of Quintus Caecilius Marius, with ibises added, I would believe that this <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>Valeriano uses "hieroglyphic" to mean a symbolic image that conveys a hidden or sacred meaning.</note> signifies the province of Africa tamed by him, and the extension of the Empire's jurisdiction as far as Egypt. Furthermore, elephants drew the chariot of Pompey the Great in his African triumph. And on a very beautiful silver coin of General Scipio, there is an inscription where his helmet has the appearance of an elephant's head;
<margin>F</margin>

<margin>A silver coin of Scipio.</margin>
below is a plow, and in front, a stalk of grain is placed. All these things refer to the triumph over Africa and the abundance of the grain supply <term>annona</term> brought from there. The inscription reads: Q. METELLUS.

->T H E   F I F T H   L E G I O N.<-

In military standards, elephants were painted on the banners of the Fifth Legion. This was because, when Gaius Caesar was fighting the civil war against Lucius Scipio, that legion requested the task of fighting against the elephants be given to them; by their strenuous action, they provided the opportunity for the rest of the victory.

->T H E   E A S T.<-

But for Augustus, the Senate and People of Rome <note>original: "S. P. Q. Romanus"</note> decreed a chariot drawn by four elephants on coins because, as I think, laden with the spoils of the East, he led an Imperial triumph from there. Tiberius Caesar, after attaining the empire, took care to grant this honor to his father, as the inscription itself testifies, which reads: TIBERIUS CAESAR, SON OF THE DIVINE AUGUSTUS, AUGUSTUS, CHIEF PONTIFF, HOLDING TRIBUNICIAN POWER FOR THE 28TH TIME. TO THE DIVINE AUGUSTUS, FROM THE SENATE AND PEOPLE OF ROME. <note>original: "TIB. CÆSAR DIVI AVG. F. AVGVST. P. M. TR. PO. XXIIX. DIVO AVGVST S. P. Q. R."</note>

<margin>A coin of the Divine Vespasian.</margin>
Claudius took care to decree the same honor for his grandmother Livia in the Circensian procession—namely, a chariot of elephants similar to that of Augustus. So too for the DIVINE AUGUSTUS Vespasian...

<summary>This page explores the elephant as a symbol of Roman military conquest and imperial authority, specifically regarding the subjugation of Africa and the East. It details how the animal appeared on the coinage and military standards of figures like Julius Caesar, Scipio, and various Emperors.</summary>
<keywords>Julius Caesar, Africa, Roman coinage, Fifth Legion, Augustus, military triumphs, numismatics, symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the elephant as a symbol in Roman numismatics and classical moral philosophy, following the previous page's introduction to the animal's royal associations.</meta>

# Second Book.

A chariot drawn by four elephants appears on a coin, the other side of which bears an inscription relating to the remaining honors of a third consulship: for he who had been raised by his own virtue to the summit of Imperial dignity, having conducted affairs well in the East, was to be honored with distinctions equal to those of Augustus: thus the joined Elephant will hieroglyphically signify a subjugated native province. There is also a coin of the Emperor Philip, in which a walking elephant appears under a driver with the inscription, THE ETERNITY OF THE AUGUSTUS <note>original: "ÆTERNITAS AVG."</note>.
<margin>A coin of the Emperor Philip.</margin>

## MUNIFICENCE.

<margin>B</margin>
Now, among the royal virtues, there is one which is accustomed to win the goodwill of peoples above others: munificence <note>generosity or large-scale giving</note>. In displaying this, great princes have made extensive use of the elephant. Hence you may see on a coin of Antoninus Pius an elephant with its trunk <term>promuscis<gloss>the trunk of an elephant</gloss></term> raised upward in a crescent-shaped semicircle: the inscription is, THE MUNIFICENCE OF THE AUGUSTUS. And likewise on a coin of Lucius Septimius Severus Pertinax, Augustus, Emperor for the eighth time, there is an elephant with its trunk curved in the same manner, with that very same inscription, THE MUNIFICENCE OF THE AUGUSTUS, by Decree of the Senate <note>original: "MVNIFICENTIA AVG. S. C."</note>. For this shows that they were exhibited in the spectacles, and from this we are to understand the munificence of those Emperors, who presented them now for combat, now for dancing, and now to climb and walk upon ropes.
<margin>A coin of Antoninus Pius, and of Severus Pertinax.</margin>
<margin>B</margin>
But if you wish to apply this also to the liberality of these animals themselves, you would certainly find them munificent and liberal, whether they were accustomed to bestow gifts upon their keepers or even upon girls with whose love they showed themselves to be captivated: for Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus, a Roman author of natural history</note> writes of their wonderful goodwill toward men and their grateful spirit toward their educators, using the example of that elephant who fought long and hard for his master, and when the master was killed by arrows, the elephant lifted him with his trunk and carried him away to his own stall.

## TEMPERANCE.

But what of the fact that they also expressed the meaning of temperance, or of one who knows how to keep a limit in things, through an elephant taking food? For being accustomed to a certain measure of fodder, if you set out anything more generous or opulent, it takes only as much as it has been trained to eat by daily habit. In Syria, when one was being raised in a house, the master, having received the measure of barley, would intercept half of it every day: and when once, in the presence of the owner, he had set out the entire measure,
<margin>F</margin>
as soon as the elephant noticed that the portion of fodder had been doubled, it divided that barley into two parts with its trunk, as equally as if someone had measured it with a <term>modius<gloss>a Roman dry measure, roughly a peck</gloss></term>, and leaving one portion for the master,
<margin>C</margin>
it consumed its usual part: Plutarch mentions this deed.

## EQUITY.

Nor indeed should another example be passed over, from which we may confess this same animal to be most observant of equity <note>fairness or justice</note>. A certain man used to mix both pebbles and dust into the measure that was paid to the master, in order to cheat him of some of the weight: the elephant noticed meat being boiled at the man's house, and approaching, it threw ash from the hearth into the pot to make things equal <note>a "tit-for-tat" punishment for the man's dishonesty</note>.

<insert>THE AVOIDER OF MADNESS.</insert>

<image-desc>An engraving of an elephant in a wide landscape. The elephant stands in the foreground, facing right with its trunk slightly raised. The background features a winding river or bay, a walled city with towers and classical architecture, and distant mountains under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

It is truly a royal trait to avoid insane and demented men. Therefore, when the Egyptians sought to show a man endowed with such prudence that he wished to have nothing to do with the foolish and the fatuous, they used the elephant and the goat as a hie—

# Pierius Valerianus: The Elephant

<margin>D</margin>
...they were accustomed to represent hieroglyphically. Regarding the Goat, indeed, those things which have come into use were fully described in its own commentary. They say the reason for this image is that the elephant, upon seeing a goat, flees as quickly as possible; so great is the discord of nature and character between them that virtue can in no way tolerate vice. Also contributing to this is the most ancient institution of the Mosaic Law <note>The Torah or Pentateuch</note>, by which princes, when they have transgressed, are admonished to perform sacrifices with a male goat, whereas otherwise private individuals would sacrifice a female goat or a lamb.

<margin>VAIN TALK avoided.</margin>
<margin>E</margin>
When they wished to show that a boastful or vain-talking class of men was held in contempt by any king and diligently avoided, they would paint an elephant and a pig together. For the elephant, upon hearing the grunting of a pig, immediately rushes into flight, not unlike how noble horses—especially those of the Scythians—dread the braying of donkeys with an uncontrollable horror, a fact explored through the example of Darius <note>Referring to a story from Herodotus where Darius's horses were panicked by the braying of donkeys during a Scythian campaign</note>. Furthermore, nothing is so fitting for a King as to remove sarcasm and vain-talking far from his house, and to establish it as an enemy to himself, so that it is a received proverb to attest to "Royal Faith" when asserting those things we wish to be entirely believed. Regarding the pigs, however, which were smeared with pitch and then set on fire and sent by the Megarians against the elephants of Antipatrus <note>A Hellenistic general</note>, causing them to flee in terror, that history is recounted in the commentary on the Pig itself.

->[Image: A woodcut illustration showing an elephant on the right facing a small pig on the left in a rural landscape with hills in the background.]<-

<margin>A KING who pursues VILE THINGS.</margin>
<margin>F</margin>
Because no animal has horns larger or more beautiful than the elephant, for this very reason it also represents a king. For horns, as was declared in the commentary on the Deer, have the significance of dignity and sublime honors. Therefore, if the Egyptians wished to signify a man honored with some greater dignity and title, who nevertheless—having no regard for majesty or decorum—pursued the most vile things and scrutinized all sorts of filth, they proposed the hieroglyphic of an elephant hunting mice; a labor indeed unworthy of such a vast bulk. By this same mark, that deed should be branded with infamy by which we have heard the Emperor Domitian was mocked for piercing flies with a stylus, or his father Vespasian for exacting a tax from urine <note>The famous "pecunia non olet" (money does not smell) anecdote</note>, even though the scent of that small gain seemed good to him.

->[Image: A woodcut illustration of an elephant standing in a field with several small mice scattered around its feet.]<-

<margin>The Elephant ignores mice, a Proverb.</margin>
But concerning this matter there is a well-known proverb: *The elephant ignores mice.* And lest anyone should quibble that the elephant's features are tusks <note>original: "dentes," literally teeth</note> and not horns, Pausanias <note>A 2nd-century Greek traveler and geographer</note> contends they are entirely horns, both by the example of certain animals whose horns grow either on the brow or the nose, and because they fall out when decayed over certain periods of years and new ones sprout up, and they can be softened by fire—which by no means happens with teeth—and because they originate in the skull.

### THE FEARFUL MAN.

It is also a characteristic of almost all kings that they tremble at their own shadow (as they say); thus fear of every single thing is added as a companion to power. The ele—

<meta>The text cuts off at the catchword "ele-", which will begin the word "elephant" on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page explores the elephant as a symbol of kingship, detailing its supposed natural fears of pigs and goats as metaphors for a ruler's avoidance of vice and vain talk. It also discusses the elephant's tusks as "horns" of dignity and mocks rulers who pursue trivial or "vile" matters, such as Domitian hunting flies.</summary>
<keywords>Pierius Valerianus, Hieroglyphics, Elephant, Kingship, Domitian, Vespasian, Pausanias, Symbolism, Natural History, Proverbs</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal symbolism from Book One, now transitioning into the specific hieroglyphic meanings of the elephant in Book Two.</meta>

# Book Two.

<margin>D</margin>
It is evident that the elephant is a hieroglyphic for consternation <note>or overwhelming dread</note>, especially if they are depicted being led to pools of clear water where they can gaze upon themselves as if in a mirror. They dread this sight beyond measure, or certainly shrink from and turn away from it; for that reason, they only drink water that is completely turbid <note>muddy or stirred up</note>. And hence the peoples of India, among whom there is a great abundance of these beasts, are accustomed to observe moonless and cloudy nights if they prepare to swim across any rivers with them.

### WHAT IS ACCOMPLISHED OVER A LONG TIME.

There are those who depict a young elephant—one whose teeth have not yet begun to grow—if they wish to signify something accomplished after a long interval of time. Theophrastus writes that this animal is carried in the womb for ten years, although Aristotle, Aelian, and others set the years at far fewer. Regardless, a common taunt arose from this: "Elephants give birth sooner," used whenever we wish to attack the slowness of some business. Regarding those things which we have no hope can happen, Ennius said, "A locust shall sooner give birth to a Lucanian ox." <note>original: "Lucam bovem"</note> For that is what the Romans first called elephants, because they were first seen by our people in Lucania <note>during the war with Pyrrhus</note>.

<margin>B</margin>
### PROVISION FOR SUSTENANCE.

There is also that celebrated hieroglyphic regarding the elephant found in Horus <note>referring to Horapollo, an author on Egyptian hieroglyphs</note>: if they wanted to indicate a man who is frugal and concerned with his manner of living, they would depict an elephant hiding its teeth in the earth. For they say that as soon as it realizes its teeth are failing <note>falling out</note>, it pulls them out of its own accord and buries them in the ground. However, I would believe this should be read as the animal looking out for its life rather than its sustenance, and that Philip <note>the author's source</note> himself was perhaps deceived by some ambiguity in the Egyptian word. For it is clear from the evidence of accepted authors that elephants sometimes knock out their teeth against trees when being harassed in the hunt, knowing they are sought for the sake of these tusks; thus, by leaving the prey behind, they look out for their own lives. And so that they do not provide a sign to hunters, whenever the teeth fall out due to old age, they bury them even then in the soil. Nevertheless, let everyone be free to follow whichever opinion they prefer.

<margin>F</margin>
### THE EFFECTS OF CONCORD AND DISCORD.

<margin>C</margin>
To this meaning we shall subjoin that by which some have—not unsuccessfully—devised a new invention to depict the sentiment of Sallust, which is: "By concord small things grow, by discord the greatest things fall apart." In the first image, an Ant grows into an Elephant with a caduceus <note>the staff of Hermes, representing peace/negotiation</note> placed nearby; in the second, an Elephant ends as an Ant, with water and fire added as the most contrary of things. Truly, if you remove the caduceus, water, and fire from this sort of fiction, a perfect understanding can still be signified: one who has best attained knowledge of the smallest things as well as the greatest, and has joined them all into one single science, as it were. But indeed, I do not deny that this interpretation, like the other, is arbitrary. But someone will say, "Why an Elephant compared to an Ant?" Furthermore, Plutarch, having spoken many magnificent things about the ant—which is the smallest of all animals—professes that he will say similar things about the largest.

### STUPOR.

And here is an animal that has exhibited so many proofs of mind that (as Cicero asserts in the first book of *On the Nature of the Gods*) "no beast is more prudent"; yet, because of its sheer vastness, it is held by some as a mark of stupor <note>dullness or senselessness</note>. This can be read in Diogenianus, to whom Philostratus also seems to subscribe when he said that neither the elephant nor its teeth have anything to do with the lyre, nor indeed with skilled men. These are the words of Amphion <note>a legendary musician</note>, which I have chosen to set down in Greek because they are read in a corrupted form in the printed copies: "The elephant has never touched the lyre, O men." <note>original: "Ἐλέφας οὐδαμοῦ τῆς λύρας ἤπτω οἱ αἴθρωποι"</note>

<sig>C</sig>

<summary>This page explores the symbolic meanings of the elephant in Renaissance thought, ranging from its fear of its own reflection to its use as a metaphor for long-term projects and the preservation of life. It also discusses the elephant's relationship to the ant as a symbol of growth through harmony and its paradoxical reputation for both great intelligence and physical dullness.</summary>
