# Pierio Valeriano’s Elephant

<margin>D</margin> <note>original Greek: "ἀλλ᾽ οὔτ᾽ αὐτῷ τῷ θηρίῳ, οὐδ᾽ ὅτι τοῖς κέρασιν αὐτοῦ χρῶνται"</note> "But neither for the beast itself, nor because they use its horns <note>ivory</note>." This is much like the proverb told of the donkey: "He has nothing to do with the lyre" <note>A Latin expression meaning someone is totally unsuited for a task, like a donkey trying to play music</note>. Indeed, Plautus follows this sentiment: "My master has the heart of an elephant, not his own, and has no more wisdom than a stone." Regarding this sentiment, the Greeks say, "An elephant would never come from an elephant" <note>meaning a great thing cannot come from something dull-witted, or perhaps referring to the difficulty of breeding</note>. Even Tertullian calls it a "most foolish beast," even if it carries some image of divine writing. Eucherius says that a man who has committed great and enormous crimes is indicated by this sign, citing the passage of Solomon for this: "And they brought to Solomon apes and elephants" <note>1 Kings 10:22; usually translated as ivory, but interpreted here as the animals themselves</note>.

And yet the Indians—though their elephants are the most ferocious of all, so much so that they can be tamed only with difficulty—pay close attention to the striking of musical instruments and the melodies of songs; they prick up their ears to listen, and are so soothed by this that no other device restrains their wildness more effectively. Truly, authors place elephants into three kinds: those of the marsh, the mountain, and the plain. Those of the marsh are said to be entirely mindless and fickle; those of the mountain are perverse and treacherous; but those of the plains are more easily tamed and far more teachable. Those who have seen them in theaters do not consider them stupid at all, asserting from the testimony of many that they are skilled in recognizing signs, figures, and various changes.

It is recorded that this happened in Rome: when a troop of them was being taught dances and gliding circles, one was found to be slower at learning. When he had been scolded and punished by his masters, he was seen at night, by the light of the Moon, attempting those lessons of instruction by himself, meditating and practicing. But perhaps these things, which they are forced to learn with hunger as their teacher, should not seem so admirable; let us rather admire those things which they do in the wild, though never instructed by any laws: in integrity, prudence, <margin>E</margin> and equity, they are inferior to almost no other living creatures.

->PIETY<-
<image-desc>A woodcut illustration titled "PIETY" depicting an elephant standing in a landscape, raising its trunk toward a radiant, personified sun in the sky above clouds.</image-desc>

What of the fact that, endowed by their own accord with a certain sublimity of nature, they cultivate piety and observe religion? For when the New Moon appears, they go of their own will to where they live by their own law and purify themselves in a running river. If they are troubled by diseases, they implore the help of the Gods, tossing herbs toward heaven, sending up prayers with the herbs acting as messengers. This gesture is certainly to be adopted by those who wish to express Piety through a painted elephant. Likewise, being closest to human senses, they understand their native tongue, they seek glory and honors, and when marked by disgrace, they prefer death to an infamous life; sometimes, when other opportunities for suicide are denied, they have ended their lives by starvation. How often, indeed, when overwhelmed in the theaters and having lost hope of escape, have they entreated the mercy of the common people <margin>F</margin> and, bewailing themselves with a kind of lamentation, moved the entire gathered audience in the stands to tears.

->GENTLENESS<-
<image-desc>A woodcut illustration titled "GENTLENESS" showing an elephant in a landscape gently interacting with a small horned animal, such as a goat or sheep, using its trunk.</image-desc>

Since they possess such equity and gentleness that they can by no means be forced to serve the cruelty of others—as was seen long ago in the failed attempt of Bocchus <note>King of Mauretania</note>—they utterly refuse to fight against weaker beasts, and do not fight larger ones unless provoked. When walking among a flock of sheep, they move aside those they encounter with their hand <note>trunk</note>...

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the word "move."</meta>

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of the elephant, contrasting its proverbial reputation for dullness with its observed intelligence, religious devotion to the moon, and inherent sense of justice and gentleness. Valeriano uses classical and biblical references to establish the elephant as a symbol of Piety and Gentleness.</summary>
<keywords>elephant, symbolism, Piety, Gentleness, animal intelligence, Roman theaters, moon worship, Plautus, Solomon, natural history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the elephant's nature from Book Two, focusing on its legendary gentleness, intelligence, and capacity for anger when provoked.</meta>

# Book Two.

<margin>A</margin> [The elephants] take care lest they accidentally trample anyone, and they possess such great reverence for this weaker flock <note>referring to humans or smaller animals</note> that even wild beasts are wonderfully tamed at the sight of a ram and become gentle and manageable. The author of this is Plutarch in the twelfth book of his *Table Talk* <note>original: "Symposiacon"</note>. What of the fact that when they encounter a man wandering in the wilderness, they not only refrain from killing him or doing him any other injury, but mildly and calmly offer themselves as guides and show him the way? Regarding what we were saying about their speech—that they understand their native tongue and even have a specific language among themselves—Oppian <note>A Greco-Roman poet known for works on hunting and fishing</note> records this, though he says it is understood only by their masters. Indeed, it has become well known to our age through clear experience that they are obedient to the word of their master, whether they must move forward or retreat. They learn from the master’s speech whom to strike, from whom to abstain, and whom to attack, and when their anger must be restrained. Thus, the opinion is not without merit that there is a kind of fellowship between this beast and the human race, such that we may repeatedly employ the saying of Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero</note> on that matter. Since, therefore, the elephant seems in a way to be an image of just and moderate rule, it has deservedly earned the name of "King," both for the virtues we recounted above and for this very mildness and mercy. Marcus Antonius, surnamed

<margin>B</margin> the Philosopher <note>The Emperor Marcus Aurelius</note>, used to say that there was nothing that more commended a Roman Emperor to the nations than mercy <note>original: "clementia"</note>, and for that reason, he did not wish to act cruelly even toward his rebels. This quality added Caesar to the number of the Gods, this consecrated Augustus, and this gave the surname "Pious" <note>Antoninus Pius</note>. For this same reason, the Roman Senate—freed from the fear it had contracted from the cruelty of the Emperor Maximinus—decreed statues with elephants for the most merciful Emperors Maximus, Balbinus, and Gordian. Although for another reason, a chariot drawn by four elephants was decreed for Gordian III, namely because he had conquered the Persians and was to celebrate a Persian triumph. But what great mildness there was in this brute animal, as Plutarch relates! At Rome, when some boys had playfully pricked an elephant’s trunk with their writing styluses, the elephant, becoming angry, snatched one of them up as if to hurl him on high. When a huge cry was raised by the frightened children, who were mourning the fate of their companion about to die, the elephant, perceiving

<margin>C</margin> such great sorrow from everyone, gently placed the boy he had seized back on the ground, considering it enough to have punished the boyish audacity with that fright.

->**ANGER PROVOKED.**<-
<note>Translation of the image text: "IRA LACESSITA"</note>

*A rectangular woodcut illustration showing an elephant in the center. The elephant has its trunk wrapped around a small child, lifting him into the air. To the right of the elephant, a group of four small figures (representing the children mentioned in the text) watch with gestures of alarm or distress. In the top right corner of the illustration frame, the text "ANGER PROVOKED" is printed.*

However, although they are gentle by nature, they are nevertheless sharply incited to anger, and when disturbed by some more bitter irritation, they become monstrously wild (for as it says in the Divine Scriptures, "A just man, when he is angry, is worse") <note>A common proverbial sentiment often attributed to biblical wisdom regarding the intensity of a righteous man's wrath</note>. In that state, they are as dangerous to their own drivers as to the enemy. Since Hannibal was not ignorant of this nature in the animal, he turned it to his own use. Indeed, when he could not compel the elephants to cross a certain river and did not have a supply of material to build ships, he ordered a very fierce elephant to be wounded under the ear, and commanded the striker to run forward immediately by swimming across the river. The elephant, exasperated to pursue the author of his pain, crossed the river, and thus provided an example of daring to the rest. This is recorded in the first book of *Stratagems* by Julius Frontinus; Livy records that this happened during the crossing of the Rhone, although he admits the memory of the event is varied. Truly, the Moors affirm that the elephant has a double heart: with one it becomes angry, and with the other it is led to gentleness. And this is what [explains] both of these functions beyond measure.

<sig>c ij</sig>

<summary>This page explores the elephant's dual nature of extreme gentleness and fierce anger, citing classical anecdotes of elephants acting as guides to lost travelers and showing mercy to children. It also describes Hannibal's tactical use of an elephant's rage to force a river crossing.</summary>

# Pierio Valeriano on the Elephant

<margin>
A pleasant story about the elephant.
</margin>

...continues. Indeed, up until these times, the elephant had been considered a creature of fable in Italy through a long series of centuries. Nor could any man be persuaded that such a misshapen and immense beast—as large and strange as those seen in pictures—could excel in those virtues for which it was held as distinguished among the historians. For although three hundred years ago the Emperor Frederick II <note>Frederick II (1194–1250), Holy Roman Emperor, who famously kept a menagerie.</note> transported an elephant to us from Idumæa <note>A region south of Judea; here used generally for the East.</note>, and in a later age another was seen in Italy, nothing remained in memory concerning them beyond the vastness of their bodies, which was deemed worthy of celebration.

However, the elephant Hanno fulfilled all the credibility of ancient history. As we said at the beginning, he was sent as a gift to Pope Leo X by Manuel, King of Portugal and conqueror of India. We saw him at Rome, Most Reverend Lord: he displayed such examples of character, intelligence, and docility at that tender age (for he was brought here at four years old) that we not only believe what was handed down by the writers, but there is a suspicion that the authors themselves were almost envious of this genus of animal, and downplayed many things that ought to have been illustrated far more magnificently. Hence it happened that I have approached the task of recounting its meanings and their causes more expansively; for before I inspected the animal, I feared lest a good part of these accounts be taken for old wives' tales. We can, however, profess this: regarding other animals, we acquiesce to the authority of writers in whatever seems to exceed belief; but regarding the elephant, whatever wonders are handed down, we have detected in Hanno—by the evidence of our own eyes—either the equal or things far greater than those ancient accounts. We call Rome itself, the most crowded theater of the whole world, as witness to this fact.

But I must by no means conceal in this place what Michael Sylvius <note>Miguel da Silva (c. 1480–1556), Portuguese ambassador to Rome.</note>—the ambassador of his King to the Supreme Pontiff, a man, as you know, of the highest integrity and most sacred faith—and others have narrated, confirming the matter by the testimony of many, concerning the character of this same Hanno. When King Manuel had destined him to go to Rome as a gift for the Supreme Pontiff, and the ship he was to board was already equipped and ready for departure, it could in no way be brought about for several days that Hanno would either allow himself to be led to the ship or, if he were somehow led there, be willing to board it; he so recoiled from a departure of this kind with such aversion.

When the King was quite anxious over this matter and invited everyone with great promises who would undertake the business of leading and transporting Hanno, and yet no one would offer their name, it was finally whispered into the King's ears that this was happening through the trickery of the beast's keeper. This man, desperately in love with a certain girl, was loath to be sent away to such distant regions, and had therefore persuaded Hanno not to allow himself to be taken away to be carried off. He told the beast he was being taken to sterile, unpleasant, and barbarous places, where he would be treated with mockery and every insult as an animal from a hostile land, and that neither the necessities nor the comforts for his food or adornment would be supplied. Added to this was a long and inconvenient journey, and the vast surface of the sea to be plowed, so that it would be better to be slaughtered at once in the land where he then was, than to arrive at a most wretched life through so many difficulties of the road.

The King, having learned of this, ordered Hanno's keeper to be summoned to him immediately. He told him he had discovered his tricks and commanded that he see to it that Hanno was placed on the ship within three days. If this were not done quickly, he threatened that he would immediately punish the man with the ultimate penalty, to serve as an example to others who dared to deceive the royal commands. Terrified by the King's severity, and seeing no safety in delay, the elephant's keeper approached his pupil, the beast. He told him that he had been misled by the deceit of certain malevolent people who envied the happiness of them both and were disobedient to the best of Kings; the matter was far different than what those liars had said. For he was not being sent to a cruel...

<summary>Valeriano describes the arrival of the elephant Hanno in Rome, noting that its intelligence surpassed ancient accounts, and recounts a story of how Hanno's keeper nearly sabotaged his transport to Italy due to a romantic entanglement.</summary>

# Second Book.

<margin>A</margin>
...they were not about to set out for desolate and wild places, but to the city that is mistress of the world <note>Rome</note>, situated in a lovely spot, where a most fertile abundance of all things and delights—as many as even the most greedy person's desire could seek—would overflow. There, he said, was the Prince, to whom almost the entire world rises in respect: in whose presence he would be held as a favorite, and like a "nightingale's chick" <note>A Latin idiom for a pampered or precious pet</note>, he was to be nourished with an affluence of luxuries and charms. Therefore, he should be of good heart, and at the first opportunity, joyful and eager, he should board the ship with him; the Gods would be present for the voyage and would follow their entire course with incredible success. Thus Hanno, having been led to a different opinion by the same master, boarded the ship of his own accord when the signals were given, and never refused any commands throughout the entire journey.

-> ## ON THE RHINOCEROS. <-

<margin>The Rhinoceros on the coins of Domitian.</margin>
Those who have examined the Rhinoceros on the coins of Domitian <note>Roman Emperor, r. 81–96 AD</note> are accustomed to ask what that symbol signifies. I would say it was done for the flattery of the Prince, who presented animals of this kind in the public spectacles, a gift of the sort celebrated by Valerius Martial <note>A Roman poet famous for his epigrams</note>:

<margin>B</margin>
> *The Rhinoceros, exhibited for you, Caesar, in the whole arena,*
> *Provided battles which he did not promise.*
> *O, how he blazed into terrible anger, head down,*
> *How great was his horn, to which a Bull was but a tossing-ball.*
> <note>original: "Præstitit exhibitus tota tibi Cæsar arena..."</note>

For there are those who understand, through the hieroglyphic of this animal, a slowness to anger followed by a heat of passion that knows no limit once provoked. For authors agree on this: that it requires great irritation; but when it begins to be angry, it is most ferocious, which Martial expressed in another epigram:

> While the trembling trainers provoke the Rhinoceros,
> And the great anger of the beast gathers itself for a long time.
> The battles of the promised God of War were despaired of,
> But yet that previously known fury returned.
> For with his double horn he lifted the heavy bear,
> Just as a bull tosses the balls placed upon him toward the stars.

-> ANGER MADE MORE FEROCIOUS <-
-> BY ITS SLOWNESS. <-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a rhinoceros and a bear. The rhinoceros is armored with thick, folded skin and a sharp horn. A bear is depicted above or behind the rhinoceros, which appears to be tossing or confronting it. This illustrates the quote about the rhinoceros tossing a bear like a bull tosses a ball.</image-desc>

-> A POWERFUL KING ATTACKED BY <-
-> THE STRATAGEM OF A WEAKER ONE. <-

<margin>C</margin>
There are those who, wishing to represent a powerful king harassed by the arts of someone weaker, depict an elephant provoked into combat by a Rhinoceros. For this beast is born a natural enemy to the elephant, nearly equal to it in length, but with much shorter legs, and of a boxwood color, as Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist</note> says. Having sharpened its horn against rocks, it prepares itself for the fight: in the struggle, it especially aims for the belly, which it understands to be softer, and having pierced it, it immediately overcomes the elephant.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a rhinoceros in combat with an elephant. The rhinoceros is positioned low, aiming its horn toward the elephant's soft belly. The elephant is larger, with prominent tusks and a trunk.</image-desc>

-> ### THE ROBUST MAN. <-

Indeed, the Divine Scriptures, as Eucherius <note>Saint Eucherius of Lyon, a 5th-century bishop</note> relates, accept the image of the Rhinoceros to represent the strong and robust man; and that passage of Job is cited from the Hebrew: *Will the rhinoceros be willing to serve thee?* <note>Job 39:9</note> The rhinoceros, he says, is a terrible beast, which has twin horns on its nose; and so we have observed on the coins of Domitian, in which the Rhinoceros...

<sig>c iij</sig>

<summary>This page discusses the symbolism of the rhinoceros in Roman coinage and literature, focusing on its slow-to-boil but unstoppable anger and its legendary rivalry with the elephant. It also touches upon the animal's appearance in the Bible as a symbol of strength.</summary>
<keywords>Rhinoceros, Emperor Domitian, Martial, animal symbolism, Elephant vs Rhinoceros, Pliny the Elder, Book of Job, anger, strength</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Rhinoceros and the Unicorn, likely following a description of the Elephant's natural history and its symbolic rivalry with the Rhinoceros.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano’s Rhinoceros

He is seen distinguished by a double horn on his nostrils, such as Martial <note>Marcus Valerius Martialis, a Roman poet known for his Epigrams; he described a rhinoceros in the Roman arena.</note> celebrates. Furthermore, one was <margin>D</margin> recently brought from Lower India to the King of Portugal, an image of which we saw sent to Pope Leo X. <note>This refers to the famous "Ganda," the rhinoceros sent to Rome in 1515, which drowned in a shipwreck but was immortalized in a sketch by Albrecht Dürer.</note> It indeed has one horn on the nostril, while another breaks out above—not so large, but all the Portuguese who saw the animal attest that it is very powerful. But this is far more curious to observe: in the Divine Scriptures, the "monoceros" <note>original: μονοκώρωτα; Greek for "single-horn" or Unicorn.</note> and the "naricorn" <note>original: εἰκονώρωτα; likely a corruption or specific term for a "nose-horn" or Rhinoceros.</note> have been accepted confusedly in many places, specifically by new and old theologians. Because they were ignorant of natural history, they believed these two were one and the same; thus, you may find "unicorn" placed by some and "naricorn" by others in one and the same passage. I think this happened because, as we said, many of them thought the Rhinoceros and the Monoceros were the same animal, whereas they are otherwise quite different. Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist.</note> placed each separately, saying in Book Eight, Chapter Twenty-One, that the Monoceros is a most fierce wild beast, with a body like a horse, the head of a deer, feet like an elephant, the tail of a boar, a deep bellow, and one black horn two cubits long projecting from the middle of its forehead.

This description indeed signifies the one the common people call the "Halicorn" <note>A variant of "Alicorn," the horn of a unicorn.</note>, who they say is so loving of chastity that he can only be captured by the help of a <margin>E</margin> virgin girl. Hunters place her sitting where they notice the animal goes to drink or feed. For the unicorn runs to such a girl, places his bowed head in her lap, and is soon occupied by a very deep sleep. When the girl gives the signal to the hunters, they rush in and capture the beast with no trouble. This is done for a single reward: they proclaim it is powerful against poisons. Indeed, its shavings are said to heal most effectively, and if a piece of it is placed on the bedding when something tainted by poison is brought near, it will break out in a sweat.

Pliny, however, places the Rhinoceros in the same book, Chapter Twenty, as one who has a horn not on the forehead, but on the nostrils. He is the other enemy of the elephant, born of the Dragon, and what was brought up earlier regarding the battle between the two. Certainly elsewhere in the Divine Scriptures, the Rhinoceros is a hieroglyphic for a strong and robust man, as Eucherius <note>Saint Eucherius of Lyon, a 5th-century theologian.</note> notes, citing the passage from Job: *Will the rhinoceros be willing to serve thee?* And you may find it written in Balaam, *Whose strength is like that of the Rhinoceros*, as almost all Latin interpretations have it, although Sanctes <note>Santes Pagnino, a Dominican philologist and Bible translator.</note> rendered it "unicorn." Therefore, if any divination is to be made, since in this Twenty-First Psalm the comparison refers to <margin>F</margin> the most robust and untamable animals, and implores divine help against their onset and force—as the Chaldeans and Arabs explained: *Save me from the most ferocious and strongest like the lion, and from the powerful king, whose power is like the Naricorn*—and since many readings approve this, I would not hesitate to assert that one should read "Rhinoceros" in this place.

But where mention is made of a more lovable animal in Psalm Twenty-Eight, called *Sarion* among the Hebrews, which the Greeks rendered as "beloved as the son of unicorns" <note>original: ἠγαπημένος ὡς υἱὸς μονοκερώτων</note>, the Latins rendering it word for word as "beloved like the son of Unicorns"—and even the old translation has "Unicorns"—I believe there is no doubt that the more chaste reading of that place is "of the unicorns" <note>original: μονοκερώτων</note>.

And these, my Lord, are the things I was able to collect concerning the Elephant (who brought the Rhinoceros with him) despite my busy schedule. You, who are accustomed to spending your whole life and every hour in diligent reading, will perhaps find far more illustrious and recondite things. If these could not reach my hands, I must be indulged; for you are not unaware of how much the service of Princes and the disturbances of business subtract from my literary studies, calling me away with frequent interruptions. Therefore, you and others who look upon the account of my life, be more fair to me, especially since Maro <note>The poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note> warns that there is no one who can do all things.

AD

<summary>Valeriano distinguishes between the Rhinoceros (nose-horn) and the Monoceros (forehead-horn/unicorn), correcting theological confusions by citing Pliny and biblical translations. He concludes with a personal note on his busy life serving princes, which limits his scholarly output.</summary>
<keywords>Rhinoceros, Unicorn, Monoceros, Pliny the Elder, Pope Leo X, Biblical translation, symbolism, medicinal horn, virgin capture myth</keywords>

# BOOK THREE.

# TO THE MOST LEARNED LELIO TORELLI,
# CHIEF JUSTICE TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS COSIMO, DUKE OF THE
# Florentines, concerning those things which are signified by the bull and
# every kind of cattle according to the sacred
# writings of the Egyptians.

<note>The "sacred writings of the Egyptians" refers to hieroglyphics, which Renaissance scholars interpreted as mystical symbols of moral and philosophical truths.</note>

I might seem to sin against the public good, most learned Torelli <note>Lelio Torelli (1489–1576) was a prominent jurist and auditor to Duke Cosimo I de' Medici.</note>, if I did not blush to dedicate these "bellowings" of my Bull to the Most Serene Duke Cosimo—mine, or rather yours, or rather the patron of all honorable men. For although I am not unaware that he applauds the interests of scholars, following the example of his ancestors—who all acquired the highest and eternal praise by ensuring, with all zeal and effort, that letters, virtues, and all good character should flourish and be held in honor in Italy, and especially in that most renowned city of Florence—nevertheless, the business by which this most distinguished young man is currently detained, amidst such great movement and disturbance of Italian affairs, does not permit me to approach him importunely. This is true even though he tempers and moderates all things most wisely and with great commendation from his citizens, so that everyone admires the care and zeal of an old man in such a youthful age.

Therefore, I have given the Bull to be nourished and cared for by Torelli instead, a most kind friend, for whom the food upon which our Bull feeds is abundantly sufficient. Thus, when it is opportune and the matter itself suggests it, Torelli may, in his own time and place, bring forth and reason upon those things in the Bull which are praiseworthy and even worthy of great admiration. He may occasionally remind the Prince, who is of outstanding intellect, of the gifts with which this animal is endowed and—if one may say so—the virtues for which it is considered distinguished.

For as to the things that men do rightly and laudably: since they were born to do these things, and each person can take examples from his own mind which excels in reason, and because these gifts flow from human nature, they are not so much to be wondered at in us. But the things that wild animals do—to whom almost single impulses are granted for single actions—if they are carried beyond the boundaries of their own nature, they are judged all the more precious the more they are found at times to challenge the human race. We recounted many such admirable things regarding the Elephant in the last Commentary <note>original: "Commentario"</note> addressed to the Most Reverend Sforza <note>Guido Ascanio Sforza di Santa Fiora (1518–1564), a powerful Cardinal and patron.</note>, and we are now about to say things perhaps not inferior to those concerning the bull or every kind of cattle.

<sig>c iiij</sig> <note>Printer's signature mark for gathering the pages.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the formal dedication of Book Three to Lelio Torelli, transitioning from the previous study of elephants to the symbolic significance of bulls and cattle in Egyptian hieroglyphics. The author praises the Medici family's patronage of the arts and justifies studying animal behavior as a mirror for human virtue.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bull (Taurus) as a hieroglyphic symbol, specifically focusing on its association with self-control and moderation. The catchword from the previous page, "ditatem," likely concluded a word like "pudicitatem" (modesty/chastity).</meta>

# Pierius Valeriano: The Bull.

<margin>D</margin>
->### TEMPERANCE.<-

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'A' featuring an ornamental design with what appears to be a bird (possibly a swan or goose) with its neck entwined through the letter.</image-desc>
When the Egyptian Priests observed a certain admirable restraint in the Bull, they used its image to signify a man endowed with temperance—one who is not indeed a stranger to human passions, but is most observant of modesty. They would depict a Bull of a very robust and powerful nature; it seems to me that Philostratus described such a likeness in his account of Pasiphaë, when he portrays that leader of the herd as combative, proud, exultant, with beautiful horns, snowy in color, a broad throat, a thick neck, and dewlaps hanging far and wide, with limbs that inspire awe at the mere sight. Indeed, we see such a face on a certain silver coin inscribed with the name **M. L. THORIUS**. <note>Marcus Livius Thorius, a Roman mint magistrate known for coins featuring a charging bull.</note> Philosophers provide the reason for this hieroglyphic: although this animal is extremely hot-blooded and particularly potent in its loins—so much so that it can most effectively impregnate with a single mounting without further movement—it is also restrained. If it should happen to wander from its natural place, it might wound a cow in whatever part it encounters with the sheer strength of its loins, as if it had attacked her with a sharp weapon. <margin>E</margin> Indeed, its seed is so ready that Aristotle records that a castrated bull has been known to impregnate; hence many ancient writers called the male genitals "the Bull," just as they called the female parts a <term>brechlion <note>original Greek: βρέχλιον; a rare anatomical term</note></term>. Yet this animal, though so incited and raging with lust, mounts no more than twice a day; moreover, from the moment the cow conceives, it becomes modest and chaste, attempting nothing further. Many attribute the cause of this to the cow, who—much like does or even tortoises—rarely endures the bull because of the stiffness and excessive tension of his genitals. Hence Horace said of a fleeing girl:

> She is not yet strong enough to bear the yoke with a submissive neck, <br/>
> nor to match the weight of a rushing bull, <br/>
> nor to endure the duties of a mate in love. <note>Horace, Odes 2.5</note>

But it is clear that this example of restraint has its cause in the bull itself, for the bull voluntarily withdraws and beds down apart once conception has occurred, as if a divorce had taken place. As can be seen especially in Epirus, he often disappears for a space of three months, but wanders—as Virgil writes—"his snowy flank supported by soft hyacinths, chewing the pale grass under a dark oak." And, as Aristotle says, he <term>apegela <note>original Greek: ἀπηγελά; meaning to leave the herd</note></term>, which <margin>F</margin> some have not unhappily translated as "to un-herd," that is, he grazes apart from the company of cows, their flock, and their society. I wanted to mention this so that everyone might know in what posture a bull should be painted to signify restraint. To return to the word *apegela* and the proverb from history, these things aim to show that we should understand a limit placed on petulance and the highest form of restraint from that picture. Perhaps that line in Theocritus should be understood in this way: "the bull has gone away to the woods." <note>Theocritus, Idylls 1.75</note> However, because these matters have been treated copiously and successfully by many most learned men, especially **Erasmus of Rotterdam**, we shall pursue them no further. Nor am I unaware that in Epirus there were "Pyrrhic cows" of wondrous size which, because they were kept free from Venus <note>i.e., sexual intercourse</note> and entirely untouched, were called <term>asygeu <note>original Greek: ἀσυγεῦ; likely a corruption of a term for "unyoked" or "untouched"</note></term>. But this was given to them by the care of men, not by nature. These same cows were never yoked, and were thus called "unyoked" because they displayed a symbol of untainted modesty. They were sacrificed primarily to Minerva as a sign of virginity, which knows no yoke. From these examples it is clear enough that in the tempering of lust, we are beyond doubt surpassed by this species, which is otherwise known to be most provocative and most combative toward rivals. Therefore, it is for no slight reason that the celestial Taurus is dedicated to Venus; nor did the wisest Poets idly call Venus "golden," she who represents a sincere, chaste, and pure desire for procreation...

<summary>This page explores the Bull as a symbol of temperance and sexual restraint in Egyptian and Classical thought. Despite its physical power and potency, the bull's natural habit of withdrawing from the herd after mating is presented as a model for human modesty and self-control.</summary>
<keywords>Taurus, Temperance, Egyptian Hieroglyphics, Aristotle, Philostratus, Erasmus, Epirus, Chastity, Animal Symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the symbolic nature of the bull and the ox, transitioning from physical characteristics to moral virtues like continence and modesty.</meta>

# Book Three.

<margin>A</margin> Heaven inspires wealth <note>original: "additatem"</note>, not petulant lewdness, foul adulteries, or the shameless dishonor of the human race. Mortals devoted to pleasure, while pursuing it more greedily than is right through total lack of self-control, dare to claim they are driven by the force of the stars. They do this to spread a veil over their own impudence, even though Plato most truly says that nothing emanates from heaven that is not entirely holy. But if we cannot be trained toward honor by the customs of civil life, nor by the sciences, nor the precepts of the wise, nor even by the threats of the law—well then, let us now obtain a right way of living from the brutes. By following their natural character, let us bring aid to our own weakness. Fame sings that Drusus was endowed with such self-restraint; he remained faithful to his wife for as long as he was on the Germanic expedition. And regarding what we were saying about the bull’s habit of sleeping apart, they say that Pescennius Niger was also a stranger to venereal matters, except for what was required for procreating children. You will find examples of this kind even in more recent histories, such as that of Pemo of Belluno, whom the people of Udine chose as their Prince because of his integrity, justice, and supreme wisdom. <margin>B</margin> Nor should the female sex be cheated of its praise in this regard, since the famous Zenobia is commended in a letter by the Emperor Aurelian, even though he was her enemy. When Gallienus was administering the republic negligently, she acquired the empire for herself. They say that once she had conceived, she was accustomed to restrain herself until her menstrual cycles returned to see if she were pregnant; if not, she granted her husband the power to seek children again. Among the Seres <note>The Silk People/Ancient Chinese</note>, it is commonly held as a sin for a woman to be approached after conception. For the sake of this continence and modesty, for which oxen are commended as has been shown, Mycerinus, King of Egypt and son of Cleops, buried his daughter in a wooden cow covered with solid gold; she had hanged herself because she most indignantly bore the fact that she had been defiled by her father. Furthermore, in the most ancient rites of the Hebrews, to offer a calf is to have conquered the lack of control of the flesh, as Origen interprets in his commentary on Leviticus. Indeed, we sometimes interject examples of this kind because all this labor of ours is undertaken for the use of those who delight in painting, so that <margin>The use of Hieroglyphics.</margin> besides the Egyptian and many other mystical matters, they may also have history which they can adapt to whatever subject they have chosen.

## MODESTY.

<margin>C</margin> Nor for any other reason would I believe that the women of Elis, when pouring out prayers to Bacchus whom they worshipped, were accustomed to pray that he would come to them "with a bull's foot" <note>original: "βοΐω ποδι"</note>, except to display their own modesty in this way. For nothing less becomes a woman than headlong haste or the fire of audacity. Therefore, when Virgil’s Dido is introduced as modest, she speaks briefly and with a downcast look. The Greeks used to depict Dionysus in the form of a bull because they understood him to be the same as Osiris; concerning his honesty, justice, and supreme benefits to the human race, many things must be said in many places in this type of commentary. Furthermore, Dionysus was surnamed "bull-born" <note>original: "βουγενης"</note> among the Argives, as if they wished to say he was born of a cow or from the bovine race. Regarding what was said above—that the celestial Taurus inspires pious, honest, and lawful loves—I am not unaware that Julius Maternus threatens those who have Taurus in their horoscope with unchastity. I would have preferred him to call it "prolific" or "fruitful," lest he undeservedly brand heaven with that mark of shame. Indeed, for the sake of this fertility and the immediate faculty for offspring, they say it was established as a good omen that in founding towns, when pressing that first furrow, an uncastrated male bull and a cow should be yoked together; and when they sacrificed around the walls every fifth year, they slaughtered unblemished animals. Truly, Orpheus calls the moon a "one-horned calf" <note>original: "μονοκόρωτα μόσχον"</note>. And Eustathius, in his commentary on the first book of the *Iliad*, says the moon is called an ox, inasmuch as it is the bringer of generation.

<summary>This page explores the bull and ox as symbols of sexual restraint and modesty, citing historical figures like Zenobia and Pescennius Niger, and mythological associations with Bacchus and Osiris. It emphasizes that these "hieroglyphic" interpretations serve as a resource for artists and scholars to represent moral virtues.</summary>
<keywords>continence, modesty, Zenobia, Bacchus, Osiris, celestial Taurus, procreation, Egyptian rites, Origen, Dido, fertility symbols</keywords>

# Pierio Valeriano: The Bull

<margin>D</margin>
<margin>A bridle for PETULANCE.</margin>
<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a bull standing next to a leafy wild fig tree (caprificus). The bull has a branch or garland draped over its neck and shoulders.</image-desc>
<margin>The Bull overcome by Hercules.</margin>
Furthermore, those Egyptian guardians of sacred rites, when they wished to signify a man who, turning away from his former petulance <note>insolent or impatient behavior</note>, takes a more modest counsel for himself, they did not propose the simple form of a bull, but one with its head, neck, and shoulders wreathed with wild fig. For the wild fig <note>original: "caprifico"</note> possesses such power that, when draped around the neck of frolicking and even the most ferocious bulls, it so restrains their nature that it renders them almost immobile. Greece fashioned the mystical fable of the bull suppressed and tamed by Hercules in this manner, just as the overcoming of other monsters hieroglyphically signifies that vice has yielded to virtue. Nevertheless, I am not unaware that what is handed down concerning the Herculean bull is referred by many to the river Achelous <note>A river god who wrestled Hercules and took the form of a bull</note>; because that story is so well-worn in every school, I shall pass over it for now. <margin>E</margin> Origen Adamantius <note>An early Christian theologian (c. 185–253 AD)</note> says that the sacrifice of bulls, which was commanded to be done in the tabernacle of the Divine Power through the law of the Hebrews, signifies that among us the swelling of pride and insolence must be slaughtered. Regarding the offering of a calf as a burnt sacrifice, he writes most copiously in his second homily on Leviticus.

->THE AMOROUS GAZE.<-

Nor should this be omitted: that the amorous gaze, when performed while maintaining modesty, was accustomed to be referred to the eyes of the Bull by the most learned. Whence comes that phrase, *to look like a bull* <note>original Greek: "βλέπειν ταυρηδὸν" (blepein taurēdon)</note>, which you may notice is used with nearly the same meaning in the *Symposium* of both Plato and Xenophon, as well as in the *Phaedo* and elsewhere. However, some have pulled the meaning toward a sign of indignation and interpret it as "to look grimly." With this meaning, it was said by Aristophanes regarding Aeschylus: *He looked, at any rate, like a bull, casting his eyes downward* <note>original Greek: "ἔβλεψε γοῦν ταυρηδὸν ἐγκύψας κάτω"</note>. Thus, he looked in the manner of a bull with his lights <note>eyes</note> cast down to the ground. For Aeschylus is introduced by Aristophanes with this face when angry. Nor am I unaware that "grimness" <note>original: "toruitatem"</note> is said to be derived from the ferocity of bulls, as the authors of etymologies hand down, who interpret grimness as the bitterness of the bull. <margin>F</margin> Far different from this is *ox-eyed* <note>original Greek: "βοῶπις" (boōpis)</note>, by which indeed the beauty and grace of the eyes is signified. Yet in those authors I cited above, it is certainly understood as the amorous gaze, in which modesty holds the first place; and this is usually done with a sideways glance, and that is what is "grim" <note>original: "toruum," which can mean both piercing/grim and askance</note>.

->THE OBEDIENCE OF THE GOOD.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a man in robes leading a bull. A thin cord is tied around the bull's front right leg, and the man holds the end of the rope, symbolizing the animal's submission and obedience.</image-desc>
Furthermore, by another kind of picture, if they wished to indicate a man endowed with such modesty that you could easily turn him away from wrongdoings, they painted a bull bound by its right knee. For if you bind a bull by the right knee, you will find him tame and following his bonds. When a certain Greek fellow <note>original: "Græculus"</note> accomplished this at Rome during the reign of Clement VII, leading a most ferocious bull many times through the entire city—which he had tied by the knee with a very thin cord—he attained the name of a most skilled Magician among the unlearned. Moreover, the hieroglyphic of tamed ferocity among...

<summary>This page explores the bull as a symbol of restrained passion and modesty, using the wild fig tree as a natural sedative and the "bull-like gaze" as a complex metaphor for both love and anger. It also recounts a contemporary 16th-century anecdote of a bull tamed by a simple cord around its knee.</summary>
<keywords>bull symbolism, Hercules, wild fig, Origen, Aeschylus, amorous gaze, modesty, Clement VII, animal taming, hieroglyphics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of symbolic and natural philosophy, specifically focusing on the duality of right and left as they relate to gender and the cosmos.</meta>

# Book Three.

<margin>A</margin> I saw at the house of Giorgio Anselmo <note>A 15th-century Italian physician and scholar</note> a bull stamped upon a coin, with its tail raised, its head bowed, and its left front leg kneeling beneath an oak trunk from which a laurel hung; on the other side was a head armed with a lion’s skin. The inscription read: MILO OF CROTON <note>original: "MILOCROTON"; Milo was a famous Greek athlete known for his strength</note>.

->MALE OFFSPRING, FEMALE OFFSPRING.<-

Those same Egyptian priests, when they wished to describe male offspring mystically, depicted a bull dismounting toward the right side after mating; but if they wished to express female offspring, they painted the same bull turning away toward the left. For it has been discovered that if a bull, departing from conception, turns to the right, a male has been sired; if he departs to the left, it is a female fetus. To this, Parmenides <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher</note> asserts that the fetus is similar to the father when it is produced from the right side of the womb, but similar to the mother when from the left. Hippocrates <note>The "Father of Medicine"</note> records that if the left testicle is bound, a male is generated, but if the right is bound, a female; and so great is the observation of the right and left parts in recognizing males and females, that if the right breast of a pregnant woman should wither or suddenly become thin, they say that if she were to bear twins, the male would be aborted; but if the left breast withers, the female is lost. Furthermore, 
<margin>B</margin> the Interpreters of Dreams <note>original: "Onirocritæ"</note> understand these same things: when they prophesy that dreaming of losing teeth on the right side signifies the death of male kinsmen, but on the left side, the death of females. It is also established that male fetuses move more often in the right side of the mother's womb, and females in the left. Albertus <note>Likely Albertus Magnus, the medieval polymath</note> says he discovered a certain woman who was accustomed to bearing only females for the reason that she always lay on her left side after conception; but having been advised, she began to rest on her right, and thereafter bore only males. Nor should we conceal what pertains to the Egyptian commentary: that a male power resides in the North, which even Aristotle did not hide. For he teaches that if we wish more males to be created, we must choose dry days and the breath of the North Wind <note>original: "Septentrionis"</note> at the time of mating, and the flocks should be pastured facing that wind; but if we wish females to be generated, the blasts of the South Wind <note>original: "Austri"</note> must be sought and the pastures directed toward it, because a womanly power resides in that wind. Africanus <note>Referring to Cassius Dionysius or a similar agricultural writer</note> also touches on this matter in his *Georgics*: he says if you want a male, mating must be done while the North Wind blows; if a female, the South. But indeed, there is a great disagreement among authors concerning the left and right parts of the world. For together with the Egyptians, Empedocles understands the right parts of the world to be the summer solstice and the left to be the winter; concerning which Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> says: *Empedocles says that the right parts are those of the summer tropics, and the left are those of the winter.* <note>original Greek: "Ἐμπεδοκλῆς δὲ δεξιὰ εἶναι τὰ τῶν θερινῶν τροπικῶν, ἀριστερὰ δὲ τῶν χειμερινῶν."</note> Nor are there lacking those who fabulate that the first females were once generated by the earth toward the East and South, but males toward the North and West. However, Plato 
and Aristotle, dividing the globe by another reasoning, taught that the Eastern parts are the right, from which the beginning of motion comes, while the Western are the left, as the same Galen recites: *Pythagoras, Plato, and Aristotle said the right parts of the cosmos are the Eastern ones, from which is the beginning of motion, and the left are the Western.* <note>original Greek: "Πυθαγόρας καὶ Πλάτων καὶ Ἀριστοτέλης δεξιὰ τοῦ κόσμου ἔφασαν εἶναι τὰ ἑῷα, ἀφ’ ὧν ἀρχὴν εἶναι τῆς κινήσεως, ἀριστερὰ δὲ τὰ ἑσπέρια."</note>
On the contrary, Varro, in the sixth book of *The Latin Language*, says the sky is called a "temple": he establishes four parts of that sky—the left from the East, the right from the West, the front toward the South, and the back toward the North. Pliny subscribes to this, saying that the wandering stars <note>The planets</note> take a course contrary to the world, for the world's course is always headlong to the right, while theirs is to the left. Although Festus calls that front part of the sky, which is illuminated by the sun toward the South, the "right"—for so he interprets "front"—and the back part toward the North, the "left." Alcinous <note>A Middle Platonist philosopher</note> says it is noted in the monuments of the Greeks that the custom of sacrificing was long preserved so that they would run around the altars, beginning the course from the left toward the right, in a certain image of the Zodiac, whose motion is different from the world, turning toward the rising sun; but soon they would run from the right toward the left, by which motion it is manifest the heavens rotate. Contrary to the Egyptian position, Titus Livius, in the first book of *From the Founding of the City*, places the right toward the South—

# Pierius Valerianus: The Bull, or...
<meta>catchword: through the—</meta>

<margin>D</margin> He says that the parts of the day, by the institution of the Augurs <note>Ancient Roman priests who interpreted the will of the gods through signs like bird flight</note>, were established as being on the left toward the north. Supporting this view is what is read in Psalm 89 among the Hebrews: *The North and the right hand you have created* <note>original: "Aquilonem & dextrum tu creasti"</note>; which some interpret as the South, others as the sea. However, the Chaldeans <note>Babylonian scholars/astrologers</note> agree with the Hebrews, placing the South there. Indeed, the South is not ineptly accepted for the sea if the sphere is positioned upright with the poles inclined toward the horizon. For the Egyptians—whom Cleomedes, an author of no small name, followed—described the figure of the entire world differently, inasmuch as they established the Dawn as the head, the Northern regions as the right, and the Southern regions as the left. They abstained from maritime fish for various reasons (which we have reviewed in their proper place), but chiefly because they said the Nile rose from the left—the feminine and fertile part—and flowed into the right—the masculine and sterile part (for such were their fictions)—where it was corrupted by meeting the sea. For we have spoken of the sterility of the sea itself in its own place; and thus they established the right shore, that is, the maritime side, as a symbol of perdition, considering the geography of Egypt. Nor should it be concealed here that in the writings of Eucherius <note>Saint Eucherius of Lyon, a 5th-century bishop</note>, where he speaks of the North Wind <note>original: "Aquilone"</note> as representing the Demon, you will find him called "the one on the right," and thus he even claims the name of the right hand for himself. <margin>E</margin> For Jerome also relates that in the fourth camp of the Hebrews, the North is called the right; but he says it is a false name when understood as the Demon, the master of that wind, who, being sinister from every side, offers all sinister things to mortals to be desired. Yet Eucherius judges the cause for the name "right" to be different from this: namely, that he is present on the right of one wishing to act wrongly by complying, persuading, and more easily attracting them. For it is evident, he says, in the Sacred Scriptures that the devil carries the name of the North Wind, and by that expression, whatever opposes good endeavors is understood: for he extinguishes the heat of good works and makes everything grow cold. Because of this, both infidels and the impious, as well as stubborn and wicked men, are marked by that <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>Valerianus uses "hieroglyphic" to mean a symbolic representation or sacred sign</note>. Whence the saying: *From the North all evil shall be spread* <gloss>spread forth</gloss>. And because a violent blast is more effective at stirring up a fire it hits the colder it is, it is said: *From the North evils shall burn over the earth.* Our poets also touched upon the right and left parts of heaven. However, Maro <note>Virgil</note> and Naso <note>Ovid</note>, "not bound to swear by the words of any master," did not distinguish them. Lucan adhered to the Egyptian faction and declared his opinion in that passage:

> You Arabs have come into a world unknown to you,
> Wondering that the shadows of the groves do not go to the left.

<margin>F</margin> That is, they do not stretch toward the Southern parts. In this matter, the difference lies in where one establishes the "head" of the universe; since it is not our purpose to say more on this argument, let us return to the meanings of the things themselves.

->**READY HEARING.**<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration framed by a black border. At the top right of the image, the Latin text "AVDITVS PROMPTVS." (Ready Hearing) is printed. The scene depicts a stag (Cervus) reclining in the foreground next to a tree, and a bull (Taurus) standing in the background. The landscape features light hatchwork representing ground and foliage.</image-desc>

For in hearing, two of the quadruped kind are most powerful: the Stag and the Ox. Although, as was said regarding the Ass, the interpreter of Aristophanes prefers the Mouse and the Ass—let others see to that. But the Stag, although of the sharpest hearing, only excels in it as long as it keeps its ears erect; when it lets them down, it not only fails to excel in hearing but even suffers from deafness. In the Bull, the ears are always ready for hearing, and especially at that time when the female, driven by the itch of desire, lows, at which point no more than...

# Book Three.

<margin>A</margin> She is affected for three hours at most; in that brief space of time, unless a bull approaches, she gathers her nature <note>likely referring to the peak of estrus</note>, and the male is not sought by the female again until the twentieth day after, which however rarely happens, because the bull, no matter how far away he may be, runs to her immediately upon hearing her voice. For that reason, the Egyptian priests indicated both hearing and diligent obedience by a painted ear of a bull. In like manner, the heifer also shows herself submissive to the bull. Indeed, the Egyptians dedicated the Cow to Venus, whom they call "the Celestial One": and they displayed the forehead of their Isis marked with horns, surely on account of the obedience with which she herself also obeys the bull as soon as she hears his lowing inciting her to love. Although indeed individual living creatures have their own voices for mating, such as pigs, goats, and sheep, none is deeper than that of female cattle. Furthermore, in other kinds of animals, the males sound deeper than the females: in cattle it is the opposite, says Aristotle: for the female of the cattle has a deeper voice than the male.

## NEPTUNE.

<margin>B</margin> But since the lowing of the sea is not unlike that of a bull, they used to sacrifice bulls to Neptune himself, and called him the "Bellower" <note>original: μυκηταὶ (mykētai)</note> from that same lowing: because the same God was sometimes called "Bull-like" <note>original: ταύρεος (taureos)</note> by the priests. Those bulls which they wished to be sacred to Neptune, they ordered to be chosen as black as possible from everywhere, so that they might thus exhibit the color of the sea. For it is the custom everywhere to call the water of the sea dark, black, or obscure: whence they also claim the Eagle <note>Aquila</note> was named from the color of water <note>aqua</note>, being dusky, and we find the god himself everywhere described as blue-haired and clothed in a blue garment. To these features are attributed a very powerful chest and shoulders more fleshy in the manner of bulls. Hence, <margin>A coin of the Syracusans.</margin> on a very beautiful coin of the Syracusans, you may see a wide harbor, in the middle of which a bull lies down with that proportion of limbs, with dolphins playing around him. However, those games which were called the "Bull Games" <margin>Bull Games.</margin> among the Romans were by no means instituted for Neptune, but for the gods of the underworld to settle a pestilence which was raging against pregnant women during the reign of King Tarquinius Superbus: it was said to have arisen because bull meat had been sold to the people. Regarding Neptune, who is called "Bull-like" <note>taureos</note>, as in Hesiod's *Shield of Hercules*, some say it is because of the sound of the waves, which, as we were saying, low like cattle; others, because Neptune used to wear a bull-shaped helmet; or because the Boeotians called him so from the sacrifice of a bull which was instituted for him, concerning which Maro <note>Virgil</note> says:
> A bull to Neptune, a bull to you, beautiful Apollo.

## RIVERS.

<margin>C</margin> Furthermore, they everywhere depict the rivers themselves as horned or "bull-headed" <note>original: ταυροκράνας (taurokranas)</note> and "bull-faced"—to translate a splendid expression among the Greeks. Hence the Tiber is described by Maro <note>Virgil</note>:
> The horned river, ruler of the Hesperian waters,
> And the golden Tiber with his twin horns and bull-like face.

<margin>Eridanus</margin> As in Ovid's *Metamorphoses*: "born of the two-horned Granicus," not as the common copies have it, "related to the slender Granicus." Thus the Aufidus is "bull-formed" to Horace. For you may read in Pindar's *Pythian Odes* that the bull given by Perilaus as a gift to Phalaris was an image of the river Gela. And Timaeus asserts that although the bull was so dedicated, it was thrown into the sea by the people of Agrigentum, so that no memory of such a cruel tyrant might remain. Finally, we find that in many places rivers were accustomed to be fashioned in a bull-like form, as were the Eurotas among the Spartans, the Cephisus among the Argives, and the Asopus among the Sicyonians and Phliasians: whom the Athenians, however, figured with a human face, with horns only protruding from the forehead: either because of the lowing which, as was said, rivers seem to stir up like bulls, and they are fierce like bulls, as Fe...

<margin>D</margin>
### T H U N D E R

Regarding the bellowing of the bull, the mythological philosophers suggest that the cattle of Geryon <note>A three-bodied giant whose cattle were stolen by Hercules as his tenth labor.</note>, driven away by the force of Hercules, should be understood as a hieroglyphic sign for thunder. This is the opinion of those who interpret the philosophy of Hesiod, specifically because poets have frequently described heard thunder as "bellowing." They are said to be driven away by Hercules because they understood the Sun to be represented by Hercules. Therefore, when the heat of the Sun glides into the clouds and is compressed by their stiffness, as soon as it bursts forth from the broken clouds, it is accustomed to provoke those bellowing sounds.

### J O Y

This hieroglyphic is widely diffused, such that joy is signified by the sign of a calf. From this comes the word *Vitulari* <note>Latin: to celebrate or rejoice; etymologically linked by ancients to 'vitulus' (calf).</note>, which means to offer a sign of joy, and to leap or gesture with exultation. Among the ancients, you will find those who are rejoicing and full of gladness described as *vitulantes*. Thus, you may read among the Hebrew interpreters of dreams—and not without reason—that to imagine a frolicking and running bull in a dream is a sign of future joy. They add that it signifies approaching profit if they are seen grazing during such a rest. Because of this playfulness, the Psalmist cries out that he is "surrounded by many calves" and "obsessed by fat bulls." <note>A reference to Psalm 22:12.</note>

### A B U N D A N C E   A N D   S C A R C I T Y   O F   G R A I N

Since we have encountered both dreams and that significance of the ox by which all happy things are set before us, it would be not at all out of place to recall the history from our Divine Scriptures regarding how and in what manner the bovine herd signifies the abundance of the grain supply, and likewise its scarcity. For it is well enough known how that Joseph the dreamer, son of Jacob, was sold by his brothers out of envy and brought into Egypt, where he served a long servitude, and how he afterwards obtained the first place under the King, after he interpreted that nocturnal image which had presented itself to Pharaoh as he slept deeply. For he had imagined seven oxen, beautiful, cheerful, and fat in appearance, wandering from the bank of the Nile toward a marsh; these were overtaken and consumed by seven others emerging from that same marshy place, deformed by leanness. Soon after, he thought he saw seven ears of corn, conspicuous with much fruit and ready to be cut; these were seen to be seized by an equal number of ears marked by notable sterility from <margin>F</margin> nearby. (This vision appearing a second time—which in the Etruscan discipline regarding omens is sought after—added to the first, makes it attested, as we also said regarding Gideon’s fleece.) This young man, most prudent in affairs, promised from this that a certain extraordinary abundance of seven years was portended, which those oxen seen with the fruitful ears of corn best announced. Indeed, because the animal is especially suited for the cultivation of fields, it is proper to interpret the fertility of the earth through them. The cause of this joy would be the flooding of the Nile, from which those oxen were seen to emerge. This is signified more clearly through the ears of corn which were cheerful in appearance: namely, a most joyful harvest of seven years. The other things which appeared contrary to these demonstrated a famine of the same number of years, which would immediately follow that plenty. Truly, a marsh is an indication of sterility, and is called sterile everywhere by both poets and orators. When the King had approved an interpretation of this outcome, relying on Joseph’s counsel, he not only drove famine away from Egypt but also abundantly satisfied the neighboring peoples. And so the Egyptians say, mindful of this benefit, they wished to show gratitude to the man such that they decreed the statue of Serapis <note>A Graeco-Egyptian deity; Valeriano is linking the god's iconography to the biblical Joseph.</note> with a grain-measure (*modius*) on its head to be a monument to this matter. But we speak of Serapis elsewhere. It is a wonder, however...

# Book Three.

It has already been recorded how much honor antiquity attributed to those who, through industry, care, or private resources, provided for human sustenance. The author Livy <note>Titus Livius, Roman historian</note> relates that a gilded ox was erected by the people outside the Trigeminan Gate in honor of Minucius Augur, using funds collected from the public, because he had reduced the price of grain to a single *as* <note>a small copper coin</note> at the local markets. For Verrius Flaccus <note>a Roman grammarian</note> recorded that the Roman people lived on spelt for three hundred years from the founding of the City.

Among the Persians, there was a foreign ceremony where an ox, its forehead shaped into the likeness of a lion's face, was seized by the horns and led out from a cave for sacrifice; concerning which Papinius <note>the poet Statius</note> wrote:

->**FRUITFUL EARTH.**<-

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration titled "FRUITFUL EARTH" depicting a man in classical dress plowing a field with a pair of oxen. The man holds a whip and steers the plow through the soil.</image-desc>

> *Disdaining to follow the turban that twists its horns.*
<note>original: "Indignata sequi torquentem cornua mitram." from Statius, Thebaid 1.720</note>

Indeed, since the bull is very much an animal of the earth, condemned to the powers of the soil and the underworld, yet dedicated to the Sun by that nation, it seemed to demonstrate the power which the Sun exercises over the entire earth. Moreover, that it was drawn out from a cave indicated that its power lay hidden in the bowels of the earth; to call this power forth and provide for the fertility of crops, they instituted this kind of sacrifice. This is also discussed under the heading of the Lion. Nor is there any other reason for the fable of the ox <note>referring to Io</note> guarded by Argus, who was endowed with so many eyes, except to show that the earth itself is observed diligently at night by the heaven itself, marked by the lights of so many stars, until it is overcome by Mercury—that is, by the Sun—at whose rising beam the lights of the stars are seen to be extinguished everywhere. Furthermore, what else was that "speaking ox" seen to portend, 

<margin>The omen of the ox.</margin>
which, having shaken off its yoke, burst into the dining room where Vespasian was eating? After the servants were put to flight, as if suddenly exhausted, it fell at the very feet of those reclining at the table and lowered its neck. What did this mean, if not that the world, pressed by the bitterest yoke of Vitellius's domination, was imploring help from the Flavian family, which was now about to take control of affairs? Thus, by that omen, the ox represented the Earth.

## THE IMPLORING OF AID.

This imploring of aid made through the ox brings to memory another custom of the Scythians involving an ox hide. Among them, the imploring of help and assistance was usually done by sacrificing an ox; on its hide, the person seeking help from friends would sit with his hands thrown back behind his back. Those who promised help would step on the hide with their right foot, declaring how much they were able to provide. Lucian describes this custom at length in his dialogue on friendship titled *Toxaris*. And what is said regarding the sacrifice of the Homolottians—who, when sanctifying treaties, would cut an ox into the most fortified parts—still endures among those same Scythians who inhabit the shore between the Tanais <note>the Don River</note> and the Phasis, now called Circassians. Some wish them to be the Zicci. If these people have received some heavy injury and are about to implore the help of friends, they sacrifice an ox, and having pulled off the skin and spread it on the ground, they sit upon it. They divide the meat of the ox into very small pieces and offer a portion to each person who arrives. Those who accept it understand themselves to be so bound by that treaty that they are prepared to expose themselves and all their fortunes to avenge the injury of the friend who provided this feast of entrails.

## THE ABUNDANCE OF CROPS.

<margin>The ox on the coins of Vespasian and C. Marius.</margin>
Truly, the joined oxen found on a coin of Vespasian (now Emperor and in his fifth Consulship) and others similarly yoked on a coin of C. Marius, certainly signify plow-

# Pierio Valeriano’s Bull.

parts are provided for, and they declare the benefits of the grain supply: for among the <term>conjectors</term><note>interpreters of dreams or omens</note>, as was said above, to imagine oxen plowing foretells a most joyful harvest and an opulent happiness in affairs. Indeed, even the name for the ox <note>Latin: *bos*</note> is derived from "nourishing," for the Greek word *bo* <note>original: *βῶ*</note> means "I nourish": for by its labor in working the earth, it continually feeds us. Hence the Greeks call a successful agriculture *bophagon* <note>original: *βώφαγον*</note>, for whom *phagon* means agriculture, and it is also an epithet for the ox.

-> J U S T I C E. <-

**S**ince the very opulence of the harvest often falls under the term "justice," and for that reason the Nations called Ceres the "Law-giver": the Divine scriptures much more fittingly understand by the ox those who draw the yoke of justice, and direct their labor toward good and frugal works. Concerning these, Paul cites that passage from Moses: *You shall not muzzle the ox while it treads out the grain.* Which he soon explains thus: *Is it for oxen that God is concerned? Or does he say it altogether for our sakes? Because he who plows ought to plow in hope, and he who threshes in hope of perceiving the fruit.* And Hesychius of Jerusalem says in many places of the divine scriptures that the ox is put for the man who is intent on works of justice: and that is what it means to offer an ox in the sacred rites: just as to offer a sheep means to abstain from evil. Elsewhere, however, Aaron <meta>E</meta> is commanded to sacrifice a calf for himself and for his house as one who is just, but a goat for sin. But what Hesychius observes must be recalled to memory: in Leviticus, the High Priest—that is, the teachers—are prohibited from entering the Ark of the Testimony at just any time, lest they inquire too curiously into how the Word was made flesh: how He who was in the form of God was made in the form of God: how He emptied Himself and yet remained in the heavens. For faith in these things brings salvation, but inquiry brings danger. But now at last he is permitted to enter, when he has offered a ram as a burnt offering for sin, put on a linen tunic, hidden his private parts with linen breeches, been girded with a linen sash, and placed a linen turban on his head: that is, when he has been reconciled to God, thoroughly purified in mind and bright in body, chaste, just, and crowned with understanding by the grace and beauty of the divine gift—that is, with wisdom.

-> M O N E Y. <-

<margin>The image of an Ox on the coinage of the Athenians.</margin>
**T**he Athenians, moreover, in order to return thanks for such frugal labor, honored the image of the Ox on their coinage: this was the <term>didrachm</term><note>a silver coin worth two drachmae</note>, as Pollux says in his ninth book: whence the proverb, *An ox walks upon the tongue* <note>original: *βοῦς ἐπὶ γλώσσῃ βαίνει*</note>: said of those who, having accepted money from their adversaries, soon acted in bad faith and abandoned the cases they had undertaken, as Demosthenes is said to have done, who even turned it to his own glory that he had received more to keep silent than he who had pleaded for a long time. Hence in Homer and others you may find the price of things set by such a number of oxen, as that in the *Iliad* book 2, *worth nine oxen* <note>original: *ἐννεαβόοιοι*</note>, each of which was valued at a hundred gold coins. For the ancients held such honor for the ox, on account of the many utilities we obtain from it, that they would mint the image of an ox on one side of a gold coin and the face of the prince on the other. Now, as for the claim that money was once made of ox-hide, and that Dido’s trade pointed to this, I would not dare to assert it: although the commentary of Tiberius Donatus likes the idea, and some of the Greeks say that leather and shell money were in use before Numa: which Suetonius Tranquillus also committed to writing, and rumor holds that wooden and leather <term>asses</term><note>Roman copper coins</note> were given in a <term>congiarium</term><note>a gift to the people</note> by the same Numa, who afterwards marked them with mixed bronze. <margin>Leather coins.</margin> Among historians you may often find that Commanders of armies, through a lack of ready money, paid wages in leather, and soon, when money was brought in, the just price was restored to each man. There is the illustrious Michieli family in Venice, who carry golden coins engraved on their shields, which was given for that honor, as they say: because in the war which they waged against the Saracens...

# Book Three
<note>original: "Liber tertius"</note>

<margin>A</margin> When a campaign for Jerusalem had been undertaken by King Baldwin <note>Baldwin II, King of Jerusalem (reigned 1118–1131)</note>, and Domenico Michele, the Doge of Venice, was commander of the fleet, a shortage of supplies and pay occurred for the rowers and naval allies. He devised a plan to stamp small pieces of leather <note>original: "corium"</note> and distribute them as pay—some valued as silver coins and others as gold. He commanded by solemn proclamation that these be accepted for a fixed price by the camp followers, innkeepers, and merchants of every kind of ware, with heavy penalties ordained for those who refused. Thus the necessity of the army was met. When he returned home to fulfill the public trust, he summoned all who held that leather money and paid them an equal amount in silver or gold coins. But this leather coinage, which was also distributed by Numa <note>Numa Pompilius, the legendary second king of Rome</note>, served more as a token <note>term: "tessera" (a small tablet or voucher used as a substitute for currency)</note> than as actual money, for it was temporary. Nevertheless, Saint Jerome also acknowledges leather money. Regarding what we were saying about prices being pronounced in terms of oxen, we read in Homer <margin>Cicero: 2. Coro:</margin> of a "Hecatomb" being established, which some interpreters understand as being purchased for the price of nine oxen. In the laws of Draco <note>The first recorded legislator of Athens, known for his harsh "Draconian" laws</note>, a certain fine was imposed <margin>Arg: Coi:</margin> at the spectacles celebrated at Delos; the herald used to announce that so many oxen <margin>B</margin> were to be given. From this, some thought that the "ox" was a coin of the Delians rather than the Athenians. However, Plutarch records that the custom of striking the figure of an ox onto coins was instituted by Theseus, either because of the Marathonian Bull which he himself subdued, or because of <margin>Why Theseus engraved the figure of an ox on coinage.</margin> the general of Minos <note>referring to Taurus, a general of King Minos of Crete</note> whom he defeated in single combat, or so that by showing this honor to cattle he might entice the citizens toward agriculture. Indeed, as the same Plutarch says in the *Life of Publicola*, the price of an ox was reckoned at one hundred obols (though Pollux, as mentioned above, records it as a didrachm), while a sheep was ten. For before the use of coins existed, guilty parties were punished with cattle and beasts of burden. For this reason, the signs of an ox, a sheep, and even a pig are frequently engraved on coins.

### THE FIRST VOWEL

But to return to our subject, honor was also paid to the ox by the Phoenicians, who—as the story goes—were the inventors of letters. They called the ox by the name of the first vowel <note>The letter Aleph (A) originally represented the head of an ox in Phoenician script</note>, since it is an animal most necessary for human use. That which is necessary, as Hesiod says, ought to be neither second nor third, but immediately first. Truly, the first letter is A, which by its own nature is pronounced freely from the depths of the throat <note>original: "barathri"</note>, needing no other instrument like the rest—such as the tongue, teeth, or lips—and <margin>C</margin> it is recognized as the first articulate sound in infants. So too the Earth, of which the ox is a symbol, was the first of all to provide for human weakness, the first to supply nourishment, and the first to bestow the use and utility of all things upon those who ask of her.

### ITALY

Furthermore, the Romans attributed so much honor to the ox that they claim Italy itself was named after the "Itali," as oxen were called of old. This is despite the fiction of the Greeks and especially the fabrication of Eratosthenes, who said Italy was named after King Italus, who succeeded Morges. Yet Varro did not think it reckless to value oxen above other livestock in honor, especially in Italy, both because of their utility and because of the name. Nor did the Romans disdain—as Plutarch mentions—to admit the name "Bubulcus" <note>term: "Bubulcus" (Ox-driver or Plowman)</note> into their families, just as others were not ashamed to be called "Porcius" <note>from "porcus" (pig)</note> or "Caprarius" <note>from "capra" (goat)</note>.

There was once seen in the forum of the Argives a bull overcome by a wolf; this was a hieroglyphic <note>In this context, a symbolic visual omen</note> indicating the fight between Danaus and Gelanor for the kingdom. Indeed, when this portent of animals appeared while the assembly was being delayed, and the wolf was the victor, the votes of all turned toward Danaus, whom they regarded as...

<summary>This page discusses the historical use of leather "token" currency during the Crusades and in early Rome, the symbolic origins of the letter 'A' from the Phoenician word for ox, and the etymological connection between cattle and the naming of Italy.</summary>
