<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bull (Taurus) as a symbol in Pierio Valeriano’s "Hieroglyphica," specifically focusing on the bull as a representative of the citizen and the sanctity of labor.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano: The Bull

...they said the wolf was a foreigner, since Gelanor <note>The last mythical king of Argos</note> had lived among them and handled their civil affairs; therefore, the citizen is regarded as a bull, which, being accustomed to human society, does not shrink from it. The Argives <note>People of Argos, Greece</note> wished that figure to be a monument to this event, which we have explained more extensively in our commentary on the Wolf. Furthermore, it is recorded in memory that there was a most ancient law stating that he who killed an ox should be condemned to death no differently than if he had killed a citizen. Hence, among the Athenians during the reign of Erechtheus, when it was necessary to sacrifice an ox for a certain rite, it was instituted—so that the sacred rite might be performed correctly without violating the law—that the Priest (whom they called a *bouphonon* <note>original: βούφονον; literally "ox-slayer"</note>), as soon as he had struck the ox, should leave the axe at the altar, immediately take flight, change his soil <note>go into exile</note>, and flee entirely from Attica. So that this deed would not go unpunished, it was decreed that the axe itself be put on trial as the defendant; from this, that annual custom of judging grew over time. Among the Ancient Romans, you will also find it was a capital offense if anyone killed an ox.

->A CITIZEN OPPRESSED<-
->by a foreigner.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut of a bull's head (bucranium) in profile, severed at the neck. A thick cloth or blindfold is tied around the animal's forehead and eyes, with a large knot secured at the top of the head between the ears.</image-desc>

---

->WORK AND LABOR.<-

<margin>A passage of Horapollo emended.</margin>
Regarding work and labor, certain hieroglyphics are commonly circulated: the horns or the head of a bull are described as the work of a male; but those of a female signify labor and anxiety. However, in the printed editions of Horapollo <note>An ancient author on Egyptian hieroglyphs</note>, the word "bull" is missing from both entries. It should be written according to the ancient manuscript codices: *a male bull signifies toil* <note>original: βούς ἄρρην κόπος</note> and further down, *a female bull signifies hardship* <note>original: βούς δὲ θήλεια μόχθος</note>. The male and female are distinguished by the appearance of the horns: indeed, those of the males are both larger and more twisted, while those of the females are smaller and notable for a single curve, almost like the horns of a new Moon. But in this meaning, some have even tied hoes, mattocks, and—if it pleases God—rakes to the horns. There are those who, making no distinction between male and female, present a bull’s head stripped of its skin and cleaned of its flesh <note>a bucranium</note> to represent labor and endurance.

---

->FRUIT FROM LABORS.<-

If they wish to indicate the fruit received from labors, they make a head of this kind wreathed with flowers and garlands, and they hang clusters of berries and wreaths here and there from the horns. A band of this sort used to encircle another mass <note>referring to the Tomb of Hadrian</note>, as shown by certain fragments that have remained as an intact part of the work. Whether this is a sign of sacrifices, namely the *Taurobolium* <note>A ritual bull sacrifice</note> or something of this kind—since a sacrificial bowl is also usually sculpted nearby, which we see everywhere on the walls of temples between the triglyphs <note>Architectural slots in a Doric frieze</note>, and for that reason those crowned heads are very common—or whether we prefer to interpret them as the large rewards of labor. For example, in Rome, on that same Mass of Hadrian <note>The Castel Sant'Angelo</note>, the sacrificial bowl shows munificence, while the bull’s head shows labors or brave deeds. For after the course of a life nobly led and labors overcome, *apotheosis* <note>elevation to divine status</note> itself was decreed as a reward for Imperial families; or those mausoleums, pyramids, or columns were erected to demonstrate that those buried there were raised above mortals by glory. At the house of Pietro Melino in Rome, we saw a bronze bull’s head of very ancient and skillfully made workmanship, which was once used as a support for the edge of a beam, or a corbel, or a crown, made in such a gesture that it seems to have sustained something with a laborious effort: for the head is pressed against the throat, and with its curling dewlaps, it shows the appearance of an effort...

<summary>Valeriano discusses the bull as a symbol of the citizen and the sanctity of the animal in ancient law, while also interpreting the bull's head (bucranium) in art and hieroglyphics as a symbol of labor and its eventual rewards.</summary>
<keywords>bull, citizen, labor, Horapollo, bucranium, sacrifice, Hadrian's Tomb, Athens, Roman law, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal symbolism (hieroglyphics) in classical and sacred contexts, moving from general labor to specific cultural and religious interpretations of the ox and horse.</meta>

# Book Three. 28

<margin>D</margin>
<margin>A</margin>
<margin>The ancient custom of the Germans in leading a wife.</margin>
A [The ox] demonstrates what is undoubtedly to be referred to as a sign of endurance and labor. Truly memorable was the institution of the ancient Germans: to send as a dowry for a wife-to-be a yoke of oxen, a bridled horse, and a shield with a spear and sword. This was done so that the wife (as Tacitus <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus, Roman historian, in his work *Germania*</note> says) might be warned by these auspices of the beginning marriage that she comes as a partner in labors and dangers; she would suffer and dare the same in peace as in battles. For the yoked oxen announced labor, the prepared horse announced war, and the given arms announced the same. Among Greek authors, you may find the ox called *gaion* <note>original: γαΐον, likely derived from *gaea* or earth</note> because it is destined for the labors of tilling the earth. Among the Mathematicians <note>Astrologers</note>, those born when Taurus is rising are said to be hardworking and devoted to perpetual servitude, because the animal is born especially for the yoke. For this reason, when the Tyrians attempted to build Carthage, the work was interrupted when an ox's head was found, and the city walls were moved elsewhere; for that omen seemed to threaten nothing but labor and anxiety, until the head of a horse was soon found, which they received as a joyful and spirited sign of war. Virgil <note>The Roman poet in the *Aeneid*</note> concealed one of these, as he was writing a poem and not a history, yet he seems to have recognized the matter when he said the race would be "unconquerable in war" but "easy in sustenance through the ages," <note>original: *facilem victu*</note> since it had either accepted the yoke or was to be restrained by the bridle—though interpreters of no small name prefer to interpret "easy in sustenance" as pertaining to the gathering of food, an opinion held by Servius <note>A 4th-century grammarian and commentator on Virgil</note>.

## AN ABSURD THING.

<margin>B</margin>
And lest we depart too far from the ox and the horse, there is a hieroglyphic <note>In this context, a symbolic image or emblem</note> derived from one or the other of these: when we wish to show that something has been done absurdly or unskillfully, we depict an ox with a pack-saddle, or a horse joined to a plow. By this we mean to suggest a province <note>A task or duty</note> that is truly absurd and foreign to one's natural talent or strength. Horace <note>The Roman lyric poet</note> elegantly encompassed this in a single verse:
> The lazy ox longs for the saddle; the nag longs to plow.
Quintilian <note>A Roman rhetorician</note> also touches on this from the *Letters to Atticus*, where in the fifth book he says: "The pack-saddle on the ox is not our burden." For Cicero had said in the fifth book to Atticus: "Pack-saddles have been placed on the ox; surely that is not our burden, but let us bear it." But let us return to our own matters.

## THE JEWS.

<margin>C</margin>
In the Divine Scriptures, the Jews, as has often been said, are understood hieroglyphically through the ox. For Hesychius of Jerusalem <note>A 5th-century priest and exegete</note> takes the ox to mean one who simply observes the mandates of the Law, inasmuch as the ox is a sign of labor. Eucherius <note>Saint Eucherius of Lyon</note> extends the meaning to all who spend their lives in labors; and he interprets that Mosaic law, *You shall not muzzle the ox while it treads out the grain*, to mean that no one should be defrauded of their wages.

## THE APOSTLES.

He also understands the Apostles through oxen, who, having taken up the yoke of Christ, plowed the entire world with the plow of the Gospel. However, that phrase, *Like an ox led to the slaughter*, which some take to be said of senselessness, he understands entirely as referring to the Lord, who for our salvation took up that "folly," as it were. Before the doors of the Temple, twelve oxen supported a bronze basin for washing the hands of those entering; these oxen showed only their fronts, while their backs and hindquarters were hidden. Through the oxen fashioned in that hieroglyphic gesture, Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great</note> understands the Pastors of the Church, whose works we see open and set in public view, but what they do or feel in secret must be for God’s judgment, not ours; for he says this is the meaning of their hindquarters being hidden.

<sig>d iiii</sig>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Bull" (Taurus) from Pierio Valeriano’s *Hieroglyphica*, specifically focusing on the symbolic meanings of cows and oxen in sacred and secular contexts.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano: The Bull.

<margin>
<note>original: "Hiero. ad Eucher. ad Valer." likely referring to St. Jerome and St. Eucherius of Lyon</note>
</margin>

### THE VICIOUS MAN. <margin>D</margin>

**A**s for what pertains to the cow, through its hieroglyphic, the Divine Scriptures—as Eucherius likewise relates—indicate a man full of the vices belonging to the body. He cites a passage from the Psalms: *Among the cows of the people.* <note>Psalm 68:30. In older translations, "cows" or "heifers" represented the unruly or the luxurious.</note> But regarding those milch cows which the Philistines hitched to draw the Ark of the Lord without a driver, while their calves were kept shut up at home, he interprets it thus: they represent the faithful who are indeed tormented by human affections <note>i.e., their love for their offspring/earthly ties</note>, yet being bound to that path, they do not deviate from the right way because they carry the Ark of the Lord—that is, His commandments firmly seated in their minds. And the same author elsewhere, writing to Valerian: "Then we truly head toward Beth-shemesh <note>The city where the Ark was returned</note> when, walking the path of righteousness, we do not turn aside to the neighboring flanks of error, even if the love for our children calls us away."

### THE GOUTY MAN. <margin>E</margin>

**B**ut what of that sign by which they signified a man with gout by the foot of an ox? For the ox suffers especially in its hooves with joint pain; although it does not die from this, it sometimes loses its hooves, and the feet swell vehemently. Hence a myth was created by the Egyptians that the foot of the Celestial Bull extends beyond its own region into Ethiopia, whose people's feet and joints are known to be remarkably prone to swelling. And having crossed into Italy, you will find very few who do not suffer from this defect of the joints.

### REST FROM LABORS. <margin>F</margin>

**F**urthermore, an ox released and returned to the manger shows rest gained from labors. This most ancient Egyptian hieroglyphic was followed by the Greeks with a proverb, saying *an ox at the manger* <note>original Greek: βᾶς ἐπὶ φάτνῃ</note>, when they wished to signify a man discharged from service, presented with the wooden sword <note>the "rudis," a symbol of a gladiator's honorable retirement</note>, and restored to a quiet and more tranquil life. This is because it is the custom to stand oxen at the manger after their labors and supply them with fodder. However, there are some who twist the proverb to signify a softer life, contending that a disposition prone to lust rather than labor is understood by this. Indeed, Hebrew interpreters say it is a sign of cessation and idleness when someone seems to see sleeping bulls in a dream. In any case, those twelve elders who spontaneously took up the governance of the kingdom left by Sabaco, King of the Egyptians, wished to testify that such great care of the realm was more a burden than an honor. In that famous pyramid which they built as a common tomb for all colleagues, they distinguished the ceiling structure (which consisted of a single stone) by carving mangers upon it—signifying that there, after so many labors exhausted and so many cares cast aside and sent away, they would finally rest. The opposite of this is the *Ox under the yoke*, referring to those fatigued by perpetual labor. But as for us being considered "asserted into liberty" by Jesus, it must be understood thus: the yoke of the Law was imposed on the Jews as if on slaves, so that they might be drawn to obedience of the precepts as if by a chain, and thus learn and become accustomed to serving God. For the Law commands: *Thou shalt not commit adultery, thou shalt not kill.* But shall we, because we are free, abuse that license to commit adultery or commit murder without fear? By no means. Rather, we are commanded not even to lust, nor even to be angry. Therefore, the bonds of our yoke are tighter—but not truly so, if we weigh the matter rightly. For the Law commands imperiously that we do nothing wrongly; our Deliverer warns that the causes of vices must be cut away, so that we do not even let the thought of doing anything against right or divine law enter our minds. For this is true liberty: when we ourselves spontaneously cast off the yoke of vices, when we break the workshops of lust, and emerging from the whirlpools of evil thoughts, freed from all chains, we escape into the free fields of sincerity. We abstain from outrages not as slaves fearing the whip, but as free-born men, piously and holily educated, spontaneously upright and modest sons—and because we love our father...

<summary>This page explores the symbolic meanings of cattle, ranging from the "vicious" cow in scripture to the "ox at the manger" representing retirement and rest. It concludes with a theological reflection on the "yoke" of Christian liberty versus the Mosaic Law.</summary>
<keywords>hieroglyphics, symbolism, Ark of the Covenant, gout, retirement, Egyptian kings, Sabaco, Christian liberty, Mosaic Law, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the third book of the treatise, transitioning from a discussion on the moral law and the Church Fathers to the symbolic use of the bull in ancient mythology and superstition.</meta>

<header>Book Three.</header>

<margin>A</margin>With all zeal, meditation, and effort, we strive to obey his warnings in such a way that we do not wish to even think of any crime. For those who are good, unless they are good of their own free will, attain no reward from their goodness. For indeed Abraham and Enoch, Noah, and many others of the fathers, pleased God without the servitude of the Law. But the Law was given then, when through slavish defiance they began most stubbornly not only to refuse to listen to Moses, but even to the commands of God himself—who had claimed them for liberty from the oppression of the Egyptians while they saw and felt it. Irenaeus <note>Saint Irenaeus (c. 130–202 AD), a Greek bishop known for his writings against heresy.</note> disputes many things to this effect. But now we shall turn to the various gods whom ancient superstition worshiped under the image of a bull.

### BUCEPHALI.

<margin>B</margin>It must not be passed over, even if as a side-note <note>original: "κατὰ πάρεργον" (kata parergon) - as a secondary matter</note>, that the mark of a bull's head branded upon horses gave them their name among the Thessalians, namely that they were called *Bucephali* <note>Literally "Ox-head" in Greek.</note>. For so the People called those horses distinguished by that mark, of which kind was that most famous Bucephalus who was so dear to Alexander of Macedon. However, those who think that horse was so named from a bull-like head, or from horns with which that animal is said to have been endowed, are mistaken. For Aristophanes shows in *Anagyrus* that some horses among the Thessalians are called *Bucephali*. Indeed, how great the recommendation of Thessalian horses was is proven even by the Delphic oracle:

->The Thessalian steed is best, and the Laconian woman.<-

And so Strabo continues.

### JUPITER.

<margin>C</margin>Nor indeed did only the Greeks feign that Jupiter was changed into a bull, but even the Egyptians indicated that Jupiter of theirs—who is called Osiris, and Father, and Just, and Leader, and King, and Counselor in their sacred writings—through a bull, and they surnamed him Apis. They followed him with such great worship that huge volumes were written about him by many; and they progressed to such vanity that they proclaimed him born from an otherwise sterile cow. For they were persuaded that a cow, breathed upon by lightning, became pregnant, and from that conception Apis was born. Since there is no fable more chanted than this one, I will deliberately pass over it, if I only indicate the signs by which Apis was marked. He was black in his whole body, he displayed a square of whiteness on his forehead, an image of an eagle on his back, a beetle <note>original: "cantharum" - referring to the scarab mark</note> on his palate, and double hairs in his tail. He who is seen sculpted in many places also carries the appearance of a Moon on his side, as in a certain palace in Rome near the church of St. Marcellus. And this is that Apis whom the Hebrews, while Moses delayed on the mountain, fashioned into a God for themselves, following that custom which they had seen to exist in Egypt.

### DIANA.

You will also find that Diana, whom the Scythian race of Tauri worship, is called *Taurione*; for they think she helps the bulls. The same is also called *tauropolos* <note>bull-rider or bull-slayer</note>, as in Apollodorus; and from the shape of horns *tauropos* <note>bull-faced</note>; but *Tauro* among the Tauri. Why she is called *tauropolos*, there is varying opinion: for some say, "because all travel as if on a bull" <note>original: "ὅτι ὡς ταύρῳ πορεύεσθαι πάντας"</note>; others, because she drove the bull sent by Neptune against Hippolytus through the whole earth with a gadfly attached. Others say when Iphigenia had fled from Scythia into Africa and had dedicated a statue of the Goddess, Diana was called *tauropolos* because she had been brought there from the Tauric peoples. Others think the name was taken from that history which Nicander mentions: that a bull was substituted by Diana in place of Iphigenia when the Greeks were about to sacrifice her at Aulis; although Phanodemus says it was a bear, and many say a hind. Such was the variety and inconstancy in that superstition.

<summary>This page discusses the symbolic association of the bull with ancient figures and deities, including Alexander the Great's horse Bucephalus, the Egyptian god Apis (linked to Jupiter), and the various "bull-themed" epithets of the goddess Diana.</summary>
<keywords>Bucephalus, Alexander the Great, Jupiter, Osiris, Apis, Egyptian mythology, Diana, Iphigenia, Scythia, animal worship, religious symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bull (Taurus) as a symbol in classical mythology, Egyptian ritual, and dream interpretation, following previous entries on the bull's association with various deities.</meta>

<header>Pierio Valeriano’s The Bull.</header>

<margin>D</margin>
### P A L L A S.

Furthermore, Pallas <note>Athena</note> was called *tauropolos* <note>original: "ταυροπόλος" — bull-tender or bull-slayer</note>, and among the people of Andros she was known as the Bull-Tender. For when Anthropius gave a bull to Agamemnon and Menelaus, he commanded that wherever the bull should leap forth from the ship, they should erect a statue of Minerva <note>Athena</note> there. He claimed that in this way the deity could be won over and their voyage would be prosperous. It happened that the bull leapt out onto the island of Andros; the kings, mindful of the instruction, saw to it that a statue was erected, which afterwards gave that title to Minerva.

### B A C C H V S.

And Bacchus <note>Dionysus</note>, as mentioned above, is also addressed by the name of a bull. The ancients called him the *taurophagon* <note>original: "ταυροφάγον" — bull-eater</note> because, as Sophocles testifies, whoever won the Dithyramb <note>a choral hymn to Bacchus</note> received an ox as a prize, just as a goat was once owed to the tragic poets. Aristophanes, however, twists this name into an insult against Cratinus, as if accusing him of being a drunkard. One may read in Athenaeus that Bacchus is like both a bull and a leopard because of the violence of drunkards; for it is noted in its proper place that the leopard is fond of wine. Lycophron calls him a bull altogether. The poets sing of the Dragon as the father of the Bull, understanding Jupiter <note>Zeus</note> by the former and Bacchus by the latter. <margin>E</margin> But to return to Apis: he was received in the city of Memphis as an image of the Sun, and at Heliopolis a bull was sacred to the Sun, which they called Neriton. I judge it to be a mere fable, however, what they relate of the town of Hermithis: that there was a bull there which changed its spots every hour of the day; Macrobius writes of this. Truly, this has nothing to do with hieroglyphics.

### T H E  S T R E N G T H  O F  B I T H O N.

A bull seen at Argos being carried on human shoulders indicated the deed of Bithon. Relying on his own strength while the Argives were sacrificing to Jupiter, he is said to have hoisted the bull onto his shoulders. Among the ancients, the ultimate test of strength was to surpass bulls in power. Hence Milo is celebrated in the works of Theocritus, who seized a bull by the foot and dragged it to his mistress. Hence also the name of Busiris, because he alone could drag two oxen wherever he wished. He was a king of Egypt whom the poets noted for his most atrocious cruelty toward the human race. On the coins of Seleucus, one sees either a bull or, in other places, only the horns; this is because when Alexander was about to sacrifice, Seleucus caught a wild bull that had escaped its bonds and held it with both hands. In his statues, you may sometimes see horns added to his head.

<margin>F</margin>
### P I E T Y.

In ancient works of art, two youths placed under a yoke were seen drawing a chariot; these were the brothers Bithon and Cleobis. This hieroglyphic indicated their piety, as they drew their mother in the chariot to the temple of Juno <note>Hera</note>. Pausanias and others have recorded this history.

### S P E E D  I N  B V S I N E S S.

Two bulls seen fighting each other in a dream are said to foretell speed in business affairs, as certain Hebrew interpretations of dreams have handed down. Astronomers say that a person will be driven by furies if the eighteenth degree of Taurus shines upon them at birth; for that reason, the ancients wished that place to be marked by two bulls attacking each other with their horns.

### E L O Q V E N T  O F F S P R I N G.

The Hebrews say that to imagine a young, butting bull in a dream is a sign that one will father very eloquent offspring. This perhaps refers to that line of Virgil, regarding a bull to be raised for Pollio:
> *Now let him butt with his horn, and scatter the sand with his hooves.*

<summary>This page explores the symbolic associations of the bull with various deities like Pallas and Bacchus, as well as its representation of physical strength and filial piety through figures like Bithon and Cleobis. It also touches upon astrological and dream interpretations of the bull in Hebrew and Classical traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Pallas Athena, Bacchus, Bithon and Cleobis, strength, piety, dream interpretation, Seleucus, Egyptian mythology, Apis bull, Virgil</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of symbolic imagery and hieroglyphics, moving into military signals, Roman customs, and Christian iconography.</meta>

# Book Three.

<margin>as a secondary matter <note>original Greek: "κατ' πάρεργον"</note></margin> A. Because he creates new songs, which are indeed the offspring of genius; and I will add this (although I am not unaware of what the Grammarians dispute regarding new expressions) that it can be said Virgil looked back to that Pindaric saying found in the *Olympian Odes* for Epharmostus:
> Praise old wine, but the flowers of newer hymns. <note>original Greek: "αἴνει δὲ παλαιὸν μὲν οἶνον, ἄνθεα δ' ὕμνων νεωτέρων."</note>
For it is fitting to commend wine for its age, but poems for their novelty. Indeed, the interpreters say "flowers" is used to represent a fresh thing.

## HIDDEN COUNSEL.

Among the Romans, military signals were called either "vocal" or "mute." The vocal signals were those which the General gave to the tribunes, and the tribunes soon gave to the ranks, so that—whether at night, or during the performance of watches, or while conducting explorations, and sometimes even in the battle line itself when everything was confused by tumult—they might recognize one another in turn. The mute signals were carried forward fashioned into various figures of animals, but each had its own significance. To provide one example, the image of the **Minotaur** <note>The mythical half-man, half-bull kept in the Labyrinth.</note> was raised when they were being warned to conduct a matter secretly and silently. For that <margin>B</margin> **hieroglyphic** <note>The author uses "hieroglyphic" to mean a symbolic image conveying a hidden concept.</note> showed that the counsels of leaders ought to be no less hidden than was the seat of the Minotaur, namely the **Labyrinth**. Hence that wise General of theirs, Quintus Caecilius Metellus Macedonicus, when the tribunes asked where he was driving the army here and there, replied: "If I thought my own shirt were aware of my plans, I would tear it off and burn it immediately." For it is believed to be safest in expeditions (as **Vegetius** <note>A 4th-century Roman military writer.</note> says) for the things to be done to remain unknown.

## VICTORY.

The sacrifice of an ox, such as are seen everywhere, was a sign of great victory and a part of the triumph, especially among the Romans; hence **Juvenal** wrote with this meaning:
> Lead to the Capitol the great, chalk-whitened ox.
But it is worthy of observation that the Spartans followed a different reasoning from the Romans in this kind of sacrifice. For while the Romans were accustomed to sacrifice an ox when victory was gained through the slaughter of enemies, but to sacrifice a sheep when they gained control of affairs without battle and blood; the Spartans, by contrast, sacrificed a bull for a peaceful victory. If, however, they had conquered by fighting, they sacrificed a rooster; for they judged those things to be grander and more <margin>C</margin> worthy of man which were accomplished by reason and prudence than those finished only by force and bodily strength.

## LUKE THE EVANGELIST.

And since there are many things concerning the institutions of our piety that pertain to this teaching—which the assemblies of Christians have allowed to be expressed through images in their decrees—these must by no means be ignored. Such is the image which shows **Luke**, the writer of the Gospel, by means of a **winged ox**. This is either because of that very restraint and temperance of the bull (which was mentioned above), since Luke himself was most chaste from his early youth, as it is said, and lived his whole life uncorrupted, immaculate, without a wife, without children, persevering in celibacy, and never knew any practice of Venus <note>A metaphor for sexual intimacy.</note>; or because, through the mystical face of the calf, he preaches to us the sacrificed Christ, whom David predicted would be placed upon the altar; and he commended to posterity the gentleness which God-made-man displayed, illustrated by the testimony of his literary monuments; or because he takes the beginning of his work from sacrifices or from the priesthood, as **Eucherius** says, for he writes thus: *There was in the days of Herod a priest named Zacharias*; an interpretation which **Irenaeus** also admits.

<summary>This page explores the symbolic meanings of the ox and Minotaur, ranging from Roman military secrecy and sacrificial customs to the iconography of St. Luke the Evangelist.</summary>
<keywords>Minotaur, Labyrinth, military signals, Metellus Macedonicus, Vegetius, Victory, sacrifice, Spartans vs Romans, St. Luke, winged ox, celibacy, Pindar</keywords>

# Pierio Valeriano’s *The Bull*

->GENTLENESS<-

<margin>D</margin>
Regarding the gentleness of this animal, the poet Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BC), Roman poet and philosopher</note> provided the reasons for it as follows:

> *But the nature of oxen lives more in a placid air,*
> *Nor is the torch of anger ever applied to stir them too much,*
> *Spreading smoky shadows of blind darkness,*
> *Nor do they grow numb, pierced by the shafts of icy fear;*
> *They stand between both: the deer and the savage lions.*
> <note>original: "At natura boum placido magis aëre uiuit..." from De Rerum Natura, Book 3</note>

A little earlier, when he was speaking of the variety of the mind, he had contended that this gentleness exists in "airy" bodies. Because he had given the reasons why the lion is indignant and why the deer is fearful—not forgetting that human nature embraces every kind of disposition—he adds this:

<margin>E</margin>
> *So it is with the race of men: although education may make*
> *Some equally polished, it still leaves behind*
> *The first traces of the nature of each mind;*
> *Nor should one think that evils can be pulled out by the roots,*
> *Lest one man run more headlong into sharp angers,*
> *Another be tempted a little sooner by fear, while a*
> *Third accepts certain things more mildly than is right.*
> *In other matters, it is necessary that the various natures*
> *Of men and their following habits differ greatly.*

Indeed, according as a man is moved by various passions, he is compared to the nature of that brute animal which he is made to resemble; for in brutes, natures are for the most part simple, as we said at the beginning.

->FEROCITY TO BE AVOIDED<-

How different, however, is the fact that while they signify gentleness by a painted ox, they likewise indicated ferocity and raging insolence by a bull when they had tied a bundle of hay to its horns. This is now accepted among everyone, whence Horace said:

> *He has hay on his horn; flee far away.*
> <note>original: "Fænum habet in cornu, longè fuge." A Roman proverb meaning "he is a dangerous character."</note>

<margin>F</margin>
Our Psalmist <note>Referring to the author of the Biblical Psalms</note> uses the same witty saying when he says in Psalm 44: "When you were with us, we pushed our enemies with horns." For the Greek codex has *keratioumen* <note>original: κερατιοῦμεν — "we will gore/strike with horns"</note>, and the Theologians interpret this as a powerful and victorious fight. And in the first chapter of Ecclesiasticus you may read: "Care must be taken lest we lift up our souls like a bull." Regarding the hay, Plutarch says it was customary to do this so that we might be warned that ferocity and wantonness result from an excessive abundance of fodder. 

I have found that the saying gained fame from a certain Sicinius, who, while he attacked everyone with the most lavish sarcasm, was found to spare Marcus Crassus alone. When asked why he spared that one man, he replied that "he carries hay on his horn." Truly, it was a Roman custom to bind the horns of goring oxen with hay, so that those coming toward them might be warned to beware. By this figure, Hesiod named the "people-devourers" (if I may be allowed to make the Greek word *demophagous* <note>original: δημοφάγους</note> into a Latin one), who, wanton with riches, would attack and trample the rest. There is something similar in Sophocles, and a saying of Solon on this matter is by no means to be despised. 

But let us rather bring forward what is written in Psalm 22: "Many calves have surrounded me; fat bulls have besieged me." Here, the interpreters understand the "calves" to mean the common multitude of the Jews—namely, the wanton and loose youth lacking any rule—while by the "bulls"...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the next page likely continues the interpretation of "bulls" as the powerful leaders or elders.</meta>

<summary>This page explores the symbolic duality of the bull and ox, representing both Lucretian gentleness and Roman/Biblical ferocity. It explains the cultural origin of the "hay on the horn" warning as a metaphor for dangerous or wealthy men who trample others.</summary>

# Book Three
<note>original: "Liber tertius"</note>

<margin>A</margin>But the High Priests, Scribes, and Elders were called "fat ones" <note>original: "pingues"</note>—doubtless on account of their age and the prerogative by which they stood above the rest—because they enjoyed many of God's gifts before all others. For elsewhere, Moses, foreseeing the future long in advance in this same vein, says: *Jacob ate, and was filled, and the beloved kicked back; he was distended and covered with fatness and flesh, and he abandoned God his maker.* <note>A reference to Deuteronomy 32:15, describing Israel's rebellion after prosperity.</note> In the same way, theologians interpret the saying in Psalm 68 <note>Psalm 68:30</note> regarding the "assembly of bulls" as meaning the High Priests, Scribes, and the rest of the Jewish leaders, who were both reckless and "horn-pushers" <note>original: "cornupetæ," meaning animals that gore with horns</note>, destined to fiercely attack Christ and the Apostles. Truly, it must by no means be overlooked in this place that our theologians transfer Jacob's prophecy concerning Joseph—*His beauty is that of a bull*—to Christ. He is indeed a Bull on account of a twofold disposition: one wild as a judge, the other gentle as a defender who gave salvation to the human race, whose "horns" were the extremities of the Cross. For we even find it said of the yardarm of a ship:

> ->We turn the horns of the sail-clad yardarms toward us.<-
> <note>A quote from Virgil's Aeneid, Book 3, line 549.</note>

<margin>B</margin>By this power of the Cross, and being "horned" in this manner, Tertullian says, He even now winnows all nations, carrying them from earth to heaven through faith, and then He will winnow them through judgment, casting them down from heaven to earth. Where it also speaks of Simeon and Levi: *In their lust they hamstrung a bull* <note>Genesis 49:6</note>, they wish it to be understood likewise of Christ, whose "sinews" the priests took care to have pierced with nails.

---

### PIERIUS VALERIANUS TO PIETRO VETTORI, ON THOSE THINGS WHICH ARE SIGNIFIED BY THE HORSE ACCORDING TO THE SACRED WRITINGS OF THE EGYPTIANS.
<note>Pietro Vettori (Petrus Victorius) was a famous Florentine humanist and scholar.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'C' showing a landscape with figures.</image-desc>
<margin>C</margin>*YOU DESIRED, Pietro Vettori, as I have understood from several friends, that just as you heard I explained the hieroglyphics of the Lion, the Elephant, and many animals of that kind, I should also describe for you what the meanings of the Horse were among those same Egyptians. But you should know that those things which those Priests handed down concerning the horse are few; for they rejected that animal as profane, nor was it permitted for a Priest to be carried by a horse. But indeed the Greeks, Latins, and also the Hebrews have committed to memory many things most worthy of being known about it, which we shall explain in this Commentary. For besides the fact that I judged it most pleasant to comply with you, I was invited to this kind of scholarly labor by the dignity of the animal itself, its vigor, and a certain most remarkable excellence of its nature. For it is not of that kind which we ought to value little, since so many conveniences come to the human race from the horse, whether it is to be used for domestic or public purposes. But I begin to praise the horse here in vain, since so many and so illustrious things are found concerning it everywhere throughout the Commentary: what sort of things these may be, let us now begin to say from here, as we have established.*

<keywords>bull symbolism, Christology, Cross, Jacob's prophecy, Tertullian, Pietro Vettori, Pierio Valeriano, horse symbolism, Egyptian hieroglyphics, animal allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the entry for the Horse (Equus) in Pierio Valeriano’s "Hieroglyphica," continuing the encyclopedic exploration of animal symbolism in Egyptian and Classical thought.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano on the Horse.
<sig>D</sig>

<margin>WAR.</margin>
FIRST of all, the horse is a hieroglyphic of war, by the very clear testimony of Virgil <note>Publius Vergilius Maro, Roman poet</note>, who says:
> *Horses are armed for war, these herds threaten war.* <note>original: "Bello armantur equi, bellum haec armenta minantur."</note>

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut illustration of a horse standing in a field, turning its head back to look toward its hindquarters. The horse has a thick mane and a long, flowing tail.</image-desc>

And Lucretius calls the offspring of horses "warlike." We have learned from Plutarch that the Egyptians understood this same thing, which we also touched upon in the chapter on the Lion: namely, that after the death of Horus, he appeared to Osiris, whom he taught the use of many things. When Osiris asked which animal was more useful for war, Horus replied that the horse was most suitable for that business. And when Osiris thought the Lion was superior, Horus replied: the Lion could indeed be of help, but once it had taken to flight, it would depart irrevocably; whereas otherwise it was necessary for a warrior sometimes to yield, and sometimes to attack the enemy again from a line that had already wavered, and to accommodate oneself to the commands of Fortune according to the place and time. Therefore, the horse is altogether more dutiful in all these duties. Hence Xenophon, in the third book of the *Return of the Greeks* <note>The Anabasis</note>, says: In one thing the cavalry excel, because in flight they have a more certain refuge. Aristotle, in his *Ethics*, praises that virtue in a horse especially by which it is made fit for running and carrying a man, and is least fearful in attacking and awaiting the enemy. Galen, in *On the Parts*, says it is a very powerful and noble animal when he calls it <term>strong</term> <note>original Greek: ἰσχυρὸν</note> and <term>not at all spiritless</term> <note>original Greek: μή τι ἄθυμον</note>. Hence that line in Maro <note>Virgil</note>:
> *A noble colt in the fields.*

---

### PUGNACITY.

<margin>WASPS.</margin>
Nor did the Egyptians hand down without reason that wasps are signified <term>with tails held erect</term> <note>original Greek: ὀρθοπυγωνικῶς</note> from the carcass of a horse; indeed, they said it was not by chance that wasps are procreated from its corrupt carcass. For you may find this much in Horus <note>Horapollo</note> regarding the horse, and a verse well-known among all is cited:
> <term>From horses comes the birth of wasps; from bulls, bees.</term> <note>original Greek: ἵππων μὲν σφηκῶν γένεσις· ταύρων δὲ μελισσῶν.</note>
> *For gnats come from the horse, but bees from the origin of the bull.*

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting the carcass of a horse lying on the ground. A swarm of small winged insects, identified in the text as wasps, is shown rising from the body.</image-desc>
<sig>E</sig>

For diligent observers of natural things have handed down that bees are born from the brain of a bull's occiput; hornets from the brain of a donkey, just as wasps from a horse, and a serpent from a human carcass. However, "wasp" is not to be understood simply here, but rather what they were accustomed to signify repeatedly through the wasp: by whose hieroglyphic they showed pugnacity and a hostile disposition toward enemies, concerning which we have discussed in its proper place among the insects.

---

### SWIFTNESS.

IN addition to these, the horse is an indication of swiftness: and what is read in Lucretius as "the force of a strong horse," Marcellus explains as "swift"; and they wish that line of Virgil to look toward this:
> *What is so remarkable if a woman trusts in a strong horse?*
where Arruns accuses Camilla of being reliant on the speed of the horse, not being strenuous by her own valor. Indeed, you may find many things among historians concerning the speed of horses, but I will mention only this: that when the Alans were conquered by the Emperor Probus, a horse was found in the prey which, as the captives attested, could run a hundred thousand paces <note>approx. 92 miles</note> in a day, and would endure for eight or ten days with its vigor not at all broken. But indeed that...

# Book Four.
<sig>e ij</sig>

<margin>A</margin>
The commander himself despised skittishness in military horses, judging that a rejected, fleeing horse was more suited to a cowardly soldier than a brave one. You may find the name *Hippios* <note>original: "Ἵππιος" - meaning "of a horse"</note> applied to a Nymph in Hesiod, a name which interpreters claim was devised because of her speed: for they understand her to represent the force of waters, by which ships are carried along in a very swift course. And the horses that are depicted with Neptune are held to be indicators of that same swift course which occurs in maritime navigation. For ships provide the use of horses and chariots in abundance: whence we see both horses and a chariot attributed to Neptune; for he, in the works of Maro <note>Virgil</note>,

> Steers his horses and, flying in his chariot, gives rein to the favoring wind.

There is also a proverb used by Cicero regarding speed, involving both horses and sails, in his letter to his brother Quintus: *So, with you often urging me on in my course, I shall correct your slowness with both horses and sails.* Indeed, concerning every effort applied, he says in *On Duties*: *One must contend with these with both sails and horses.* <note>A metaphor for using all possible means.</note> Astronomers, at the eleventh degree of Virgo, place a horse to indicate the speed of a man who has begun his life with the light of that degree. Neptune’s horse was depicted with its front part in its own form, but the rear part in the likeness of a fish, <margin>B</margin> which folded its tail into a coil: this is shown openly both by ancient sculptures and many coins of Gallienus, which bear the inscription, TO NEPTUNE, THE CONSERVATOR OF THE EMPEROR. <note>original: "NEPTVNO CONS. AVG."</note> Furthermore, the fable is well known that in the council of the Gods regarding the naming of Athens, a wild beast leaped forth from a rock struck by Neptune. For an interpreter of Pindar touches upon a matter of this kind. Everywhere among the ancients, Neptune is called "The Equestrian." They say, moreover, that the first horse which Neptune produced was named Scyphius: so consistent was all of Greece in its trifles, that there is no fable so absurd that it lacks some testimony of assertion. And as for what was imagined by the Poets—that Saturn was turned into a horse, whom they then sing of as remarkable for his swiftness, as Maro says,

> Swift Saturn also filled high Pelion with his equine neighing as he fled.

—certain Astronomers refer this fiction to the speed of the star, since it traverses the vast spaces of heaven most swiftly in its daily motion, if we consider the interval of its distance and how great a circuit it is carried through above the remaining planets; although in its other motion, which they call its natural motion, it appears the slowest of all planets. From this kind of swiftness, it was said by the prophet Habakkuk, *Because <margin>C</margin> thou shalt ascend upon thy horses*, which theologians wish to be a prediction concerning the Apostles, from whom sudden salvation was to come to all the pious. And surely for the sake of speed alone, Virgilian Aeneas proposes the first prizes—a horse distinguished with trappings—for him who first reaches the goal in a footrace. And on coins, sometimes a running horse, sometimes Pegasus himself with wings added to his course, shows a matter carried out with the greatest alacrity.

---
->S O L.<-
<note>The Sun</note>
---

<margin>Otherwise</margin> At other times, a winged horse is also attributed to the Sun. For on the coins of Gallienus there is a winged horse with the inscription, TO THE SUN, THE CONSERVATOR OF THE EMPEROR. <note>original: "SOLI CONS. AVG."</note> But on a coin <margin>A coin of Gallienus.</margin> of Q. Titius in which Pegasus appears, as if the speed he had used in managing the affairs of the Republic were not sufficiently expressed by it, wings were added to his bearded head struck on the other side, with which appearance Mercury is seen distinguished in several ancient monuments. There are those who record that Pegasus was also on the coins of the Corinthians, whom Eubolus calls the "winged colt" <note>original: "πτερωτὸν πῶλον"</note> of Anchises. Furthermore, Pindar records that Bellerophon, carried by that Pegasus, received a golden bridle as a gift from Pallas <note>Athena</note> in a dream, from which the use of bridling horses later flowed down to posterity: and by that monument the Corinthians wished the memory of the event to be attested. Our Maro, however, records that bridles were the invention of the Lapiths in that passage of the *Georgics*,

# Pierio Valeriano’s *The Horse*.

<margin>D</margin>
> The Lapiths of Pelethronium gave us the bit and the ring,
> Mounted upon the horse's back; they taught the rider under arms
> To gallop over the earth and to gather his proud steps.
<note>original: "Frena Pelethronij Lapithæ..." This quote from Virgil's Georgics credits the Lapiths, a legendary Greek people, with inventing horsemanship.</note>

<margin>The slipperiness of HUMAN LIFE.</margin>
There are also those who take the Centaurs in this same sense: because they are fashioned with a human form down to the waist and the rest as a horse, they think it signifies that the boundary of our life is occupied by a swift course, because we are always snatched away by an incautious and wondrously slippery fall. For no one is so old that he does not hope to survive yet another year. But indeed, the Centaur will have its own place elsewhere.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular border depicting a centaur—a creature with the upper body of a man and the lower body of a horse. The human portion is depicted as a seated figure, as if on a chair integrated into the horse's back, holding a staff. The scene is set in an outdoor landscape.</image-desc>

I am truly not unaware that there are authors who contend that Pegasus—such as we see everywhere on the coins of Hadrian and Lucius Papirius Cursor <note>A Roman general famous for his speed, hence the nickname "Cursor" or "The Runner."</note>, and certain others—is an indication of fame rather than speed. He is born from the slaughter of Medusa: for indeed, when virtue has cut away terror, it generates fame. We showed that the head of Medusa is a <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>Valeriano uses "hieroglyphic" to mean a sacred or symbolic image that conveys a hidden moral truth.</note> of terror and wonder in the section on the Serpent, when we spoke of Domitian’s emblem. Moreover, as soon as Fame is born and begins to fly through the mouths of men, she awakens the fountain of the Muses on Parnassus, because the noble deeds of illustrious men provide poets with the subject matter for writing. Truly, whereas we had said that in the case of Papirius—honored by the very surname of "The Runner"—Pegasus signified the man's alacrity and haste, the hide of a fleeing goat, the thunderbolt, and the arrows seen on the same coin further support our understanding.

<margin>E</margin>
-> F A M E. <-
<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of the mythical winged horse Pegasus in flight, shown in profile with large wings extended. The image is enclosed in a rectangular frame.</image-desc>

---

-> T H E  F V G I T I V E. <-

But since I see there are those who think Perseus also rode upon Pegasus, we must examine the passage in Hesiod, in the *Shield of Hercules*, where the poet uses the phrase *the path of the horseman* <note>original Greek: "ἱππότα κέλευθος" (hippota keleuthos)</note>. In this place, the Commentators say it should be noted that *horseman* <note>original Greek: "ἱππότα" (hippota)</note> signifies a fugitive; and that is the interpretation of the <term>glossographers</term> <note>Scholars who wrote explanatory notes (glosses) on ancient texts.</note>. For his grandfather Acrisius expelled Perseus <margin>F</margin> from his home, and it is not at all the custom to depict him anywhere as a horseman, but rather as one who cleaves the liquid air, endowed with the winged sandals received from Mercury. Of which Ovid writes:
> *He binds his wings again upon both feet,*
> *Girds on his hooked sword,*
> *And cleaves the clear air with his moving winged sandals.*
<note>From Ovid's Metamorphoses, describing Perseus's flight.</note>
Hence he is also called "wing-footed" by Catullus. In the same way, you will find "Horseman Nestor" in Homer used for a fugitive, as the older grammarians interpret that passage regarding Boeotia:
> *To them spoke the Gerenian horseman Nestor.*
<note>original Greek: "Τοῖς δὲ καὶ μετέειπε γερήνιος ἱππότα Νέστωρ."</note>
By this, he means "Nestor the Gerenian exile." And they recount such a story: that Hercules brought war to Pylos because Neleus had refused to purify him from the murder of Iphitus. Then also because of the insolence of Neleus's sons, who, since there were twelve of them, relied on that number to promise themselves all manner of glory. The war was protracted for a long time, nor could the city ever be taken as long as Periclymenus, one of Neleus's own sons, lived. But after he changed into a bee and sat upon Hercules' chariot as a spy, he was killed by Hercules at the warning and betrayal of Pallas Athena. Likewise, Py-

He also provides a philological argument that the Greek term for "horseman" can sometimes signify a "fugitive" or "exile," using the myths of Perseus and Nestor as evidence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of historical and mythological figures, transitioning into a symbolic analysis of the horse in classical and sacred contexts.</meta>

# Book Four.
<sig>e iij</sig>

<margin>D</margin> <margin>A</margin> [Hercules] was [victorious], and the eleven brothers of Nestor were slain. But Nestor had previously been sent away to the Gerenians to be educated; as the sole survivor, he was restored to his fatherland from there, and was called "Gerenius" from the place of his upbringing, but "Hippota" <note>original: "Hippota," meaning horseman or knight</note> from the escape by which he was saved.

->FEROCITY BRIDLED.<-

It is a most common argument that a man of fierce and unconquerable spirit, who nonetheless submits to authority and reason, is signified hieroglyphically by a bridled horse. Hence Panaetius, in the works of Cicero, says that Africanus <note>Scipio Africanus the Younger</note> used to say: just as men are accustomed to hand over horses—who leap with ferocity due to the frequent struggles of battle—to trainers so that they may use them more easily; so too must men, who are unbridled by prosperity and over-confident in themselves, be led into the ring of reason and learning. It is surely a fierce and high-spirited animal, which nevertheless submits to laws and "did not reject the bit from its mouth," as Horace says of the horse that implored the help of man and allowed itself to be bridled. And Maro <note>Virgil</note> writes:

> But yet these same creatures were accustomed in time to come under the chariot,
> And to bear the harmonious bits upon the yoke.

<margin>B</margin> In this same vein is the well-known saying of Scipio. When the Romans had already crossed into Asia against Antiochus, and the latter finally sent legates to Scipio concerning peace, he replied: "That should have been done before, not after he had accepted both the bit and the rider." But one must also observe the omen of Carthage, which was presented to those digging the foundations of the walls: for as found in Maro <note>Virgil, Aeneid 1.444</note>, the head of a spirited horse was shown as a sign that the nation would be preeminent in war. As for what he adds, "Easy in sustenance" <note>original: "Facilem victu"</note>, this is not to be understood as a bull's head (as Servius thought), but of that very same horse; the interpretation of the omen is that we should explain it as a nation easy in sustenance through the ages, and one that would eventually be easily conquered. This approaches closer to Virgil’s meaning, although in our commentary on the Bull we attempted to illustrate Servius's understanding. 

Dream-interpreters, to return to that, suspect that those who dream they have the head of a horse, a dog, or a donkey, are to be delivered into slavery or similar hardships. For these three animals are wonderfully subject to servitude. I shall add also that interpretation of the same Dream-interpreters <note>Oneirocritics</note>, in which they say a horse seen in a dream carrying someone

<margin>C</margin> promises that a mistress will be easily subjected to the command of her lover, and will be compliant in the future. Regarding an unbroken filly representing a girl as yet untouched, the poems of Anacreon will be recited a little further down in their proper place. 

In Holy Scripture, where Elisha beheld the mountain full of auxiliary horsemen through the opened eyes of the boy, and those whom the prophet Habakkuk <note>original: "Abachus"</note> calls the horses and cavalry of the Lord, Adamantius <note>Origen</note> understands these to be souls who receive the bridle of holier discipline and bear the yoke of sweetness which proceeds from the Lord himself, allowing themselves to be ruled by His spirit. He calls those souls blessed which have curved their backs to receive the Word of God as a rider upon their spine; whose bits they endure, and whichever way He urges, they take up the journey. Finally, moderated by His reins, they are turned, begin their course, and stand still, not by their own lust, but by the will of so great a rider. 

Eucherius certainly says it is found in Holy Scripture that the horse stands for a man endowed with holiness; and that the saying of Habakkuk elsewhere looks to this: "Who shall ascend upon your horses." Indeed, the Greeks hand down that the name of Pallas <note>Athena</note> was given from a tamed horse; and, as we said above, you may read in Pindar that the bridle was invented by her at Corinth and given as a gift to Pelops. And in the capture of Ilium <note>Troy</note>, the Greeks dedicated a horse as large as a mountain to Pallas. This signifies that all ferocity is softened and tamed by learning, which Horace expressed so clearly:

<keywords>Nestor, bridled horse, ferocity, Scipio Africanus, Virgil, Carthage omen, dream interpretation, Elisha, Habakkuk, Origen, Pallas Athena, discipline</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the horse as a symbolic figure in classical antiquity, specifically focusing on its connection to imperial power and omens.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano’s *The Horse*

<margin>D</margin>
> *No one is so savage that he cannot be tamed,*
> *If only he lends a patient ear to cultivation.*
<note>original: "Nemo adeò ferus est..." from Horace, Epistles 1.1.39. It suggests that even the wildest nature can be refined through education or discipline.</note>

->S O V E R E I G N T Y<-

In the discipline of augury <note>The ancient Roman practice of interpreting omens from the behavior of animals</note>, we find that horses have often been a sign of imperial power. For instance, a horse was born to Julius Caesar that had feet almost like a human’s, with hooves divided in the manner of fingers. The Soothsayers <term>Aruspices: diviners who interpreted omens from entrails or unusual births</term> responded that the empire of the whole world was foretold for the man who possessed such a horse. Consequently, Caesar raised it with great care, and as it would not suffer any other rider, he was the first to mount it. He even dedicated a statue in its likeness before the Temple of Venus the Mother <note>Venus Genetrix, the goddess from whom Caesar's family claimed descent</note>. Nevertheless, he held other omens in such contempt that he was never deterred or delayed from any undertaking, no matter how adverse the signs were. Yet, the same species of animal that had predicted his sovereignty also forewarned him of his death. Indeed, the herds of horses which he had consecrated when crossing the Rubicon <note>The river Caesar crossed to begin the civil war</note> and had left to roam without a keeper, were found shortly before he was killed to be stubbornly abstaining from food and weeping abundantly. Furthermore, many horses feel such affection for their <margin>E</margin> masters that they mourn them when they are slain, as Pliny says, and sometimes shed tears out of longing. Hence Virgil wrote quite skillfully regarding Pallas:

> *Then the war-horse Aethon, his trappings laid aside,*
> *Goes weeping, and wets his face with heavy drops.*
<note>original: "Post bellator equus..." from the Aeneid, Book 11. Aethon is the horse of the fallen hero Pallas.</note>

In imitation of this, and perhaps with no less emotion, Silius Italicus sings of another horse’s love for its master:

> *The steed recognized him, and with ears pricked up,*
> *Letting out a sharp neigh, he struck Vagesus to the ground,*
> *Who was then carrying him into battle on a captive back:*
> *From there, gathering his rapid course through the slippery ground*
> *Thick with standing blood and corpses mutilated by slaughter,*
> *He flies forth, and stands before the face of his fallen master:*
> *Then, with neck bowed and shoulders lowered,*
> *He offered his back to be mounted, with legs bent*
> *In the customary way, trembling with a certain love of his own.*
<note>original: "Agnouit sonipes..." from Silius Italicus' Punica, describing the loyalty of a horse to its master in the Punic Wars.</note>

But to return to portents, it is recorded in memory how Ne- <margin>F</margin> ro, in a dream, seemed to see the stallion he most delighted in turn into the likeness of an ape in its hindquarters, with only its head remaining intact and letting out melodious neighs. This omen signified that the horse-like ferocity of the Emperor would turn into the mockery of an ape. A horse also gave a sign of seizing the throne to Aelius Pertinax, a fierce man who took power after the removal of Commodus. For at the hour he was born, a foal climbed onto the roof tiles, and having stayed there for a short time, it simultaneously fell and died. This was an augury of future loftiness—though it would be short-lived—for this man born in the Villa of Mars and destined for the business of war.

->R E S T  F R O M  L A B O R S<-

<margin>Horses on the coins of Nerva.</margin> As for what the loose, grazing horses on the coins of Nerva signify, the letters struck there declare it in this manner: THE POSTAL TAX OF ITALY REMITTED <note>original: "VEHICVLATIONE ITAL. REMISSA"</note>. Some refer this to the same theme we discussed regarding the Ox at the manger. However, it should rather be understood as the tribute that all of Italy used to pay for the postal service <term>vehiculatio: the public system of transport for officials and mail</term>, so that the baggage of Magistrates, armies, and other such public goods might be transported. Nerva remitted this type of tribute to the whole of Italy as being too burdensome. I would say more on this opinion, were it not that the most learned...

# Book Four.
<sig>e iiij</sig>

<margin>A</margin> ...as the most learned man Fabio Vigile of Spoleto <note>original: "Fab. Vigil Spoletanus"; a 16th-century Italian humanist and scholar</note> had treated the matter with the utmost exactness.

->I T A L Y.<-

There are those who think that horses on this coin signify Italy, and they are led to this argument because on a silver coin and several bronze ones, the head of a bridled horse is observed with the inscription ROMA. From its back-head <note>occiput</note> emerges a scepter, on the top of which a horse's head likewise appears; on the other side is Roma herself, helmeted, as is the custom. There are other coins with single horse heads on each side, with the inscription ROMA. However, since I have observed the horse itself in many ways on Latin and Greek coins, I would not contend that it signifies Italy nor any other province, region, or city, but rather either war, or travel, or a military maneuver <note>decursio: a rhythmic military exercise or parade</note>, as in many coins of Nero; or speed, as in those of the Papirian family; and others as they are explained in their proper places.

->M A U R E T A N I A.<-

<margin>B</margin> Unless some (if they see on a coin of Hadrian a horse led by a footman with the inscription MAURETANIA, for it is written thus with an 'E' in the second syllable) wish to understand that province as having been received into surrender, or something of this sort. Indeed, several of these types of coins have the head of Hadrian on one side, and on the other a horse, which a woman armed with two javelins leads: which surely indicates that kind of fighting which that province uses most.

->T H E  F I E R C E  M O O R S.<-

Indeed, even in later centuries, certain military orders who served under the Master of the Horse <note>Magister Equitum</note> displayed on a white shield two half-horses of a reddish color, as if provoking one another, with a green circle placed between them, the boss of which was yellow; and these were called the FIERCE MOORS <note>Mauri Feroces</note>. But as for the fact that some have believed Italy to be signified by a horse on coins, they perhaps suspected it from that coin which has an exultant horse with a certain flower placed above it; on the other side of which is the inscription ROMANOR <note>of the Romans</note>.

->H O R S E  V I C T O R Y.<-

<margin>C</margin> Why should we not rather think this coin was struck as a monument to a great achievement? Because in the Punic War, which was waged against Hannibal, the Romans finally began to be superior even in horses <note>cavalry</note>, whereas previously all their strength consisted in the legions. For that flower added above indicates the beginning and the hope of good fruit. Truly Virgil by no means left such a great matter unmentioned, when he sang thus of the praises of Marcellus:

> He shall stay the Roman state when a great tumult shakes it,
> And shall lay low the Carthaginians with his horses.
> <note>Virgil, Aeneid VI.857-858. Original: "Hic rem Romanam magno trepidante tumultu / Sistet, equis sternet Poenos."</note>

Furthermore, on the coins of the Cephallenians, a horse was also customarily marked, a matter mentioned in Plutarch. Among the people of Smyrna, there was a statue of Fortune made by a certain Bupalus, a truly upright man, on whose head a foal sat, and an abundance-horn <note>cornucopia</note> filled her hand, concerning which one may read in Pausanias’s *Messenica*.

->T H E  S Y R A C U S A N S.<-

The Syracusans also used the mark of a horse. For those Athenians who were captured at Syracuse in the Nician disaster <note>The failed Sicilian Expedition of 415–413 BC</note> and sold as slaves under the spear, the brand of a horse was burned into their foreheads. On several of their coins, a horse and rider are held with the inscription HIERONOS <note>Greek: ΙΕΡΩΝΟΣ, "Of Hieron"</note>, which surely shows his glory in the cavalry, but especially in the games; for which reason the poet Pindar consecrated him to immortality with so many praises. Other Republics also, and many other Prin—

# Pierio Valeriano: The Horse.

<margin>D</margin>
They used the sign of a horse's foot, so that I see nothing private, finally, that pertains to Italy. <note>Valeriano is discussing the widespread use of equine symbols in Italian civic and military identity.</note>

### THE TARENTINES.

Furthermore, on a coin whose inscription is TARAS <note>original: ΤΑΡΑΣ; Taras was the mythical founder of Tarentum (modern Taranto)</note>, a horseman is seen throwing a javelin, clearly on account of the cavalry service which the Tarentines were accustomed to practice so excellently. Hence, among the Greeks, you may find "Tarentine ranks" composed of horsemen of this kind; and the verb *tarentinizein* <note>original: ταραντινίζειν</note> meant to exercise oneself in that specific style of warfare.

<margin>E</margin>
### THE SENIOR EASTERN ARCHERS.

Two horses of a halved yellow color, on a blue shield surrounded by a red circle, leaping away from the center (which was a yellow boss placed in the middle of a red small shield <term>pelta: a crescent-shaped light shield</term>), such that a path of the same color extended from the pelta itself to the red border—this was the insignia of the rank called the **SENIOR EASTERN ARCHERS**. They served under the Master of Soldiers known as "In the Presence" <note>Magister Militum Praesentialis: a high-ranking commander attending the Emperor</note>.

### THE SECOND THEODOSIANS.

There was also a yellow horse at the bottom of a shield of rusty coloration, whose white boss was contained by a red circle, which another yellow circle surrounded. This was the shield of the **SECOND THEODOSIANS**, who earned their pay under the same Master of Soldiers. These men were surely recruited into service during the reign of Theodosius.

### CONSTANTIA.

There was also another legion which bore a reddish horse on a blue shield: it was positioned in the middle of a green orb, upon which the four-footed beast seemed to lean with its whole chest. This legion was called **CONSTANTIA**, which served under the Master of Infantry, having been established by the Emperor Constantius.

<margin>F</margin>
<margin>Deuteronomy ch. 17.</margin>
### IMMODERATE IMPULSE.

The subject itself seems to exhort me to reveal the mystery of Sacred History as far as it pertains to the horse. For it is manifest from the lawgiver Moses that the horse is placed to represent an immoderate impulse devoid of reason; he says a prince should not be designated as a breeder of horses. According to the opinion of Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who allegorized scripture</note>, this is to be understood such that we should consider a man unfit for leadership if, devoted to pleasures, lusts, and wickedness, he knows not how to stand still, and "fiercely champs at the foaming bits" <note>A poetic description of a restless horse</note>. The law itself is in Deuteronomy, chapter 17: *Whoever is appointed king shall not multiply horses for himself, nor lead the people back into Egypt, bolstered by a great number of cavalry, especially since the Lord has commanded you that you shall by no means return again by that same way.* For he who conceives such things in his mind is like that unbridled and fierce horse, concerning which there is a common proverb: *Keep far away from the heels of a horse*—from that horse, I say, which always seeks to gallop toward Egypt, that is, the plain of pleasures and allurements. Wherefore it must be restrained with bits and chains, unless we wish to be swept away into ruin and cast ourselves headlong along with our fortunes. For as Euripides best says: *The end of unbridled mouths and lawless folly is misfortune* <note>original: ἀχαλίνων στομάτων ἀνόμου τ' ἀφροσύνας τέλος δυστυχία</note>. Moreover, in Divine Scripture, the horse often indicates a wandering and straying man who is carried along without any reason, as in: *The horse is a vain hope for safety*, and, *Be not like the horse and the mule, which have no understanding.* Truly clever was the retort of Antisthenes when Plato was vehemently praising a horse in a certain equestrian procession—a horse displaying high spirits with frequent neighing and ferocity—Antisthenes, who gladly attacked the vanity of men, said: "And you, it seems, would turn out to be a good horse." But as for Themistocles, when many wondered at his changed character from being most provocative to most temperate...

# Book Four.

<margin>A</margin> "Do you not see," he said, "that the roughest and most unbroken colts often turn out to be the best horses?" Therefore, Prudentius <note>A Roman Christian poet (c. 348–413 AD) known for the *Psychomachia*, an allegory of the soul's struggle.</note>, not unwisely describing the pride of the soul, says:

> *By chance, amid the scattered crowds, puffed-up Pride*
> *Was flying on an unbridled horse.* <note>original: "Forte per effusas inflata superbia turbas / Infræni volitabat equo."</note>

->WICKEDNESS TURNED INTO TEMPERANCE.<-

Regarding what was said about the luxuries of Egypt, Saint Ambrose <note>Bishop of Milan and influential theologian (c. 339–397 AD).</note>, following the teachings of the Apostle, says that Passover is celebrated at the time when the Israelites left Egypt; he interprets this solemnity as the "passing over" of souls: from vice to virtue, from the passions of the body to the constancy and sobriety of the mind, and from the leaven of malice and wickedness to the truth and stable firmness of a sincere purpose. Indeed, Hadrian <note>Roman Emperor (reigned 117–138 AD).</note> criticized the softness and many other faults of the Egyptians, and he seemed especially to wish that the city of Alexandria were better behaved. It is read in the Sibylline books: **ROMAN SOLDIER, BEWARE OF EGYPT.** Although many have interpreted this as being said about Pompey the Great <note>A leading Roman general whose murder in Egypt marked a turning point in the Roman Civil War.</note>, and the Romans were at one time anxious—deterred by this religious warning—lest they restore Ptolemy to his kingdom by force of arms, there seems to be another interpretation.

<margin>B</margin> This second interpretation is far truer: that they were commanded under this veil of mystery that those to whom it was given to rule nations by empire should "beware of Egypt"—meaning its luxuries and softness. These were the very things that, as soon as that bravest race of men began to grow wanton, so shamefully effeminated them that they went utterly to ruin. The empire was then transferred to those whom they used to spit upon as the most despicable of all and pursue with every kind of insult. But as much as Egypt was an ill omen for the Roman people, the oracles promised the Egyptians a much more prosperous outcome from the arrival of the Romans in that region. For at Memphis, it was written in Egyptian letters on a golden column that Egypt would finally be free when the Roman *fasces* <term>fasces: bundles of rods symbolizing judicial authority</term> and their bordered robes arrived. Pollio <note>Likely referring to the Roman historian or a commentator citing him.</note> testifies that he read this in the works of Proclus the grammarian; surely, he who admits virtue and the discipline of affairs is freed from vices. Yet, once peace was gained on land and sea, the Romans took up the soft luxuries of the Egyptians; the Egyptians, having set aside their luxuries, soon claimed for themselves the ancient Roman virtue and industry. But the *fasces* must be discussed in another place. As it pertains to the matter, Adamantius <note>A name often used for the scholar Origen.</note>, regarding the saying about Joseph,

<margin>C</margin> "And he obtained the sovereignty of all Egypt," philosophizes in this way: He surely trampled upon lust, avoided wickedness, and subdued and bridled all the pleasures of the body. For this is what it means to hold the sovereignty of all Egypt. See also Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom, an early Church Father known for his eloquence.</note> in the oration he gave on the vigil of the Epiphany, discussing many things about Egypt in this vein.

->REASON AND DESIRE.<-

There is something similar in Plato's *Phaedrus*, who proposes that Reason and Desire are to be understood through the right and left horses of a chariot; he commands that the mouths of the wicked and impure horse be broken with jagged bits even until blood is drawn:

> *Until the proud steed stamps his reckless legs*
> *Upon the ground, and the earth trembles, struck by his haunches,*
> *Then give the reins with a gentle hand, and let each*
> *Galloway <note>A small, sturdy horse.</note> be carried along equally, obeying your command.*

->PRIDE. STUBBORNNESS.<-
->STUMBLING. INSOLENCE.<-

For Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who used allegory to fuse Greek and Hebrew thought.</note> also detects four vices in that Mosaic quadruped <note>Referring to the "horse and rider" cast into the sea in Exodus 15.</note>, which must be cast into the sea and paid for along with the rider. First, the Elated...

# Pierio Valeriano’s *The Horse*

<margin>D</margin>
...that foot which they called pride; it is accompanied by the other foot, which drags along with it a swift inclination toward the ruin of one's life, into which it stumbles quite frequently, and like a pack-horse <note>original: "canterij"</note> sliding into a ditch, it falls headlong. From the hindquarters, one foot shows a subsequent stubbornness of evil perseverance, while the other shows an exultation in wrongly committed deeds: all of which describe the unbridled and exulting horse of Plato <note>A reference to the Chariot Allegory in Plato's *Phaedrus*, where the dark horse represents the irrational passions.</note> with wonderful similarity. Indeed, the Teian Anacreon <note>A Greek lyric poet known for his songs on love and wine.</note> jokingly, when attacking feminine pride and an arrogant spirit, compared a certain girl to a horse's foal upon which no one had yet mounted, and which thus wandered aimlessly, leaping about, restrained by no bridle. The following verses of this sentiment have survived:

> Thracian foal, why indeed do you
> look at me askance with your eyes
> and flee pitilessly? You seem to think
> I know nothing of skill; know
> that I could indeed throw the bit
> upon you, and holding the reins,
> turn you around the limits of the racecourse.
> But now you graze in the meadows,
> and play, leaping lightly.
> For you do not have a skilled
> rider to mount you.
<note>original Greek: "Πῶλε Θρῃκίη..."</note>

These verses I have rendered into Latin as best I could, though with hardly equal elegance:

<margin>E</margin>
> Thracian foal, why do you
> flee me so impiously, seeking me
> with grim eyes? You seem
> to know nothing: but you will be
> beautifully skilled if I bind
> the jagged bits <note>original: "lupatos," a type of sharp bit shaped like wolf teeth.</note> on you, and soon
> take up the reins, and turn you
> around the circuit and the finish line.
> But you graze in pleasant meadows,
> for nowhere is there a master
> rider for your playfulness,
> who might properly instruct you
> how to be mounted.

To this effect is that most charming Ode of Horace concerning the untamed heifer:

> Not yet is she strong enough to bear the yoke
> with bowed neck, not yet to match the duties
> of a partner, nor to endure the weight
> of a bull rushing into love:
> the mind of your heifer is set upon
> green fields, now easing the heavy heat
> in the rivers, now eager to play
> with the calves in the damp willow-thicket.
<margin>F</margin>
<note>Horace, *Odes* 2.5. Valeriano uses the heifer and horse interchangeably as symbols of unbridled youth.</note>

And another, where these lines are found concerning a mare:

> Who, like a three-year-old mare in the wide fields,
> plays exultantly, and fears to be touched,
> having no part in marriage and still
> unripe for a headstrong husband.
<note>Horace, *Odes* 3.11.</note>

As for the fact that Anacreon called a woman by the name of a horse's foal, Nicander <note>A Greek poet and physician of the 2nd century BCE.</note> does the same, skillfully following the same metaphor when he deals with those things which are useful against poison: for he notes that the milk of a girl is beneficial. He used the term "milk of a foal" in the Anacreontic sense to mean "milk of a girl," in this verse:

> Pour the juice of a female foal and another for the brides.
<note>original Greek: "Θηλυτόρας πώλοιο..."</note>

Which is more correctly read in the manuscript codices as "for the bride" <note>original: "νύμφης" (nymphes) instead of "νύμφαις" (nymphais)</note>. Indeed, he advised that the milk of a female foal—and, if possible, of a virgin flourishing in the greenest flower of her age—should be dripped into balsam (to correct in passing the common editions, in which "blasphemy" <note>original: "βλασφημου" (blasphemou)</note> is written). Here the interpreters do not want it to be said of the milk of a horse's foal, but of a woman's milk, which they confirm by the authority of Erasistratus <note>A famous Greek anatomist and royal physician.</note>, who says in his book *On Deadly Poisons* that woman's milk is useful.

<summary>This page explores the classical metaphor of the "untamed foal" to represent youthful arrogance and feminine pride, citing poets like Anacreon and Horace. It also discusses a philological and medical point where "foal's milk" is used as a poetic metaphor for a young woman's milk in ancient remedies.</summary>

<keywords>Anacreon, Horace, Nicander, Plato, Erasistratus, horse symbolism, Greek lyric poetry, ancient medicine, philology, feminine pride</keywords>
