<meta>This page continues the discussion of moral symbolism in Book Four, specifically focusing on the vice of "Prostitutes' Insolence" or wantonness, following the previous analysis of animalistic human behaviors.</meta>

# Book Four. THE INSOLENCE OF PROSTITUTES.

<margin>A</margin> Indeed, the daughters of Diomedes (I mean that most insane King of Thrace) were most famous for the madness of their lust; they are compared by the Poets to horses who would devour those who approached them. From this, Greek authors record the origin of the proverb: *Diomedian necessity* <note>original: "Διομήδειά ἀνάγκη" — a Greek idiom referring to an unavoidable or forced obligation, here linked to the violent lust of Diomedes' daughters</note>. And so the commentator on Aristophanes' *Assemblywomen* interprets it: although Eustathius records that the father was accustomed to killing guests and setting them before his horses as fodder, following the fable which he found was created by the Poets with universal consent. However, so that it may be more certain to everyone that these things ought to be understood in the sense we have extracted from the most famous authors, you will find that the skilled use the term <term>hippobinon</term> <note>from the Greek "hippos" (horse) and "binein" (to copulate); used to describe a man of excessive or animalistic lust</note> to call a man who is intemperately devoted to lust and Venusian affairs: a name derived from the nature of the horse, as no one is ignorant of. Add to this that we use the term <term>hippopornon</term> <note>literally "horse-prostitute"; a term of extreme insult</note> as an extreme insult when we reproach someone for the highest wickedness and immoderate lewdness. In the Divine Scriptures also, it is said in this vein that certain men were made insane for women, because each one "neighed after his neighbor's wife" <note>A reference to Jeremiah 5:8</note>. That prophet gave the man a horse’s voice so that he might prove the horse-like petulance within him.

<margin>B</margin> For not every man is a "man," as Adamantius <note>A name often used for the Church Father Origen</note> would say, but he who is endowed with wickedness is a "man-horse": just as a man placed in honor, yet endowed with no wisdom, is a "man-beast": for he is held to be very much like the foolish beasts. And the "generation of vipers," of which the Lord spoke, can be called "man-vipers." Nor does Maximus Tyrius wish anything else to be understood hieroglyphically through the double form of the centaur than the bond of pleasure. For whenever it happens that the wild parts seize tyranny within us, and the heat of desire snatches away, oppresses, and dooms the nobility of the soul to its own service, the man is turned into a beast on account of his obedience to foulness. Indeed, quite ridiculously and wittily, Aristophanes says in *The Clouds* that Xenophantes—a man of rustic manners and madness—is represented under the appearance of a <term>Centaur</term> <note>A mythological creature, half-man and half-horse, often symbolizing the struggle between civilization and animal instinct</note>. And the very name of Chiron gave handle for a joke based on its meaning (because it also signifies "worse" <note>A pun on the Greek "cheiron," meaning "worse"</note>), when Diogenes beheld two Centaurs fighting each other in a very poorly painted picture: for he asked which of them was "Chiron," appearing to ask about the name, while in sense he meant which of the two was "worse."

<margin>C</margin> And since in the Divine Scriptures, those who are held by foul and unclean duties are beasts and cattle, prone and obedient to the belly, Hesychius of Jerusalem calls fornicators "stallion horses," a very appropriate hieroglyphic. Likewise in Psalm 31: "Do not become like the horse and the mule": which the interpreters explain thus: "Do not rage with the love of women, like unbridled horses." For it is well known that they repeatedly betray their intemperance toward pleasures by neighing, as those who are entirely ignorant of what is good, what is evil, what is honorable, and what is foul. But indeed, no one has expressed this prostitute-like petulance—indicated by so many through the hieroglyphic of the horse's image—better or more fully than Virgil, where in the *Georgics* he sings thus:

> *Truly, the madness of mares is notable above all others,*
> *And Venus herself gave them this mind, at the time when the four-horse team*
> *Of Glaucus of Potniae consumed his limbs with their jaws.*
> *Love leads them across Mount Gargarus and across the sounding*
> *Ascanius; they surmount mountains and swim across rivers,*
> *And immediately when the flame is applied to their greedy marrows,*
> *More so in Spring, because in Spring the heat returns to the bones, they all*
> *Stand on high cliffs with their faces turned toward the West Wind,*
> *And catch the light breezes, and often without any...*

<summary>This page explores the "hieroglyphic" or symbolic association between horses and human lust, drawing on Greek proverbs, biblical metaphors, and classical poetry to argue that uncontrolled desire reduces humans to beasts. It specifically uses the centaur and the "madness of mares" as emblems of sexual intemperance.</summary>
<keywords>lust, Diomedes, centaurs, horses, Virgil, Aristophanes, animal symbolism, moral philosophy, petulance, Venus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the horse (Equus) by Pierio Valeriano, specifically focusing on the biological and mythological properties of equine passion and the substance known as hippomanes.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano on the Horse.

> Pregnant by the wind—marvelous to tell—among the rocks
> and crags and deep valleys they flee; not toward your
> East, Eurus, nor toward the rising of the sun,
> but toward the North and the Northwest, or where the blackest South Wind
> is born and saddens the sky with rainy stars.
> Here at last the "horse-madness," which by its true name
> shepherds call *hippomanes*, distills as a sticky fluid from the groin;
> *hippomanes*, which wicked stepmothers have often gathered,
> mixing it with herbs and harmful incantations.
<note>original: Virgil, Georgics 3.267-283. Valeriano quotes this to establish the classical poetic view of equine frenzy.</note>

<margin>D</margin> But the fury of horses is no less intense in their jealousy, for they suffer so greatly from it that they punish their rivals most bitterly. They say it has been seen in Syria, where herds of wild mares commonly graze, that a leader goes before them; if any younger horse, driven by lust, should mount a mare to mate with her, the leader becomes so indignant that he pursues him without delay until he catches him and deprives him of his genitals by biting them off. In this genus of animals, however, the females are more restrained than the males. For Absyrtus <note>A Greek veterinary writer, often cited in the Hippiatrica.</note> says that once a mare knows she has conceived, she will no longer admit the male. <margin>E</margin> Nor should we pass over in this place what pertains to the power of *hippomanes*, the immoderate lack of restraint in horses, and their sudden arousal to passion—that poison which, as I was saying, wicked stepmothers often gathered. It is named for the madness into which it drives horses, and is of such strength and efficacy that when a certain Phormis of Maenalus dedicated two horses at Olympia—crafted by Dionysius of Argos—the horses were so attracted to mate with them that they could only be driven away with whips. This was because, as the people of Elis believed, that fluid had been hidden inside one of them, or as others say, mixed into the molten metal. Pausanias records this history.

However, the opinion of the ancients regarding the fluid *hippomanes* varies. For Theocritus, following Hesiod, seems to have thought it was a plant, saying it was a "drug":

> *Hippomanes* is a plant among the Arcadians, and because of it all
> the colts and swift mares go mad throughout the mountains.
<note>original: Theocritus, Idyll 2.48-49.</note>

And skilled and diligent men recognize it as such. Others stubbornly oppose Theocritus, asserting that *hippomanes* is not a plant, nor does any such thing grow in Arcadia. <margin>F</margin> They claim the one truest account is that handed down by Theophrastus: that it is a small piece of flesh, like a fig, clinging to the forehead of a newborn foal. Yet Crateuas <note>An ancient Greek pharmacologist and herbalist.</note> also recognizes a plant, teaching that it has fruit like a wild fig, but a dark leaf like a poppy; moreover, it is thorny, and when mixed into love potions, it has miraculous power. Hence, some interpret that "plant" (*phyton*) of Theocritus as a "growth" (*phyma*), that is, a tubercle born on the forehead of such a foal. For Archilochus also used *phyton* to mean *phyma*, and thus it could easily be understood as the piece of flesh. But indeed, what Theocritus says—that it is found among the Arcadians—must surely refer to a plant; for one cannot say that only foals born in Arcadia carry *hippomanes* on their foreheads, since that happens to almost all foals regardless of where they are born. Regarding plants, it is very true that some grow in one place and others elsewhere, or are more potent when coming from one location than another. But all this ambiguity arises from the equivocation of this term *hippomanes*, since there are three meanings for this word: one is that fluid which a mare, aroused by passion, is accustomed to emit from her nature; concerning which Maro <note>Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note> says:

-> A sticky fluid distills from the groin. <-

And such a great author asserts that in a serious matter, it is called by its true name, *hippomanes*. Tibullus had also said:

> And that which, when Venus breathes love into the untamed herds,
> the *hippomanes* drips from the groin of the eager mare.

<summary>This page explores the classical and natural history of 'hippomanes,' a substance associated with equine sexual frenzy and used in ancient love magic. Valeriano debates whether it is a vaginal discharge, a plant, or a growth on a foal's forehead, citing authorities like Virgil, Theocritus, and Pausanias.</summary>
<keywords>hippomanes, horse biology, equine jealousy, ancient veterinary medicine, love potions, Arcadia, Virgil, Theocritus, Pausanias, Absyrtus</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the substance known as "hippomanes" and transitions into the symbolic and ritual significance of the horse in Egyptian and Roman traditions.</meta>

# Book Four.

<margin>A</margin> It is clear from the description of these things that "hippomanes" <note>A term used in antiquity for various substances associated with horses, believed to have aphrodisiac or magical properties.</note> is here considered neither as a plant nor as a small piece of flesh. However, why anyone would deny that there is a plant in Arcadia <note>A mountainous region in Greece, often associated with mythology and pastoral poetry.</note> condemned for causing amorous madness, I do not see, since so many ancient authors acknowledge the matter itself. Finally, it is handed down by Theophrastus, Pliny, and others that a small piece of flesh, like a dried fig, is found attached to the forehead of a foal; unless the mother bites it off and devours it, she is alienated from the love and care of raising her offspring. Regarding this, the same Maro <note>The poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note> says:

> Search is also made for the love-charm torn from the forehead 
> of a newborn horse and snatched away from the mother,
> <note>original: "Quæritur & nascentis equi de fronte reuulsus / Et matri præreptus amor," from Virgil's Aeneid IV.</note>

I see no reason why anyone should oppose this. Nor should we listen to those who contend in the commentaries on Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist.</note> that "hippomanes" in Maro and Theocritus—and I will add Tibullus—signifies nothing other than a "great madness" (to use their words) and is always used as an adjective. We have already indicated that this word refers to three distinct things that can be seen and touched with the hands, and is a true substantive noun. Nevertheless, we do not deny that the term <gloss>horse</gloss> <note>original: "ἵππου" (hippou)</note> applied to many names signifies magnitude or loftiness, such as *hippolinon* <note>Horse-flax.</note>, *hippomarathrum* <note>Horse-fennel.</note>, and many others of this kind which it is superfluous to list. But these points are not so significant that they can deny that "hippomanes" is a real plant in Arcadia, whose form and peculiar power for amorous favors Crateuas described in Theocritus; nor do they take away the piece of flesh, which is manifest to all; nor do they remove the discharge, which is supported by the authority of Tibullus, Virgil, Probus, and finally Aristotle himself. For that poison, the Arcadian plant, and that fig-like tumor administered by sorcerers all agree in the same efficacy of madness. Perhaps we have pursued these matters more stubbornly than the nature of the undertaken work required; but it was necessary to respond to a most grave man, eminent in our age, who attempts to remove these three things from the nature of things, if we are to be allowed to defend the dignity of our Venetian, Roman, and Sicilian poets, each supreme in their own genre.

-> PROFANE <-

<margin>B</margin> Indeed, the Egyptian priests repudiated this animal—otherwise so noble, useful, and domestic—and decreed that through its hieroglyphic, the "profane" should be understood. I suspect the cause of this was the same that commanded our ancestors in Italy to abstain from it so religiously that it was not to be touched by the *Flamen* <note>A high-ranking priest in ancient Rome.</note> of the sacred rites, because it is reported that its gall is condemned among poisons. Others, interpreting the matter more simply, say it was done so that if they wandered too far afield, fraud might sometimes occur through negligence in the sacred rites. For that reason, leisure was also given to the families of our priests, so that they would not be distracted by profane occupations or cares, but would be occupied only in offering prayers to God and saying His praises seven times a day. Moreover, it has persisted until our times that it is considered contrary to custom and decorum for the highest prelates to be carried by horse through the city, especially on solemn days—except on one most festive day of all, when the new Supreme Pontiff <note>The Pope.</note> is led to the Lateran; for then it is the custom for all prelates to be carried on white horses.

-> AUTHORITY <-

<margin>C</margin> And since among the ancient Romans the Dictatorial dignity was exceedingly sacrosanct, by a certain ancient law it was forbidden for a Dictator to ride a horse. Plutarch, in the *Life of Fabius*, mentions various reasons: either because they judged that greater strength resided in the infantry forces, and therefore it was necessary for the General to remain in the phalanx—

<sig>f</sig>

<summary>The author defends the physical existence of "hippomanes" as a plant, a foal's birth-mark, and a biological discharge against those who view it only as a metaphor for madness. He then discusses the horse's symbolic status as "profane" in Egyptian and Roman religious traditions, noting specific priestly prohibitions and papal customs.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the horse as a symbol in the work of Pierius Valerianus, specifically focusing on its relationship to Roman authority and moral character.</meta>

# Pierius Valerianus: The Horse

<margin>D</margin>
...nor to leave their post: either because the authority of that leadership was so great and, in a way, tyrannical in the administration of all other affairs, they nevertheless wished for the Dictator <note>A temporary Roman magistrate with supreme authority</note> to appear inferior to the people in this one respect. However, for Fabio Maximus, the designated Dictator after the disaster suffered at Lake Trasimene, a horse was first permitted in such a great crisis of affairs, so that the magnitude and weight of his leadership might be shown, and he might be held in greater authority among all. For the horse is an altogether proud animal, and to ride is considered magnificent. Hence Aristippus, in the works of Horace, when he was rebuking the cheapness of Diogenes, says it is much more splendid "that a horse carries me, and a King feeds me." <note>original: "equus ut me portet, alat Rex"</note> Yet Diogenes, and many others, thought this less worthy of a philosopher. For this reason, when Plato had once mounted a horse, he immediately leaped down to the ground, saying he feared "lest he be seized by horse-stupor" <note>original: μη ἱπποτυφία ληφθῇ; a Greek term for equine pride or arrogance</note>—that is, lest he be contaminated by some sort of horse-like haughtiness.

->THE VIRTUES OF FABIUS THE DELAYER<-

<margin>E</margin>
**But** to return to our images: I saw a complex subject very skillfully carved on an Onyx gemstone. It has the head of a horse, a bearded human face on the breast, the feet of a crane, and the tail of a rooster; the letters are FAB. From this invention, one might easily guess those virtues for which Fabius the Delayer <note>Fabius Maximus "Cunctator," famous for his strategy of exhaustion against Hannibal</note> was distinguished. For we interpret leadership in war from the horse's head, wisdom from the bearded human breast, foresight and delay—and even observation—from the crane's feet, and victory from the tail of the young rooster. The reasons for all these have been explained each in their own Commentaries.

->DESPONDENCY OF MIND<-

<margin>F</margin>
**Nor** perhaps would that hieroglyphic be unwelcome by which despair, or the ill-omened beginning of a matter which someone has undertaken, is signified: where one would depict a horse stumbling or fallen on its forefeet, especially since the matter itself is confirmed both by a proverb and by the auguries of the ancients. For as pertains to the proverb which is told—"I laugh, O gelding," says Galba—Sisunnius Capito interprets it thus: those who fail in spirit at the start of some newly begun business. For when Sulpicius Galba was going out to his province and noticed his gelding had fallen at the very gate, he said, "I laugh, O gelding, that you have already slipped, when you have scarcely begun so long a journey." Of portents, indeed, it may suffice to mention one which is said to have happened to the Consul Flaminius, a fierce man, at Arretium. When he was taking it poorly that the Carthaginian <note>Hannibal</note> was now wandering through the middle of Italy, wasting everything with slaughter and fire with no one resisting, and was proceeding to attack the very walls of Rome, he ordered the standards to be pulled up more quickly. As soon as he leaped onto his horse, the horse suddenly collapsed and threw the fallen Consul over its head: surely a sign of an ill-omened undertaking, by which he himself fell shortly after with his army, overcome in the most foul slaughter at Lake Trasimene.

->DIFFICULTY<-

**Nor** will it be inappropriately recorded among pictures of this kind: a horse fallen into a ditch, or submerged in some pit, where it is held back most stubbornly by the tenacity of the mud and is unable to extricate itself, if we wish to express the difficulty or inconvenience of some business. Indeed, such is mostly the nature of geldings <note>original: "canteriorum"; pack-horses or work-horses</note> which we assign to carrying loads, that if they fall into some pit, they are entangled beyond measure both by the load with which they are burdened and by the filth of the livid marsh in which they lie, from which they can hardly be lifted. Hence that taunt in Livy cast by Jubellius Taurea the Campanian: "You are least of all a gelding in a ditch," when, exhausted by a long contest with the Roman Claudius Asellus, he was meditating withdrawal.

<summary>This page explores the horse as a symbol of both high political authority and personal pride, using examples from Roman history and Greek philosophy. It also interprets a specific "hieroglyphic" gemstone representing the virtues of Fabius Maximus and discusses the horse's fall as an omen of failure.</summary>
<keywords>Fabius Maximus Cunctator, symbolism of horses, Roman omens, Lake Trasimene, Plato, Diogenes, Aristippus, hieroglyphics, ancient gems, Galba, Flaminius</keywords>

# Book Four.

<margin>A</margin> Since the reason for this was not apparent, he turned to trickery and persuaded his enemy to dismount his horse and descend into a hollow path, where he would engage him on foot. When the Roman did this fearlessly and without any hesitation, Taurea, having tossed out a mocking jest, immediately spurred his horse and fled; it was as if he wanted to say that he did not wish to lower himself into a place from which climbing out would be most difficult. <note>This refers to a famous encounter between the Campanian Jubellius Taurea and the Roman Claudius Asellus during the Second Punic War.</note>

## CERES.

But so that we omit nothing that pertains to the image of the horse, so far as our diligence can achieve: we read that among the Phigalians <note>An ancient people of Arcadia, Greece.</note> there was an image of Ceres <note>The goddess of agriculture and grain.</note> consecrated in a cave, in the posture of one sitting on a rock, similar to a woman in other respects but with the head and mane of a horse, with images of dragons and many wild beasts attached, which played about her head; she wore a black robe reaching down to her ankles, and in one hand she held a dolphin, and in the other a dove. Indeed, although some say this was an image of Ceres, I would nevertheless believe it to be of Venus; unless we say that the fodder and abundance signified by Ceres are an incitement to allurements and petulance. For that the horse's head is not foreign to wantonness is manifest from those things we said a little <margin>B</margin> earlier. That the coils of dragons signify lover's knots and voluptuous entanglements is indicated in the commentary on the Serpent. That the dolphin is an image of love we have proved elsewhere, both from history and from so many signs of Venus to which that genus of fish is applied. Finally, that the dove refers to Venus was discussed in its own chapter. Yet one must acquiesce to Pausanias <note>A 2nd-century Greek traveler and geographer.</note>, who says that she is Ceres. Indeed, as we discussed regarding the Sow, it was declared in the time of Ptolemy Assertor <note>Likely referring to Ptolemy Philadelphus or a specific commentator on Egyptian rites.</note> that Ceres and Venus are the same. Furthermore, the appearance of this kind of picture can even be a hieroglyphic of the nature of things itself: for the horse's head, on account of the speed inherent in the animal, hints at the swiftest motions of the celestial orbs; the dove at the aerial tracts, as was said in its place; the dolphin at the sea, as is seen everywhere on coins; the wrappings of the snakes at the sinuous glidings of all things throughout the whole world, about which Socrates speaks in Plato, as we have testified in the chapter on the Serpent. The remaining wild beasts seem to show all the lives of living creatures. The rock represents the firm and stable seat of the earth, containing itself by its own solidity. Finally, the cave and the black robe represent those many things which, being less understood, lie hidden concerning the causes and origins of things, as in the images of Isis where the head and feet are made of black stone, about which we have spoken elsewhere.

## TO WHICH GODS HORSES ARE SACRED.

<margin>C</margin> That the horse was sacred to Mars <note>The god of war.</note> first of all is established by the fact that on the Ides of December <note>The 13th of the month.</note>, a horse that had been the victor on the right side was sacrificed to him. For we said in the chapter on the Bull that the right side is considered masculine; and we see masculine spirits attributed to Mars everywhere: concerning which more extensively in the interpretation of the male genitals, and also in the chapter on the Beetle. Some think the cause of this sacrifice was to conciliate Mars with a victim that was most acceptable to him. For Pausanias also says that Tyndareus, intending to avenge the wrongs of his abducted daughter <note>Helen of Troy.</note>, having called together the husband Menelaus and the other suitors to one place, first sacrificed a horse and then bound them by an oath to undertake the war against the Trojans. Nor are there those lacking who think that the sacrifice which we said was accustomed to be made on the Ides of December was actually done in the month of October, and for that reason the horse was likewise called the "October Horse"; and that there was no slight contention in Rome over its head between the residents of the Subura and those of the Sacred Way, so that the latter might fix it on the wall of the Regia, and the former at the Manilian Tower. However it may be, the horse, having its head wreathed with loaves of bread, was slaughtered on the October Ides for the sake of the crops in the Campus Martius.

<summary>This page explores the symbolic fusion of the horse with deities like Ceres and Venus, interpreting a horse-headed statue as a "hieroglyphic" of nature's forces, and describes the Roman ritual of the October Horse sacrificed to Mars.</summary>
<keywords>Ceres, Venus, Mars, Pausanias, October Horse, hieroglyphics, animal symbolism, Roman sacrifice, Phigalia, nature philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the horse as a symbol and sacrificial animal in antiquity, transitioning into a new section on the symbolism of the dog.</meta>

# Pierius Valerianus on the Horse.

<note>Pierius Valerianus was a 16th-century Italian humanist famous for his work on "Hieroglyphica," interpreting ancient symbols.</note>

<margin>D</margin> Indeed, they sacrificed a horse rather than an ox because the horse is an animal dedicated to war, appearing to look toward the destruction of crops rather than their growth. The ox, however, is born for the production and benefit of the harvest. Furthermore, some authors claim that horses were not usually slaughtered by the Romans to win favor with Mars <note>The Roman god of war</note>, but rather to exact punishment upon the creature through which it is known Troy was captured—the city from which the Romans claimed to be descended. But to me, this seems a very cold and thin slander against the Roman name: that a people distinguished by so many virtues would annually punish an innocent animal in such a way; and that those who were often accustomed to forgiving the gravest injuries from their enemies would cling so stubbornly to this one immortal hatred toward horses.

The Spartans, however, as Festus asserts, were accustomed to sacrificing a horse to the winds on Mount Taygetus, and to burn it there so that the ash might be scattered as widely as possible by the blowing of the winds. Furthermore, Pausanias records that a horse was usually slaughtered to the Sun on that mountain by the Spartans, following the Persian custom. Hence, Xenophon has Cyrus give a horse to be sacrificed to the Sun, as he was well aware that this was the Sun's victim among the Persians. The same author states that among the Sauromatae <note>An ancient nomadic people of the Eurasian steppe</note>, horses are raised for sacred rites and for food; hence the line from our writer of Epigrams:

<margin>E</margin>
> *The Sarmatian comes, fed by the horse he has drunk.*
> <note>original: "Uenit ab epoto Sarmata pastus equo." A reference to the nomadic practice of drinking horse blood or fermented milk.</note>

For the Romans call those "Sarmatians" whom the Greeks call "Sauromatae," as Pliny notes in his fourth book. According to Stephanus, they are different peoples, though both are Scythian races. Among the Salentini, a living horse dedicated to Jupiter was cast into the fire; just as the Rhodians annually threw four-horse chariots consecrated to the Sun into the sea, because it is commonly believed that he is carried around the world in such a vehicle. So too in Illyria, because the earth brought forth a horse at the strike of a Trident <note>A reference to the myth of Neptune/Poseidon</note>, they would cast four horses into the sea waves every ninth year. In Philostratus’s *Heroics*, you may read that a young horse of white color, still ignorant of the bit and rider, should be sacrificed to the Sun to achieve victory; this was done on the advice of Palamedes when the Greeks were once terrified because they had witnessed an eclipse of the Sun while besieging Troy. I have chosen to add these items like "trappings" <term>phaleras: ornamental metal discs for a horse's harness</term> to the horse; if anyone approves of these things provided at our expense, let him use them. But he who seeks other ornamentation should either seek it from others or provide it at his own cost.

<margin>F</margin>
->PIERIUS VALERIANUS TO FLORIUS<-
->MARESIUS OF BELLUNO, ON THOSE THINGS<-
->which are signified by the Dog in the sacred<-
->letters of the Egyptians.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'C' depicting a creature or bird amidst floral scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>

To whom indeed should I have given my "Dog"—raised in the sacred inner shrines of the Egyptians but brought here by my own diligence—as a gift, rather than to you, my most delightful Florius? For as often as I considered those things which those wisest of men taught were signified through the <term>hieroglyphics: sacred carvings or symbols</term> of the Dog—when they hinted at the interpreter of the divine mind, the priest, and the guardian gods by using that symbol; when they understood it as a sign of a grateful soul, an image of memory and friendship, a prophet, a philosopher, and even Mercury himself, the author of letters; as well as faith, obedience, and many other things through that same animal—so often did your image present itself to me. You are my pupil, whose zeal in the disciplines, most upright character, distinguished piety, and most free and benevolent spi—

<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "spirit" (animus), continuing on the next page with "my dog" (mi can-).</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the ritual sacrifice of horses across various ancient cultures (Roman, Spartan, Persian) before transitioning into a dedication to Florius Maresius regarding the symbolic meaning of the dog in Egyptian hieroglyphics.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of symbolic meanings attributed to animals, specifically focusing on the dog as a representation of the priesthood and prophecy.</meta>

# Book Five.

<margin>A</margin> ...I had perceived my own integrity and sincerity through the long experience of many years. But they thought I was insufficiently bathed in this joy of thought unless I also took care to commit the matter itself to writing, so that I might be drenched in a far more flowing pleasure by the act of recording it. I have therefore dedicated this selection from the flock of all living creatures to you—not because it was my intention to teach you what the dog signifies, but so that in the frequent writing and rereading, I might more often remember your goodness and enjoy this very delight for a longer time. It remains for you to contemplate my goodwill toward you through the dog's own most benevolent nature, just as I am accustomed to liken your commendable character to the noble qualities attributed to the Dog. Indeed, I believe and hope that just as the dog is found by its very nature to be most ready to contemplate the images of Heroes and gods, you too—as you already do with great diligence—will set the Dog before you as a certain model for living. But come now, let us explore the nature of the Dog from the commentary itself.

<margin>B</margin>
<margin>Professor of Sacred Letters.</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a dog sitting in profile, facing to the right. The dog appears to be a hunting breed with short hair and alert ears, contained within a simple rectangular frame.</image-desc>

First and foremost, they interpreted a professor of divine matters through the image of a dog, because he who intends to profess sacred letters <note>Theology or scripture</note> must meditate on this above all: that like a dog, he should bark constantly. He must never cease to attack the vices of men, maintain a fierce spirit, and seek the favor of no profane person—just as dogs do toward those whom they detect, by sight or scent, do not belong to the master’s household. Among the Hebrews, such men were Elijah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, Hosea, and many others. Among the Greeks, the foremost was Diogenes, whose very name was commonly derived from "dog" <note>original: "cane"; referring to the Greek word 'kyon' from which 'Cynic' is derived</note>, and from him the Cynic family became famous for its perpetual austerity of life. Not much different from this is another group <note>Likely referring to the Dominican Order, the "Dogs of the Lord" (Domini canes)</note> propagated through almost the entire world even to our day; although, being established among us with holier institutions and the profession of a truer discipline, they are more praiseworthy for their contempt of every profane thing, visible by their double-folded rags and perpetual bareness of feet.

<margin>C</margin> For in every age there have been those who spurned luxury and delights, and being content with little or the bare minimum, while professing a life free from vice themselves, they would prove and chastise all impure men with rebukes. And finally, the best priest ought to advance to this point: that while he lets no shameful man pass without a verbal lashing, he should not in the least fear the mark of "impudence" <note>The dog was often a symbol of shamelessness or impudence in the ancient world</note>.

## P R O P H E T

Truly, because of the freedom of rebuking, we have likened the Prophet to a dog, and specifically to a barking one. The reason for this meaning according to Horus <note>Horapollo, an author of a treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphs</note> is different: namely, because contrary to the habit of other animals, dogs are placed to guard the statues of the Gods, and by repeatedly gazing at them, they seem to admire them—just as a prophet devoted to meditation directs every effort of his thought toward God. Indeed, Livius Andronicus <note>original: "Liuius Adastro"; likely a corruption of Livius Andronicus</note> calls the overseers of temples and the interpreters of sacred rites and oracles "Prophets," of whom he says:
> When the Prophets command the heads to be veiled with green laurel,
> They who thus purely perform the holy rites.
It must be noted, however, that every use of the dog comes either from its barking or its teeth...

<sig>f iii</sig>
<vocab>dog, prophet, professor of sacred letters, Diogenes, Cynic, Elijah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, Hosea, Horus, Livius Andronicus, priest</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the symbolic connection between dogs and religious figures, arguing that the dog's barking represents the priest's duty to rebuke vice and its watchful nature represents the prophet's contemplation of the divine.</summary>
<keywords>symbolism, dogs, priesthood, prophecy, Cynicism, asceticism, biblical prophets, classical literature</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the dog as a symbol in Pierio Valeriano’s "Hieroglyphica," specifically transitioning from the dog's "biting" nature to its role as a "barking" prophet and guardian.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano’s Dog

...sought after. And concerning biting, enough has been said. <margin>D.</margin> But as for what pertains to barking, let us not be ignorant that the mouth is the interpreter of those things which the mind meditates upon in its inner chambers. Indeed, the Prophet is likewise a seer, and through the mouth, the mind itself proclaims its oracles from the deepest and most secret shrines. For the mouth is the brother of the mind, and the mind is the source of words; and thus, all things thought out flow through the mouth like streams from a fountain. But because it would be of little value to condemn, deter, and tear apart the vices of others unless all things were converted to a right way of living—directed, I say, toward the contemplation of divinity itself—the Egyptian priests noticed that the dog (as I was saying from the fragments of Horus <note>Referring to Horapollo, the supposed author of a treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphs</note>) looks toward the statues of the Gods, contrary to the habit of other animals, and gazes at them with a steady eye. Just as a Prophet is accustomed to do the same, being devoted solely to meditation from which the secret depths of God are reported, and from this, he hunts for knowledge of the future. They quite fittingly devised a <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>A sacred carving or symbol used by Egyptians to represent concepts</note> of this kind—those who, according to the testimony of Tully <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero</note>, are thought to have attained this art of divination through the vastness of time over almost innumerable ages. And I would believe it was received from them that a statue was placed in Olympia for a certain Thrasybulus among the Iamidae (so named from Iamus, whom Pindar mentions), a very famous man, over whose right <margin>E</margin> shoulder a weasel crept, while a dissected dog with its liver exposed lay nearby. I interpret this as a sign of prophecy, although some report that Thrasybulus devised prophecy from a dog. For the <term>Magi</term> <note>Ancient Persian priests or magicians</note> say that the heart of a weasel placed under a fasting tongue excites a prophetic mind. Pausanias, however, confesses it is unknown to him who first took prophecies from a dog, although he wrote that Thrasybulus alone used this kind of <term>haruspicy</term> <note>Divination by inspecting the entrails of sacrificed animals</note>, and the conjecture of that matter is taken from the statue which we mentioned was placed for him in Olympia. But to return to the Prophet: Pope Gregory, while saying that the teachers of the laws are called priests, says that the same are also sometimes accustomed to be called prophets, because they ought to bear such a spirit as we described above in the Dog. He laments in these words that this is by no means performed by most:

> For often improvident rulers, fearing to lose human favor, are afraid to speak right things freely, and according to the voice of truth, they serve by no means with the zeal of shepherds for the protection of the flock, but in the place of mercenaries; because when the wolf comes, they flee, while they hide themselves in silence.

Therefore, the Lord rebukes these through the Prophet Isaiah, calling them "dumb dogs" who are unable to bark. <margin>F</margin> Lest anyone take an occasion for slander against seers and priests from what Saint Paul says—*Beware of dogs, beware of evil workers*—Ambrose says that in that place, it must be understood as "sea-dogs" <note>original: "de marinis," likely referring to sharks or predatory sea creatures</note>, whose entire function is only to prey upon the rest of the animal kingdom. But how horrific are the things which Ezekiel added to the rebuke of Isaiah:

> Son of man, I have made you a watchman for the house of Israel; and you shall hear a word from my mouth, and you shall declare it to them from me. If, when I say to the wicked, "You shall surely die," you do not declare it to him, nor speak so that he may turn from his wicked way and live, he himself shall die in his wickedness, but I will require his blood from your hand.

This is indeed more dangerous, says Hesychius, because this laziness is harmful to many; for a negligent priest, either sinning himself or not punishing sinners, entices those who are unlearned to similar things. Therefore, the lawgiver in <margin>Leviticus 4</margin> Leviticus called the sin of the priest himself the "sin of the people."

---

## G U A R D I A N S H I P

This is also referred, according to the opinion of Ambrose, to the watches kept for the safety of masters. He says we ought to keep the word ready in the storehouse, lest like dumb dogs we [fail] the [charge] committed [to us]...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of symbolic animals, specifically focusing on the dog as a guardian and its association with both Christian saints and Roman household gods.</meta>

# Book Five.

<margin>D</margin>
<margin>A</margin>...lest we should seem to have abandoned the guardianship of the faith entrusted to us by a certain silence of prevarication <note>The author argues that staying silent in the face of heresy is a betrayal of duty, much like a watchdog that fails to bark.</note>. With this same meaning, theologians interpret that passage from Psalm 68: *The tongue of your dogs will be stained with the blood of your enemies* <note>original: "Lingua canum tuorum hostium intinguetur sanguine"</note>: where by "dogs," they interpret the overseers of Christ’s sacred buildings. For these men must be prepared against adversaries who plot ambushes against the flocks, so that the sheep may be kept safe from every injury of the enemy. Plato followed nearly this same sentiment in the second book of the *Republic*, where he describes the soldier as the guardian of the city: "The nature," he says, "of a noble youth who is educated for this kind of discipline, differs nothing from the disposition of a noble dog: both of them ought to be keen-scented, gentle toward their own, but fierce against enemies." For such is the nature of a noble dog, that it is most mild toward family and acquaintances, but most ferocious toward strangers and the unknown; as soon as it sees them, it shows its displeasure, even if it has suffered no evil from them. It considers it sufficient cause for enmity if a newcomer is not one of the household. Not without reason, therefore, those who write the life of St. Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a major Cistercian reformer.</note>—a man who deserved the best of Christian piety—say that his pregnant mother imagined in her dreams that she was carrying in her womb a white puppy, reddish on its back, and barking. From this, she received an answer from a wise man that she would be the mother of an excellent "puppy" who would undertake the guardianship of the divine house and exercise constant barking against the profane and the impious. Hence also a dog as a companion and traveler accompanies the angel, whom Raphael, in the prophetic book <note>The Book of Tobit in the Apocrypha.</note>, thought should be joined to himself and to Tobias's son, not without purpose.

<margin>B</margin>

<margin>E</margin>By the same reasoning, dogs were said among the heathens to be the presiding spirits over the *Lares* <note><term>Lares</term>: Ancient Roman guardian spirits of the household and crossroads.</note>, and for that reason the Romans clothed the gods themselves in dog skins; because just as dogs are mild and gentle toward those of the house, but fierce and formidable toward strangers, so also the Lares were held to be, to whom the care of the entire family was entrusted. Indeed, it is recorded in memory that there was a temple of Minerva of the Iliad in Daulia, in which dogs were nourished who would present themselves as mild, gentle, and playful only to arriving Greeks; but they would always rush upon barbarians <note>In this context, "barbarians" refers to non-Greeks.</note> as fierce, cruel, and indignant. Nor are there lacking—

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration titled "THE LARES GODS." It depicts two young male figures, the Lares, dressed in short tunics made of animal skins. They stand on a pedestal, holding up drinking horns and offering bowls. Two dogs stand at their feet, looking up at them as loyal companions.</image-desc>

<margin>F</margin>
<margin>C</margin>—at this day, some nations of barbarians who feed dogs on the corpses of enemies gathered by whatever means, and soon lead them into battles, so that when released against the enemy, they may sometimes enjoy the spectacle of a most foul slaughter. In the statue of Aesculapius <note>The Greek god of medicine and healing.</note> which was at Epidaurus, fashioned by Thrasymedes of Paros, son of Arignotus, a sculpted dog stood nearby, while the God, sitting on a throne, held a staff in his right hand and extended his left over the head of a serpent; Pausanias mentions this. He says the story behind that motif was that Aesculapius, exposed in the wilderness of Mount Tilphossium by his grandfather Phlegyas (whose daughter Apollo had secretly embraced), was nourished by a goat and guarded by a dog.

-> THE ARBITER OR GUARDIAN SPIRIT. <-

But Chrysippus is said to have brought forward another reason why the Lares were covered in dog skins: namely, because they represent the *Genii* <note><term>Genii</term>: Individual guardian spirits believed by Romans to accompany a person from birth to death.</note>, who are delegated as arbiters of our guardianship for this purpose: that they may exact punishment from us for wickedly done deeds and words, and avenge all crimes and impiety whenever, having set aside humanity, we decline into the life of brutes—which the Genii themselves, no differently than the most keen-scented dogs, pursue and drag toward merited punishments. Censorinus, after bringing forward many opinions of others, finally affirms this very thing: that the Genii are so constant...

It emphasizes the dog's dual nature: gentle to the faithful/household and fierce toward enemies or the impious.</summary>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the dog (Canis) as a symbolic figure in the "Hieroglyphica" of Pierius Valerianus. The previous section likely introduced the dog's general characteristics; here, the author transitions into its role as a guardian and its connection to divine and funerary rites.</meta>

Pierius Valerianus, On the Dog.

...observers assigned to us, so that they do not depart even for a moment. And these are the ones who sometimes urge us forward and often call us back, depending on whether we seek things that are healthy or destructive. But the author of our piety <note>A reference to Christ or the Gospel writers</note>, so that we would not seek these things from others, both affirmed that Guardian Angels are assigned to our protection and reminded us that they daily behold the face of God the Father. <margin>Matthew 18</margin> <note>original: "Matth. 18." Referring to Matthew 18:10: "For I tell you that their angels in heaven always see the face of my Father."</note> It was with good reason, therefore, that the dog, as a protective animal, was hieroglyphically dedicated to the Lares <note>Roman household gods who protected the family and home</note> (since they are the same as the Genii, though we have preferred to call them Angels). But I feel I cannot, without some offense to piety, pass over what I have read in ancient writers: that there was a temple of Vulcan at Mount Etna in Sicily, whose grove was inhabited by dogs who would fawn upon those approaching with purity and piety, but would miserably tear apart the impure and polluted, as if foreseeing the matter by divine power.

### THE UNDERTAKER.

From these things, therefore, it happened that the Egyptians indicated the undertaker <term>Vespillo: a person who carries out the dead at night; a low-ranking funeral official</term>, the guardian of sacred rites, and the temple warden of the funeral goddess <note>Libitina was the Roman goddess of funerals and burials</note> through the image of a dog. This was done by observing that firm and unmoving gaze with which that animal contemplates the very statues of gods and men entrusted to its care. For since among the Egyptians corpses were preserved for a long time in glass <note>original: "vitro." Valerianus is likely referencing a misunderstood passage in Herodotus regarding "hyalos" (glass or translucent stone) used in burials</note>—not in swine fat, as some have been deceived in interpreting—and there were funeral officials whose care it was to guard them, I would believe that the dog on the tombstone of the Massaei family in Rome looks toward this. It lies on the ground on a monument of most beautiful workmanship, but with its head raised, it gazes at the inscription, which is as follows:

->TO GAIUS JULIUS URBANUS<-
->AND TO JULIA OPTATA,<-
->HIS COMPANION,<-
->AND TO GAIUS JULIUS<-
->URBANUS PECULIARIS,<-
->HIS SON.<-
->[THEY PLACED THIS] FOR THEIR OWN. <image-desc>Woodcut depicting a dog lying down with its head raised.</image-desc><-

### A GRATEFUL SOUL.

However, I am not unaware that there are some who contend that a hieroglyphic of this kind—where a dog gazes at either a statue or a preserved corpse—signifies a man mindful of a benefit received. Furthermore, the dog never consigns to oblivion those who have deserved well of it; we will subjoin some wonderful examples of this matter shortly hereafter.

### MEMORY.

Whence the dog is also an image of Memory, which is concerned with past events. In the "Triple-Head of Prudence" <note>A common Renaissance motif (the Serapis head) showing a lion, dog, and wolf</note>, the dog's head signifies this, while the Lion's head indicates intelligence and the consideration of present things, and the wolf's head indicates foresight and thus future things—which we have explained more diffusely elsewhere. Socrates also swears "by the dog" in the *Phaedrus* when he firmly believes he has committed that oration of Lysias to memory.

### FAITHFULNESS.

In the *Phaedo*, however, he swears "by the dog" when he thinks that faith and obedience must be shown to those who hold the reins of government in cities. <margin>The coin of C. Mamilius</margin> Indeed, there is a coin of Gaius Mamilius, who was for some time appointed by decree of the Senate to mark out boundaries, on which a dog seems to bark at him. By this significance, he who is put in charge of such a business is warned above all to maintain faithfulness <term>Fides: trust, reliability, or loyalty</term>.

### FRIENDSHIP.

By reason of that faithfulness, the dog is very often used as a hieroglyphic for friendship. In friendship, according to the opinion of Cicero and everyone else, faithfulness is sought first; then a spirit most ready to be of service, should the occasion arise. That dutiful fawning upon their masters is wonderfully observed in dogs. But here I must restrain myself—

<meta>This page continues the fifth book of the treatise, transitioning from general descriptions of animals to specific historical and mythological anecdotes regarding the loyalty of dogs and the allegorical meaning of Cerberus.</meta>

# Book Five.

<margin>A</margin> I cannot help but commemorate certain examples of love and loyalty which are frequently found to shine forth in dogs themselves. For I will not now conceal how the three daughters of Leuctra, having been defiled by certain Spartan youths, then killed and thrown into a well, were revealed to their father Scedasus <note>Scedasus was a Boeotian whose daughters' violation and murder led to the Spartan defeat at Leuctra.</note> upon his arrival from abroad by the barking and running to and fro of a dog at the well; instead, I shall recount other matters perhaps worthy of greater admiration. For it is said that when a Roman citizen was pierced through during the internal wars, no one was able to cut off his head until they saw the dog, who was guarding his master and fighting fiercely for him, slain and removed by those standing by. Ambrose <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397 AD), Bishop of Milan.</note> reports that when a soldier was killed at Antioch, a dog stood as a companion to the corpse; while it testified for a long time to its longing for its snatched master with mournful wailing, it drew a huge circle of men to the spectacle of its fidelity, until, having spotted the man who had committed the murder, it leaped upon him and held him gripped for so long that the matter was revealed and the striker was hauled away to punishment. But that which is handed down by Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus, a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric who wrote "On the Nature of Animals."</note> is truly worthy of special admiration: that a certain man of Colophon, together with a servant and a dog, had gone to a market in Ionia <margin>B</margin> at the city of Theon. When the servant, who was carrying the money, turned aside a little from the road to satisfy the needs of nature, he set his purse on the ground and departed, forgetting it. The dog remained to guard it until they returned, unsuccessful because the money was not present, and found the dog in the same place by the pouch; because it had persevered for so long without drink or food, it expired once the deposit was returned. But what of that which is told in Plutarch <note>Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46–120 AD).</note> concerning Pyrrhus? He once came upon a dog guarding the corpse of its master, where it had remained for three days without food. When Pyrrhus ordered the body to be buried, he commanded that the dog be carefully tended. It happened not much later that as Pyrrhus was reviewing his army, the dog sat by the king and remained silent and quiet, until meanwhile the killers of its master appeared to give their names. The dog immediately leaped up when it saw them present, and attacking the enemies with great barking, it looked back at Pyrrhus repeatedly, so that it aroused a huge suspicion of the deed not only in him, but in all who were present. They were therefore seized, and being convicted by certain very small indications, they confessed the crime and were thus ordered to pay the penalty. There was also another Pyrrhus, different from that king, who had a dog of such great fidelity that when he died and was placed on the funeral pyre, the dog likewise followed and threw itself into the pyre, most stubbornly wishing to be burned with him. There was also a dog belonging to Polo, the most famous actor among the Greeks, who, after his master was placed <margin>C</margin> on the pyre, threw itself into the same flames of its own accord. And another little dog wished to be enclosed in the same tomb where the forger Theodorus had been buried. And now even the dog of the comic poet Eupolis, named Augeas, is celebrated, who after his master died and was buried, himself wasted away from grief, finally extinguished by the longing for his snatched master.

->SHARED PHILOSOPHY<-

<margin>F</margin> The Hieroglyphic and Cerberus <note>The three-headed hound of Hades.</note> possess a symbolic meaning: for some of the Greeks, perhaps not incongruously, thought that the three-headed Cerberus, dragged out from the dark inner chambers of Erebus <note>A region of the underworld representing darkness.</note> by the labor of Hercules, indicated the uncovering of philosophy by the Hero, which previously lay hidden in secrets. Indeed, though contained by one neck, it is distinguished by three heads, one of which serves Reason, the second Nature, and the third Morals. Whence, besides so many monsters tamed, they say Hercules himself shot arrows at Juno, and even wounded Pluto with a three-pronged weapon, as Homer says. For a man who is a Philosopher, while he investigates every sublime thing with skillful tracking, having found something worthy of capture, uses his mind like a certain winged arrow with which he may attain whatever that thing might be. Nor indeed is there any place, nor any retreat so remote, so hidden, so inaccessible, where philosophy has not reached: for of the earth...

<summary>This page recounts several historical anecdotes illustrating the extreme loyalty of dogs to their masters, followed by an allegorical interpretation of Hercules capturing Cerberus as a metaphor for the revelation of philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>canine fidelity, Scedasus, Saint Ambrose, Aelian, Plutarch, King Pyrrhus, Eupolis, Cerberus, Hercules, allegory of philosophy, Reason, Nature, Morals</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the symbolic dog from the previous section, transitioning from the labors of Hercules to Egyptian and Greek deities associated with canine forms.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano, On the Dog

<note>original: "Pierii Val. Canis." - This is part of Valeriano's "Hieroglyphica," a famous 16th-century treatise on symbols.</note>

...he searches through these inner chambers, which is to strike at Pluto himself with a weapon, and even to wound him <margin>D</margin>. This, I say, is to master the very nature of things. He tames so many monsters in the world because he settles wild behaviors and subjects them to civil, honorable, and useful laws. Just as Horace <note>original: "Flaccus"</note> says of the Poet:

> He forms the heart with friendly precepts,
> The corrector of harshness, envy, and anger.

Finally, he ascends to heaven, and from there he hunts for whatever can be subject to reason; he surveys all things and examines whether everything is right according to the ruler <term>amussim: a mason's leveling tool used here as a metaphor for perfect precision</term>. Furthermore, Apuleius, in his *Florida*, interprets the club of Hercules—which that hero used in taming monsters—as philosophy, while the monsters signify the vices of the soul. And to return to Cerberus, there are those who take the matter simply: that he was depicted by the poets with dog heads because he is considered the guardian of the Underworld <note>original: "Tartareus"</note>. However, Hecataeus of Miletus relates a history about him, saying there was a serpent in Taenarum which they called a "dog" for the reason that its bite was extremely lethal, immediately killing those it struck; he claims this serpent was led by Hercules to Eurystheus. But <margin>E</margin> this has always been the license of writers: that each might fashion fables according to his own convenience. To me, however, the previous interpretation is more pleasing, as I see such distinguished men interpreting the matter so learnedly.

### A N V B I S

We shall also add the god Anubis, represented by the same Egyptians through the image of a dog. Certain ancient historians have recorded that he, along with Osiris, drove the giants out of Italy. The story that he had a dog's head comes from the fact that he guarded the bodies of Isis and Osiris—which, as said above, is a trait very peculiar to dogs. For that reason, it is the custom to engrave a dog upon helmets, which I have observed on quite a few ancient coins.

### T H E  H O R I Z O N

But indeed, Plutarch, having investigated the matter far more learnedly regarding Anubis, unlocks the more remote inner chambers of the disciplines. He relates that the "terminator" circle in the sphere <note>The horizon line that divides the visible from the invisible</note> is signified by Anubis in the fables, and by the dog in sacred and hieroglyphic writing. Indeed, he contends that among the Egyptians, Nephthys is the lower hemisphere <margin>D I A N A</margin> and Isis is the upper: the latter is bright and diurnal, the former dark and nocturnal. Anubis participates in both, as he enjoys sight equally by day and by night. For Anubis has the same power among the Egyptians as Hecate has among the Greeks, to whom they dedicated the things above and the things below. For when Hecate was depicted as triple-headed, one head was entirely canine, another equine, and the third was sculpted in human form—though they fashioned this with a bristly and rustic face. We have explained the reason for this hieroglyphic picture more extensively in the section on the Horse. <margin>F</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within a rectangular frame. It depicts a composite mythological figure with three distinct heads emerging from a single body or chest: a horse's head on the left, a dog's head in the center, and a human female head on the right. The human head wears a crescent moon crown, indicating the goddess Diana or Hecate.</image-desc>

### S A T V R N

There are those who interpret Saturn through Anubis, because he gives birth to all things from himself, and thus becomes a participant in both light and darkness: because matter is obscure, but form is conspicuous. Those who recount the fables of the things done at the beginning of the world by Oromazes <note>The Greek name for Ahura Mazda, the Zoroastrian creator deity</note> say that after the heavens and stars were fashioned, he was employed as a guardian, and from this the image of the dog was devised.

<summary>Valeriano explores the symbolic meanings of the dog in antiquity, linking it to Hercules's triumph over vice, the Egyptian god Anubis as a guardian and "horizon" between worlds, and the triple-headed Hecate.</summary>
<keywords>Hercules, Anubis, Hecate, Plutarch, Egyptian mythology, hieroglyphics, symbolism, horizon, Saturn, Oromazes</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of canine symbolism from Book Five, specifically focusing on the dog as a hieroglyphic for leadership and military service.</meta>

# Book Five. 42
-> THE PRINCE <-

<margin>A</margin> Beyond those things already established regarding the meanings of the dog, those same Egyptian priests, if they wished to signify a **prince** or a **lawgiver**, created a hieroglyphic of a dog wearing a crown, or a headband, or a small cloak, while the rest of the body remained bare. This was because—to joke along with Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus, a Roman poet and philosopher; the author refers to his description of "light-sleeping dogs with faithful hearts."</note>—the "faithful hearts" of dogs are kept in diligent watch over the statues of the Gods. In this same way, a prince who is about to give laws ought to contemplate the "naked" King <note>Likely referring to the unadorned truth or the essential nature of a predecessor.</note> who shone in times past, and examine what that headband signifies for himself. They say that Pericles of Athens was most accustomed to doing this above all others. For whenever he entered into any magistracy, he would address the very insignia of the honor bestowed upon him and every piece of his attire before the assembly; he would inquire what they meant for him and what kind of man they demanded, and he would soon exhort himself to bear the persona he was putting on with vigor and decency. <margin>B</margin> This caused such admiration in everyone that they claimed, not without merit, that he summoned both thunder and lightning, and they confessed that bees had gathered honey in his mouth when he was an infant <note>A classical trope signifying eloquence.</note>. Truly, that the headband is a royal insignia, we have shown in the commentary on *Other Attire*. Furthermore, the Ptoemphanes—a people from a part of Africa—did not worship an image of a dog for their prince, but chose a living dog itself as their king; they observed its movements and understood from it what was to be done, where to stand, and where to proceed, through a most religious form of divination.

### T H E  S O L D I E R

A dog without a headband, yet bound by a leash, is a fitting hieroglyphic for a **soldier** bound by a sacred oath. The leash, of course, represents the oath of obedience. The dog itself represents the duty of the soldier, which is primarily to be intent upon the command of the leader, whether a journey is to be undertaken, a battle entered, or when the signal for retreat is sounded to return to the standards—observances which we see followed by dogs every day. Furthermore, consider this: if dogs are quarreling among themselves and a beast they equally hate appears, they immediately make peace and turn with one mind against the wild animal. This is how soldiers ought to be; even if they sometimes disagree among themselves, they should fight unanimously against common enemies. <margin>C</margin> We have an example of this in the legions of Lucius Sylla; when they were raging in a destructive mutiny, the General ordered it to be announced that the enemy was present and a shout to be raised by those calling to arms. Thus the sedition was dispelled, as everyone turned their minds toward the enemy. And Scorilus (or as others say, Cocis, or others, Cotio), a Leader of the Dacians, when he understood that the Roman people were divided by civil wars, yet did not think he should tempt fortune for fear that an external war might reconcile the citizens, did the following: while his men were urging him and sharply demanding that the opportunity be seized, he set two dogs to fighting in the sight of his people. While they were fighting most bitterly, he suddenly released a wolf, which the dogs immediately attacked with a single mind, dropping their anger toward each other. By this example, the Leader himself restrained the barbarians from beginning a fight, lest a rash attack benefit the Romans. Moreover, that same nobility of spirit is required in a strenuous soldier, so that he does not—as Virgil says of Aeneas—"deign to strike down those with their backs turned in death," <note>original: "neque auersos dignet' sternere morte"</note> nor provoke weak warriors, nor the armed against the unarmed, nor a man against women or boys. This same greatness of soul is found in dogs, such as those which the King of Albania sent as a gift to Alexander of Macedon; for when he had given one of the two he possessed, Alexander, wishing to test its spirit...

<summary>This page explains the dog as a symbol for both the Prince (representing vigilance and the weight of office) and the Soldier (representing obedience and unity against a common enemy).</summary>
<keywords>hieroglyphics, Pericles, Lucretius, Ptoemphanes, military discipline, Lucius Sylla, Scorilus, Alexander the Great, symbolism of dogs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the symbolic and historical nature of dogs, specifically focusing on their strength in combat and their role as symbols of obedience in military and religious contexts.</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano on the Dog

<margin>D</margin>
...with youthful vigor, he ordered bears, then boars, and finally deer to be released; but the dog lay motionless in contempt. Indeed, Alexander, offended by the sluggishness of such a massive body, ordered it to be killed in the meantime. When this was reported to the King <note>The Indian King who gifted the dog to Alexander the Great</note>, he sent another, adding a warning that Alexander should not try to test the dog against small beasts. The Emperor therefore introduced a lion first, and soon after an elephant, both of which the dog immediately laid low. Pliny described this remarkable fight most accurately in the 40th chapter of his eighth book.

->THE MENAPII<-

It is therefore not at all surprising that in the Register of Provinces and Soldiers <note>original: "canone prouinciarum & militum," likely referring to the *Notitia Dignitatum*, a document of the late Roman Empire</note> who served under the Roman Emperor from the time of Constantine down to Theodosius, Honorius, and many other later rulers, a shield was featured. On this shield was a yellow dog on a white buckler, the center of which was occupied by a red small shield <note>term: pelta - a crescent-shaped or small shield</note>. This dog was positioned at the bottom, beneath the small shield itself, with its feet turned upward. This was the insignia of that rank called the **MENAPII**, who earned their pay serving under the Master of Soldiers throughout Thrace.

->THE THIRTEENTH-RANK SOLDIERS<-

<margin>E</margin>
I saw a dog in the same inverted gesture on another shield, but this one was blue in color. The buckler was likewise white, with a golden center surrounded by a red circle, which was itself circumscribed by another circle of a thicker, muddy blue. This was the insignia of the **TERTIODECIMANI** <note>The "Thirteenth-rank" legionaries</note>, who were an order of the same military service under the same Master of Soldiers.

There was also that hieroglyphic by which the Egyptian priests, if they wished to signify obedience and a readiness to return at the master's command or nod, would paint a dog with its head turned back toward its spine.

**OBEDIENCE**
<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration titled "OBSEQVIVM." within the frame. It depicts a dog in the foreground, seated and twisting its neck to look backward at a man standing behind it. The man is dressed in simple garments and appears to be observing or commanding the dog. The background shows a minimal landscape with sparse grass and hills.</image-desc>

<margin>F</margin>
Furthermore, no animal is more obedient to the word than the dog. You may recall him most easily by a shout when he is departing; even if he is just about to seize his prey, he will stop if rebuked. If ordered to pursue, he flies forward; finally, he turns here and there as often as you please. Our ancient theologians interpret that saying from Ecclesiastes, *A living dog is better than a dead lion*, in such a way that they seem to have taken this kind of obedience into account. For by the "dead lion," says Eucherius <note>St. Eucherius of Lyon, a 5th-century bishop</note>, they understand the evil demon, for whose salvation nothing further can be hoped, as he cannot return to repentance. By the "living dog," however, they accept the man who wanders and is sometimes stubborn; for although he may sometimes be a transgressor and stiff-necked, he can nevertheless be brought back to obedience and better things. 

And to return to the dog’s very compliant nature, I wish to recite from Plutarch a memorable example of obedience and easy teachability in such an animal, which was performed in the Theater of Marcellus with Vespasian as a spectator. A mime once introduced a dog trained in almost every kind of dance. When the dog later had to feign illness, the mime offered him bread; after taking it, he swallowed a "drug" <note>part of the act</note>. Soon he appeared to tremble as if in pain, to be distorted, and to suffer from a heaviness of the head. At last, he fell down as if dead and stretched himself out, allowing himself to be dragged in any direction and tossed here and there, as the plot of the play required. Then, at the opportune moment when the scene demanded it, he moved slightly as if roused from a deep sleep, finally leaped up, and gave signs of joy and cheerfulness, playing along with many other things to the great admiration of all. This is attested by that author <note>Plutarch</note>, who has never been found to undertake anything foolishly. But as far as obedience toward a master is concerned, the dog has this in common with pigs, about which...

It concludes with an anecdote from Plutarch about a performing dog's remarkable discipline during a play attended by Emperor Vespasian.</summary>

<keywords>Alexander the Great, Pliny the Elder, Menapii, Roman military insignia, Egyptian hieroglyphics, obedience, Ecclesiastes, St. Eucherius, Plutarch, Vespasian, performing animals</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of canine symbolism and lore from Book Five, focusing on behavioral traits and their hieroglyphic or biblical meanings.</meta>

# Book Five.

A. Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist</note> says it has been discovered that dogs stolen by thieves, upon recognizing their master's voice, have returned to their owner by sinking the boat through leaning heavily on one side.

## THE FLATTERER AND THE BUFFOON.

There are those who represent the flatterer, the smooth-talker, and the biting buffoon simultaneously through the hieroglyphic of a dog; one of these is prone to excessive obedience, while the other is cruel enough to invent any kind of insult against anyone. <margin>Diogenes's taunt against Alexander.</margin> This is the Diogenic interpretation, by his own testimony about himself. For when he was asked by Alexander for what deeds he had commonly been allotted the name "Dog," he replied: "Because I fawn upon those who give, I bark at those who do not give, and I bite the wicked." Indeed, even the interpreters of dreams—as I have read among the Hebrews—predict that he who imagines seeing a dog during his nightly rest will be subjected to detractors. When that same Alexander sought some sport for himself, he sent a basket full of bones to Diogenes as a gift. The Cynic <note>The term "Cynic" itself derives from the Greek word for dog-like</note> accepted it and added: "This is indeed food for a dog, but by no means a gift for a king."

## HUNGER.

<margin>B</margin> They record a certain "canine hunger," which the Greeks called <term>boulimia</term> <note>original: βουλιμίαν; an insatiable hunger</note>; we might call it "the ravenous hunger." David touched upon this most learnedly in Psalm 58, where he sings: "They return at evening, and shall suffer hunger like dogs, wandering here and there through the cities," as he was foretelling the future beggary of the Jews; for you will frequently find the very word for hunger used to mean beggary.

## THE DESPISED.

In the Sacred Scriptures, you will very often find the dog used to represent a humble, cast-off, and contemptible thing. Lest anyone think this manner of speaking belongs only to the Neapolitans, we read of Mephibosheth, the son of Jonathan, that when he had been treated with unexpected favors by David and immediately deemed worthy of the fellowship of the Royal table, he said, professing his humility: "Who am I, your servant, that you should look upon a dead dog, such as I am?" <margin>1 Kings, chapter 24. <note>Modern Bibles cite this as 2 Samuel 9:8</note></margin> And David himself, when he had his King, Saul—who was pursuing him with hostilty—alone in his hands, was content only to cut off the hem of the royal cloak; following him, he cried out: "Whom do you pursue, King of Israel? Whom do you pursue? You pursue a dead dog."

## FLIGHT.

<margin>C</margin> If we wish to denote flight, we shall likewise depict a dog, but with its tail tucked between its legs. None of those whom we follow in this argument has explained the reason for this hieroglyphic. I would believe the invention was undoubtedly taken from the thing itself, as it is a common proverb even in these times: when someone loses heart or fearfully desists from an undertaking due to cowardice, we say he "tucks his tail between his legs." For whenever dogs take flight, they tuck their tails between their legs out of fear. Since this has also been notably observed in the wolf, Maro <note>The poet Virgil</note> described the matter most elegantly, as mentioned in its proper place.

## OMEN OF DISASTER.

In the art of divination, it is recorded that this gesture of the tail tucked under the belly in sacrificial victims gave an omen of future disaster; however, if the tail were twisted, it portended the difficulty of wars, as can be read in Didymus.

## OMEN OF VICTORY.

On the other hand, when they are stirred to a run, whether chasing prey or playing recklessly, they usually have an upright or extended tail. Such an image is found on a coin of C. Posthumus, in which one can see a running dog with its tail just as we <margin>The coin of C. Posthumus.</margin> described.

<column-break/>
