--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of canine symbolism from the previous section, specifically focusing on the dog's tail as an omen and transitioning into its role as a symbol for lovers and medical conditions.</meta>

->Pierio Valeriano, On the Dog<-

...we [observed], curved upwards: which indeed, when observed in burnt sacrifices, was a portent of future victory according to the discipline of the soothsayers <note>original: "aruspicum"</note>. Furthermore, on that coin, we conjecture that a running dog signifies speed because of the javelin placed beneath it: from which it indicates a matter completed with haste, or a victory quickly won, if we follow the Soothsayers.

### THE IMPURE LOVER.

There are also those who represent quite impure lovers through the image of a dog, not only for the reason that *kyon* <note>Greek: κύων, dog</note>, as Eustathius interprets it, is derived from *par to kyo* <note>Greek: πὰρ' τὸ κύω, from 'to conceive' or 'to be pregnant'</note>, which is to love, but because it pursues hares with a certain greedy affection: a significance we have categorized among the more wicked vices. Indeed, Ovid could not have expressed the incomparable passion of Apollo more pointedly than by that most brilliant comparison he sets down, sung in these verses:

> As when a Gallic hound has seen a hare in an empty field,
> And this one seeks prey with its feet, that one safety:
> The one is like to one about to fasten on, and even now
> Hopes to hold it, and grazes its tracks with outstretched snout:
> The other is in doubt whether it be caught, and from the very
> Bites is snatched away, and leaves the mouth that touches it.
> So is the god and the maiden; he swift with hope, she with fear:
> Yet he who pursues, aided by the wings of Love,
> Is swifter, and denies her rest.
<note>A famous passage from Ovid's Metamorphoses, Book I, describing Apollo chasing Daphne.</note>

### VICES OF THE SPLEEN.

A man who is troubled by some defect of the spleen <note>original: "splenis"</note>, and things which generally pertain to the milt <note>original: "lienis"</note>, the Egyptian priests signified through the hieroglyphic of a dog. For the dog has a very slender and thin spleen: but if either death or madness <note>rabies</note> should invade it, it comes from the spleen. Moreover, those very people who wish to treat a dog seized by madness contract the disease, and those most affected by that splenic blight perish: and whoever dissects a dog suffering from that disease, being breathed upon by that exhalation, experiences the most effective force of contagion. The urine of a mad dog is also harmful however it is stepped upon, especially to those having some ulcer. Indeed, the washing of this, even of one not mad, has such a pernicious power that if anyone should collect it, he is said to become slower in generation due to a numbness of the loins. Furthermore, there is such a contagious poison in a mad dog that all things it bites go mad—except for man. For the plague that happens to him from such a contagion is mania, as the most learned men of this age dispute; for according to Festus, "rabies" is properly a canine disease. But as for what Aristotle writes, that dogs themselves perish from this disease, and those things bitten by *kynanche* <note>Greek: κυνάγχῃ, dog-strangle or sore throat/rabies</note>, some famous men read *pas d'* <note>Greek: πὰς δ', meaning 'almost all'</note>, so that they explain they perish more quickly than a man. But to set aside those things which Albertus and Avicenna recorded as happening to men bitten by a mad dog a long time afterward, I read in Plutarch that madness in man was not known or distinguished from other diseases in the time of Aristotle, nor was it known to anyone from then until Asclepiades. However, let those most famous men who have undertaken to explain the matter see to these things. I will add this regarding it: the bite of every single dog is so pernicious that they say he who has once been harmed by a dog spoils the sitting of hens, the offspring of cattle, and causes abortions by his intervention, and by his entrance the wounds of another are aggravated.

<keywords>dog symbolism, Ovid, Apollo and Daphne, spleen, rabies, medical contagion, Aristotle, Plutarch</keywords>

# BOOK FIVE.
<note>original: "Liber quintus"</note>
g ij <note>Printer's signature mark</note>

<margin>A</margin>
## 6. SMELL.
<note>original: "OLFACTVS"</note>

But to return to the meanings of the Dog: the Egyptian priests signified **smell** through this animal among others. Indeed, they understood through this hieroglyphic one who is able to detect, by his own tracking, that which had previously lain hidden. For it is well enough established that smell is the dog's primary sense; concerning its "sagacity" (for Cicero attributes this quality especially to dogs, asserting it is derived from *sagire*, which means to perceive keenly), many examples are seen everywhere. For they recognize a master who has been absent for a very long time by smell alone, and likewise a stranger if he should arrive suddenly. In the hunt, they scrutinize tracks and follow them, pulling the inquiring hunter along by the leash toward the lingering wild beast, even if it has leaped across water or hidden itself in the most secluded place. It is a daily occurrence, especially among French dogs, that if you first show one of them a coin, then order the dog to be led out of sight and hide that coin under some tapestry or rug, the dog, when brought back and ordered to seek the coin, relying on smell alone, will find the object for you as quickly as possible, signaling the hiding place where the coin was concealed with both barking and jumping for joy.

<margin>B</margin>
## LAUGHTER.
<note>original: "RISVS"</note>

Because the dog suffers those things we mentioned above regarding the spleen, and the whole life of the animal seems to depend on it, it happened that the Egyptian priests repeatedly explained their considerations regarding **laughter** through the dog. For physicians teach that laughter is very rare or difficult for those who suffer in the spleen. It is a prerogative of the dog that those are most sought after by hunters which display a more "melancholic" face, as it is commonly said; for they say this is a sign of goodness, and they proclaim those who are such to be swifter and more persevering in the chase. You may read in Avicenna <note>A famous Persian physician and philosopher (c. 980–1037)</note> that the spleen receives and attracts those things which render the blood kind, clear, abundant, and bright; authors believe this is the cause of laughter and cheerfulness, because from blood so affected, a multitude of spirits is excited, and the clarity gladdens the soul. But however this may be, interpreters of dreams also—as can be read in Artemidorus <note>A 2nd-century Greek professional diviner and author of the 'Oneirocritica'</note>—augur both laughter and pleasures from a spleen seen in dreams. Since the spleen in dogs is both thin and extremely subject to defect, it is likewise necessary that they be moved by this affection of cheerfulness either not at all or very little. The Egyptians devised their significations from those things which were either absent or present beyond measure in any animal. It can easily happen that the nature of a man is to be indicated by such memories, as in Marcus Crassus, who for this reason was called *Agelastos* <note>Greek: "The Joyless" or "One who never laughs"</note>; or as in Gaius Laelius and Socrates, who are recorded to have always maintained the same expression and the same brow.

<margin>C</margin>
## LEVITY IN CHARACTER.
<note>original: "LEVITAS IN MORIBVS"</note>

Truly, laughter itself has its own meaning. The interpreter of Apollonius says that laughter is a sign of **levity** <note>Frivolity or lack of seriousness</note>; hence Isocrates wisely warned that unseasonable laughter is by no means to be loved.

## WORSHIP OF THE DOG.
<note>original: "CVLTVS CANIS"</note>

Indeed, other nations have left it attested by many examples how much they valued this animal. They proclaim that Cyrus—that Cyrus, I say, greater than all praise—was nourished by a dog, according to the assertion of all his people, whether the story was studiously fabricated so that he might be held more venerable. Indeed, among the Romans they were also held in great honor, although since the betrayal of the Capitol <note>Refers to the legend where dogs failed to bark at invading Gauls, while geese saved the city</note> they began to hurry them to the cross. For in an annual punishment between the Temple of Youth and Summanus, they were fixed alive to forks made of elder wood, because it seemed that through their negligence that loss had occurred. Otherwise, the race...

<summary>This page discusses the dog as a symbol for the sense of smell and the absence of laughter, linking the latter to the dog's small spleen. It also mentions historical figures known for their lack of laughter and the Roman ritual punishment of dogs for failing to guard the Capitol.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the symbolic and ritual significance of the dog (Canis) from the previous section of Pierio Valeriano’s "Hieroglyphica."</meta>

# Pierio Valeriano: The Dog

<margin>D</margin>
Since it was customary for a divine rite to be performed with a puppy in place of sacrificial victims early in the morning, they considered them so pure for food, and so suitable for appeasing the deities, that it is well established by the testimony of our writers that in former times the most celebrated feasts featured dog meat; you may even find mention of this matter in Plautus’s *Saturio*. <note>original: "Saturione." A lost play by the Roman comedian Plautus.</note>

-> TO WHICH GODS DOGS ARE SACRED <-

It should not be a burden to say to which deities dogs were given. Indeed, the Romans sacrificed them to Genetia <note>A Roman goddess associated with childbirth</note>, and the Greeks to Proserpina. Commonly, however, they sacrificed a dog during the Lupercalia—which the Romans celebrated in veneration of Pan Lyceus—because by their very nature dogs are adversaries to wolves. Others hand down the tradition that this began to be practiced in favor of Romulus, as there is a persistent rumor that he was nourished by a she-wolf. Some assert, however, that these sacred rites were first instituted by Evander, relying especially on the verse of Maro <note>The poet Virgil</note>, in which:
> Evander points out the Lupercal under the cold rock,
> Named after the Parrhasian custom of Pan Lyceus.
Furthermore, among the Argives, dogs were sacred to the goddess Cyonia <note>Likely a reference to Eileithyia or a similar birth goddess</note> on account of the ease of childbirth. But it must be noted that in many places the name "Cyonia" has been corrupted by the negligence of writers, who made it "Lyonia," either because they thought the word was shaped from the ease of "loosening" <note>Latin: "soluendi"</note> the birth.

<margin>E</margin>
Among the Spartans, a dog was offered to Mars <note>The god of war</note> on account of its boldness and readiness to attack; for their youths <term>ephebi</term> <note>Young men in military training</note> who were about to compete among themselves, before entering the contest, thought they were offering a puppy to that God—as the strongest—as the most powerful victim among the tame animals. Of the other Greeks, as Pausanias says, none performed such rites except the Colophonians; for these sacrificed a black female puppy to Trivia <note>The goddess Hecate, associated with crossroads</note>. It was also a custom for the Carians to sacrifice a dog, from which comes the phrase "sacrifice of dogs" <note>original Greek: "κυνῶν θῦμα"</note>. There were also the "Canarian" auspices among the Romans, in which reddish dogs—not far, of course, from the color red—were sacrificed at the Catularian Gate, as Ateius Capito says, when they acted on behalf of the crops against the blights and the cruelty of the Dog-star <note>Sirius</note>. Regarding a sacrifice of this kind, Ovid says in the fourth book of the *Fasti*:
> *In place of the celestial Dog, this dog is placed upon the altars,*
> *And it has nothing but the name to explain why this is done.*
Most people say, however, that it was done so that the yellowing crops might be brought to maturity. For the Dog-star is exceedingly hostile to the crops.

<margin>F</margin>
We are not ignorant, moreover, that the Thracians, both elsewhere and especially in Zerinthus, a cave of Samothrace, were accustomed to sacrifice and feast upon almost nothing more willingly than a dog—notable for the death of Euripides, who, on account of a dog sacrificed by him (as some say), was intercepted by the dogs of Archelaus and most miserably torn to pieces. Although others say that by the work of the envious Arrideus the Macedonian and Crateras the Thessalian, because he held great favor with the King, those dogs were set upon him, after Lysimachus, their keeper, had been corrupted with money.

<insert>
<margin>IMPUDENCE</margin>
<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a cynocephalic figure: a standing nude male body with the head of a dog. The figure is shown from the waist up, with arms held slightly away from the torso.</image-desc>
</insert>

For that is also a meaning of the dog, and for that reason in Homer, Helen accuses herself of being a dog, because she was the abominable cause and author of so many evils, addressing Hector thus: "Brother-in-law of me, a dog, a contriver of evil, one that strikes cold horror." <note>original Greek: "δαὲρ ἐμεῖο κυνὸς κακομηχάνου ὀκρυοέσσης" from the Iliad.</note> In the same author, Agamemnon is also accused by Achilles of "having the eyes of a dog." <note>original Greek: "κυνὸς ὄμματ' ἔχων"</note> For as we read in Pollux, he is said to have the eye of a dog who is excessively impudent; if you add "importunity" to this, you would make him Homer's "dog-fly" <note>original Greek: "κυνομυῖαν"</note> for the most impudent person, the nature of the fly and the dog being joined together. The "dog-fly," Euthymius...
